*Neuroticism*********

**Neuroticism Domain Synthesis (N1–N6)**

**Orientation**

This synthesis covers the six NEO-PI-R Neuroticism facets:

  - N1 Anxiety
  - N2 Angry Hostility
  - N3 Depression
  - N4 Self-Consciousness
  - N5 Impulsiveness
  - N6 Vulnerability

Evidence comes from 26 studies spanning three streams: developmental trajectories, structural mapping, and behavioral/molecular genetics. Not all studies contribute equally to every facet. Where studies use non-NEO instruments, I note the mapping explicitly and weight accordingly.

Throughout, I use the published aspect assignments from DeYoung et al. (2007) only as data points, not as organizing scaffolding. When I reference "the {N2, N5} grouping" or "the {N1, N3, N4, N6} grouping," I am reporting what DeYoung (2007) proposed. My own clusterings in Step 2 are built independently of that assignment.

**A note on DeYoung (2007) factor loadings for N:** The inter-aspect correlation within Neuroticism was .64 — the highest of any domain. Several facets loaded substantially on both factors: N1 (.57/.78), N4 (.67/.66), N6 (.66/.72). Only N2 (.87/.43) and N5 (.59/.43) load cleanly on one side. This means published aspect assignments for N4 and N6 are soft — they sit near the boundary. This ambiguity will matter throughout.

Summary: N1 Anxiety does NOT decline with age. It either stays flat or increases, depending on sample and age range. This contrasts sharply with domain-level Neuroticism, which broadly declines. The Brandt (2023) and Ringwald (2024) findings of increase are from stronger designs (L quality). The Seifert (2023) item-level data (XL quality) show stress-handling content — loosely N1-adjacent — worsening in old age. **Confidence: Strong signal** that N1 does not participate in the normative N decline.

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loaded .57 on one factor and .78 on the other. Primary loading on the {N1, N3, N6} side. Cross-loads substantially.
  - Ludeke (2019): The {N1, N3, N6}-associated grouping maps to HEXACO Emotionality (r=.53), especially HEXACO Anxiety facet (r=.61 with the corresponding BFAS aspect).
  - Jang (2001): Moderate N factor loading; 5-HTTLPR associated (r=.074, p=.04).
  - Ashton & Lee (2016): HEXACO Anxiety facet (nearest N1 equivalent) declines ~0.5 SD from 20s to 70s — but this is HEXACO Emotionality Anxiety, not Big Five N1 directly. Mapping is approximate.

**Genetic correlations with other-domain facets:**

  - Røysamb (2018): rG with E4 Activity = −.44; rG with E6 Positive Emotions = −.40. Substantial negative genetic overlap with E facets.
  - Jang (2001): Moderate genetic correlations with A facets (specific values available for A1 Trust, ranging rG = −.27 to −.75 depending on sample; the largest are A1's, not necessarily N1-specific).
  - Nikolašević (2021): rG with intelligence = −.07 (ns). S quality — wide CIs.

**Cross-taxonomy mapping:**

  - HEXACO: Maps primarily to Emotionality domain (Anxiety facet).
  - BFI-2: The BFI-2 "Anxiety" facet is positioned as one of three N facets, roughly aligned with the {N1, N3, N6} grouping.

**Dissociation flags:**- Does NOT participate in normative N decline — dissociates from domain-level trajectory. **Strong signal.**
  - Genetically near-identical to N3 Depression (rG=.90, Røysamb 2018, M quality). This is a within-grouping *convergence* flag.
  - Declining rank-order stability in old age (Brandt 2023, L quality) — suggests the construct becomes less stable as a trait in later life.

Range: .19–.41 (additive) or up to .33 (nonadditive, Jang 1996 only). **Instability flag:** Jang (1996, M quality) found entirely nonadditive genetic architecture, but the larger Jang (1998, L quality) found standard additive effects. The nonadditive finding did not replicate. Pilia (XL) found a moderate nonadditive component (broad .304 vs. narrow .186) but nothing as extreme as Jang 1996's pure dominance model. Best estimate: moderate additive heritability (~.19–.41) with possible nonadditive contribution. The Jang 1996 pure-dominance finding was likely sample-specific.

Unique age pattern: Higher h² in older adults (Pilia 2006, XL). Most N facets show the opposite (higher h² in young). This means N2's genetic signal strengthens or environmental variance shrinks with age.

Summary: N2 robustly declines across adulthood. Convergence across Terracciano (L), Schwaba (M), Brandt (L), and Ringwald (L) is strong. The Soto & John (2012) null for CPI Irritability is from a weaker design (M quality, 125 longitudinal, women only, non-NEO instrument). **Confidence: Strong signal** that N2 declines, in contrast to N1's flat/increasing pattern.

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loaded .87 on one factor and .43 on the other. The cleanest single-factor loading in the N domain. Anchors the {N2, N5} grouping.
  - Ludeke (2019): The {N2, N5}-associated BFAS aspect maps primarily to reversed HEXACO Agreeableness (r=−.61, especially HEXACO Patience r=−.70). This is a completely different HEXACO location from the {N1, N3, N6}-associated aspect, which maps to HEXACO Emotionality.
  - Jang (2001): Phenotypic cross-loading on A factor at −.45 to −.53. Genetic correlations with A facets are large: rG with A1 Trust = −.75/−.47/−.51 across three samples; with A3 Altruism = −.42/−.43/−.53; with A4 Compliance = −.51/−.50/−.21. **N2 shares as much or more genetic architecture with Agreeableness facets as with its N domain-mates.** This is replicated across three independent samples (L quality for genetic correlations).

**Genetic correlations with other-domain facets:**

  - Jang (2001): Genetically entangled with A domain (see above). This is the strongest cross-domain genetic signal for any N facet. L quality.
  - Nikolašević (2021): rG with intelligence = −.22 (significant, survives FDR). S quality — but notable because N5 (its grouping-mate) goes in the opposite direction.
  - Kim (2017): Shares biological pathways (axon guidance, focal adhesion, ECM) with N3, N4, and N6 — NOT with N5. S quality.

**Cross-taxonomy mapping:**

  - HEXACO: N2's content (anger, irritability) lands in reversed HEXACO Agreeableness, not HEXACO Emotionality. The HEXACO model explicitly rotated this content out of its Emotionality factor.
  - BFI-2: The BFI-2 "Emotional Volatility" facet captures moodiness/instability rather than anger/irritability specifically. Soto & John (2017) note that anger content fell interstitially between N and A domains. BFI-2 Emotional Volatility still converges empirically with BFAS Volatility.

**Dissociation flags:**- Genetic architecture is uniquely entangled with A domain facets. **Strong signal** (Jang 2001, L quality; three independent samples).
  - Shares biological pathways with {N3, N4, N6} but NOT with N5 — its proposed grouping-mate. **Weak/conflicted signal** (Kim 2017, S quality; single study, underpowered GWAS).
  - In Jang (1996), showed entirely nonadditive genetic architecture (d²=.33), unlike all other N facets. This did NOT replicate in Jang (1998). **Weak signal** — treat the nonadditive finding as sample-dependent.

Summary: Mixed. Terracciano (L) and Schwaba (M) find decline; Brandt (L) and Ringwald (L) find no change. Soto & John (M) finds decline. The discrepancy between Terracciano and Brandt may reflect different age emphases, period vs. age effects, or manifest vs. latent modeling. **Confidence: Moderate signal** — N3 probably declines in adulthood, but the trajectory is less robust than N2's decline or N1's non-decline.

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loaded .59/.73. Primary loading on the {N1, N3, N6} side, but with substantial cross-loading.
  - Jang (2001): 5-HTTLPR association is the strongest of any N facet (r=.166, p<.001). Three-sample replication.

**Genetic correlations with other-domain facets:**

  - Røysamb (2018): rG with N1 = .90 (near-unity). rG with E4 = −.49; rG with E6 = −.53. M quality.
  - Jang (2001): Moderate genetic correlations with A facets (part of the broader N×A genetic overlap).
  - Nikolašević (2021): rG with intelligence = −.14 (ns). S quality.
  - Kim (2017): Shares focal adhesion/ECM pathways with N2. S quality.

**Dissociation flags:**- Genetically near-identical to N1 (rG=.90). **Moderate signal** (Røysamb 2018, single study, M quality, but the magnitude is striking and the estimate is precise enough to be meaningful).
  - Developmental trajectory is less clear than N1 (which consistently doesn't decline) or N2 (which consistently does decline). N3 sits in between.

Limited direct evidence. Two studies find decline. No contradicting evidence. **Confidence: Moderate signal** — probably declines, but data are thin.

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loaded .67/.66 — nearly equal on both factors. N4 is genuinely ambiguous in the structural data; it sits at the boundary.
  - Jang (2001): Only N facet NOT associated with 5-HTTLPR (r=.038, p=.290). All other N facets (including N1, N3, N6 in the same grouping) show 5-HTTLPR associations.

**Genetic correlations:**

  - Kim (2017): Shares axon guidance pathway with N2 and N6. S quality.
  - Nikolašević (2021): rG with intelligence = −.14 (significant uncorrected, not FDR). S quality.

**Dissociation flags:**- 5-HTTLPR isolation: N4 is the only N facet without serotonin transporter gene association (Jang 2001, L quality). Its structurally proposed grouping-mates (N1, N3, N6) all show 5-HTTLPR associations. This is a within-grouping dissociation at the molecular level. **Moderate signal** — single candidate gene, but the pattern is consistent across the other five facets.
  - Factor-analytic ambiguity: Nearly equal loadings on both factors in DeYoung (2007). This is structural data, not a dissociation per se, but it means N4's grouping assignment is weakly determined.

Range: .11–.43. The Pilia (2006, XL) estimate is notable: narrow h²=.105 but broad h²=.332. This is the largest nonadditive component in the N domain, suggesting configural gene effects (epistasis) are important for N5 specifically. The age pattern is also distinctive: N5's h² is higher in young, while N2 and N3 show higher h² in older adults. N5's genetic profile diverges from its {N2, N5} grouping-mate in both heritability level and age pattern.

        L

        Accelerated longitudinal

        20–96

        **MONOTONIC linear decline** — no late-life upturn. Qualitatively different trajectory from all other N facets.

        Largest cumulative age difference in the entire C domain (d₁₃=0.88). The Impulse Control facet conflates C6/N5 content, but the direction (increasing control) converges.

Summary: N5 declines — and critically, its trajectory is monotonic (Terracciano 2005, L quality). All other N facets show curvilinear patterns with late-life upturn. N5 never turns back up. This is a qualitatively different curve shape. The trajectory converges with C6 Deliberation, which increases monotonically. Terracciano (2005) explicitly notes this convergence. Brandt (2023) replicates the N5 decrease / C6 increase pattern. **Confidence: Strong signal** that N5 follows a different developmental path from all other N facets, and that this path converges with C-domain facets.

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loaded .59/.43. Primary loading on the {N2, N5} factor, but with substantial cross-loading. The loading gap (.59 vs .43) is modest.
  - Jang (2001): Weakest N factor loading of any N facet (.35–.48 across three samples). Authors explicitly suggest relocation to Extraversion. However, 5-HTTLPR data shows significant association (r=.117, p=.0011), contradicting the authors' own claim of non-association. L quality.
  - Ludeke (2019): The {N2, N5}-associated BFAS aspect maps to reversed HEXACO Agreeableness — but this is driven primarily by N2's anger content, not N5's impulsivity content. The aspect-level mapping doesn't tell us where N5 specifically would land.

**Genetic correlations with other-domain facets:**- Nikolašević (2021): rG with intelligence = +.09 (ns). **Opposite direction** from grouping-mate N2 (rG = −.22, significant). S quality.
  - Kim (2017): Shares NO biological pathways with any other N facet. N5 is completely isolated in the pathway analysis. S quality.
  - Cross-domain developmental convergence with C6 Deliberation is replicated across Terracciano (2005, L), Brandt (2023, L), and Jackson (2009, M).
  - Jang (1996) and the extraction notes suggest shared genetic variance with C facets, though this is inferred from convergent patterns rather than directly measured bivariate rG.
  - Røysamb (2018): Suppressor pattern with life satisfaction — after controlling other N facets, higher N5 predicts slightly higher LS (β=+.06). M quality.

**Cross-taxonomy mapping:**

  - HEXACO: N5's impulsivity content doesn't have a clean HEXACO home. It's not Emotionality (that's anxiety/fear) and it's not HEXACO Agreeableness (that's anger/patience). Low impulse control sits between C and E in HEXACO space.
  - BFI-2: The BFI-2 "Emotional Volatility" facet captures moodiness/instability, which is related but not identical to N5's impulse-control content.

**Dissociation flags:**

This is the most heavily flagged facet in the Neuroticism domain. Evidence accumulates across multiple streams:

  - **Monotonic developmental trajectory** — qualitatively different from all other N facets. Converges with C6 Deliberation. Strong signal (Terracciano 2005 L; Brandt 2023 L).
  - **Cross-domain developmental convergence with C facets.** N5 decreases as C6 increases — both moving toward reduced impulsivity. Replicated in three independent samples. Strong signal.
  - **Weakest N factor loading** in Jang (2001), across three samples. L quality.
  - **Biological pathway isolation** — shares no pathways with any other N facet. S quality (Kim 2017).
  - **Opposite-direction genetic correlation with intelligence** from grouping-mate N2. S quality (Nikolašević 2021).
  - **Suppressor effect on life satisfaction** — reverses to positive after controlling other N facets. M quality (Røysamb 2018).
  - **Heritability age pattern diverges** from N2: N5's h² is concentrated in young; N2's is concentrated in old. XL quality (Pilia 2006).
  - **Largest nonadditive genetic component in N domain.** XL quality (Pilia 2006).

No single finding is decisive alone. But the convergence across developmental (Tier 1), structural (Tier 2), and genetic (Tier 3) evidence is remarkable. **N5 Impulsiveness is the clearest candidate for structural misplacement in the Neuroticism domain.**

Range: .14–.44. Moderately stable. Residual h²=.29 (Jang 1998) indicates substantial facet-specific genetic variance. Higher h² in females and in older adults (Pilia, XL).

**Developmental trajectory:** **Study****** **Quality****** **Design****** **Age range****** **Finding******

        Terracciano (2005)

        L

        Accelerated longitudinal

        20–96

        Near-zero linear slope but **largest late-life increase** of any N facet. Authors link to brain aging and Alzheimer's.

Summary: N6 is low/stable through most of adulthood, then rises sharply in old age. The late-life increase is the most dramatic of any N facet (Terracciano 2005, L). Brandt (2023, L) confirms the increase in a midlife-to-old-age sample. Schwaba (2022) finds it already near healthy levels by 30, suggesting the action is at the extremes of age. The Alzheimer's/cognitive-decline link (Terracciano) suggests N6's late-life increase may share more variance with C-domain decline than with N-domain affect. **Confidence: Strong signal** for a distinctive late-life trajectory.

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loaded .66/.72 — nearly equal, with a slight lean toward the {N1, N3, N6} factor. Like N4, this is a genuinely ambiguous loading.
  - Brandt (2023): Consistently higher rank-order stability than the N domain across the age range.

**Genetic correlations:**

  - Jang (2001): Genetic correlation with A3 Altruism is large (rG = −.57 to −.65 across samples). L quality.
  - Kim (2017): Shares axon guidance pathway with N2 and N4. S quality.

**Dissociation flags:**- **Late-life increase** distinguishes N6 from other N facets. All N facets show some late-life upturn (except N5), but N6's is the most extreme. The association with cognitive decline suggests this is partly a C-domain-adjacent phenomenon. **Moderate signal.**
  - Higher rank-order stability than domain average (Brandt 2023, L). This is unusual — most facets track their domain's stability pattern.
  - Like N4, has nearly equal loadings on both structural factors. Grouping assignment is soft.

**Step 2: Three Independent Clusterings**

**Clustering A — Developmental Trajectories Only**

Using only trajectory shape, timing, and direction of change. Ignoring all heritability and structural data.

**The evidence distinguishes three developmental profiles:**

**Group D1: Does not decline, or increases.** N1 Anxiety stays flat or rises across the lifespan. N6 Vulnerability is flat through most of adulthood then rises sharply in old age. Both resist the normative N decline. They differ in timing (N1's increase is gradual and lifelong; N6's is concentrated in late life), but they share the key feature: they do NOT participate in the domain-level Neuroticism decline.

Evidence strength: N1's non-decline is supported by Brandt (2023, L), Ringwald (2024, L), and Schwaba (2022, M), converging across different age ranges. N6's late-life increase is supported by Terracciano (2005, L) and Brandt (2023, L). This clustering is well-supported.

**Group D2: Robustly declines.** N2 Angry Hostility shows consistent decline across adulthood, converging across four studies (Terracciano L, Schwaba M, Brandt L, Ringwald L). N5 Impulsiveness also declines, but with a critically different curve shape — monotonic (no late-life upturn), whereas N2's decline is curvilinear. N5's trajectory also converges with C6 Deliberation, pulling it toward the C domain developmentally.

Evidence strength: N2's decline is among the most replicated findings in the corpus. N5's monotonic decline is clean but comes primarily from Terracciano (L); Brandt (L) confirms the decline direction. The N2/N5 grouping holds on direction of change but fractures on curve shape and cross-domain affinity.

**Group D3: Ambiguous / mixed evidence.** N3 Depression and N4 Self-Consciousness both show decline in some studies and no change in others. N3: declines in Terracciano (L) and Schwaba (M), but no significant change in Brandt (L) or Ringwald (L). N4: declines in Terracciano (L) and Schwaba (M), but untestable in Brandt. The evidence is inconsistent enough that I cannot confidently assign them to either D1 or D2.

**Within-group tensions:**

  - D2 is weakly coherent: N2 and N5 share direction of decline but differ in curve shape (curvilinear vs. monotonic) and cross-domain affinity (N2→A domain; N5→C domain).
  - D1 is moderately coherent: N1 and N6 share resistance to decline but differ in timing of increase.
  - N3 probably belongs closer to D2 than D1 (the declining studies are from decent designs), but the signal is noisy enough that I flag it as ambiguous.

**Summary of Clustering A:** **Group****** **Facets****** **Trajectory****** **Internal coherence******

        D1: Non-declining

        N1, N6

        Flat or increasing

        Moderate — same direction, different timing

        D2: Declining

        N2, N5

        Both decline

        Weak — same direction, different curve shape and cross-domain pull

        D3: Ambiguous

        N3, N4

        Inconsistent across studies

        Low — insufficient evidence to place confidently

        .72

        Ambiguous

The .64 inter-factor correlation is the highest of any domain. This means the two-factor solution is less well-separated than in other domains.

Ludeke (2019, L quality for structural claims) provides cross-taxonomy validation: the two BFAS Neuroticism aspects map to completely different HEXACO domains. Factor I content (anger/irritability) maps to reversed HEXACO Agreeableness (r=−.61). Factor II content (anxiety/withdrawal) maps to HEXACO Emotionality (r=.53). This is strong convergent evidence that the two-factor solution captures real structural differences.

Jang (2001, L quality for structural claims) reports phenotypic factor loadings: N2 cross-loads on the A factor (−.45 to −.53); N5 has the weakest N factor loading of any N facet (.35–.48).

BFI-2 (Soto & John, 2017): Split N into three facets: Anxiety, Depression, Emotional Volatility. Both Anxiety and Depression aligned with Factor II content; Emotional Volatility aligned with Factor I. The BFI-2 did not include a separate N5-like construct because anger/irritability content fell interstitially between N and A.

**Clustering:**

**Group S1: {N2}** — anchors Factor I. Highest single-factor loading (.87). Cross-loads heavily on A domain. In HEXACO, maps to reversed Agreeableness. This facet is structurally distinctive within N; it bridges N and A.

**Group S2: {N1, N3}** — anchor Factor II. Primary loadings of .78 and .73 respectively. Map to HEXACO Emotionality. Clean structural coherence.

**Group S3: {N5}** — loads primarily on Factor I (.59) but with weaker differentiation (.59 vs .43). Has the weakest N factor loading overall (.35–.48 across Jang 2001 samples). Structurally marginal within N.

**Unplaceable: {N4, N6}** — both load nearly equally on both factors (N4: .67/.66; N6: .66/.72). Structural data alone cannot assign them to either group. They sit at the boundary.

**Internal note:** I notice that the {N2, N5} grouping from Factor I has weak internal structural coherence. N2 loads .87 on Factor I; N5 loads only .59. N2 cross-loads on A; N5 does not cross-load in the same way (its structural pull is toward C/E, not A). They share a factor not because they're structurally similar to each other, but because they're both *not* on the anxiety/withdrawal side. Factor I may be defined more by what it isn't than by what it is.

I flag this observation: the {N2, N5} factor may be less a coherent structural grouping and more a residual category.

**Clustering C — Genetic Evidence Only**

Using only heritability estimates, genetic correlations, GWAS/pathway results, and 5-HTTLPR data. Ignoring developmental and structural data.

**Available genetic evidence for within-N facet clustering:**- **Heritability architecture (Jang 1996, M; Jang 1998, L; Pilia 2006, XL):** All N facets except N2 (in the anomalous Jang 1996 finding) show additive genetic effects. When Jang 1998 superseded Jang 1996 with a larger sample, the additive/nonadditive distinction vanished. Pilia (2006) finds a nonadditive component for all N facets, but it's not concentrated on specific facets enough to form clusters. Heritability architecture does not produce a clear clustering.**- Residual heritability (Jang 1998, L):** After partialling domain variance, residual h² is reasonably homogeneous: N1 .25, N2 .21, N3 .29, N4 .26, N5 .27, N6 .29. Range is .21–.29. No strong clustering signal.**

  - Genetic correlation: N1 ↔ N3 = .90 (Røysamb 2018, M): This is the single strongest within-N genetic correlation available. It suggests N1 and N3 are genetically near-identical. However, only these two facets were modeled in Røysamb — we don't know the genetic correlations for the other 13 pairwise comparisons.

  - 5-HTTLPR pattern (Jang 2001, L):** Five N facets show significant 5-HTTLPR associations: N1, N2, N3, N5, N6. One does not: N4. This groups {N1, N2, N3, N5, N6} together and isolates N4. However, this is a single candidate gene. Its relevance to overall genetic architecture is limited.**- Pathway overlap (Kim 2017, S):** Axon guidance links {N2, N4, N6}. Focal adhesion/ECM links {N2, N3}. N5 shares no pathways with any N facet. N1 has partial overlap. This is S quality evidence from an underpowered GWAS in a single population.**- Age heterogeneity in h² (Pilia 2006, XL):** N5 shows higher h² in young; N2, N3, N4 show higher h² in old. This splits N5 from N2 (its Factor I grouping-mate) and aligns N2 with the Factor II facets on this specific dimension. However, h² age heterogeneity is a secondary signal.**- Genetic correlations with intelligence (Nikolašević 2021, S):** N2 rG = −.22 (significant); N5 rG = +.09 (ns, opposite direction). This is a within-{N2, N5} dissociation, but from an underpowered study.**- Cross-domain genetic entanglement (Jang 2001, L):** N2 is heavily genetically correlated with A facets. N5's cross-domain pull is toward C, but this is inferred from developmental convergence, not from direct rG estimates in the genetic data.**Assessment:** The genetic evidence is too thin and fragmented to support confident within-domain clustering. The strongest signal is the N1–N3 genetic near-identity (rG=.90), but this comes from a single study (M quality) that only modeled two N facets. The pathway data (S quality) suggest {N2, N4, N6} share some biology, which doesn't match any published grouping. The 5-HTTLPR data isolates N4. The age heterogeneity data separate N5 from N2.

**Clustering C: Genetic evidence is insufficient to support confident within-domain clustering.**

What the genetic data *can* say:

  - N1 and N3 are genetically very similar (one study, moderate quality).
  - N5 is genetically distinctive from N2 on multiple dimensions (age pattern, pathway isolation, intelligence correlation direction), despite being grouped with N2 structurally.
  - N4 is molecularly isolated from its grouping-mates on one marker (5-HTTLPR).
  - N2 is genetically entangled with A-domain facets more than with other N facets.

**Step 3: Convergence Assessment**

For each facet pair, I assess whether they cluster together in 0, 1, 2, or 3 evidence streams.

**Note on method:** Clustering A (developmental) produced three groups; Clustering B (structural) produced four groups; Clustering C (genetic) could not produce groups but offered pairwise signals. I assess convergence at the pair level.

**What the convergence assessment shows**

**The only pair with multi-stream convergence is N1–N3:** structural co-placement (S2 group, both anchoring the anxiety-withdrawal factor) and genetic near-identity (rG=.90). This is the strongest within-domain pairing in the Neuroticism domain. The developmental stream dissents: N1 doesn't decline while N3 probably does. This dissent matters (developmental evidence is Tier 1), but two streams of support with the dissenting stream showing mixed evidence for N3 still yields moderate-high confidence.

**N2–N5 have two-stream convergence with an important asterisk:** They share direction of decline (developmental) and Factor I placement (structural). But the genetic stream actively dissents — they diverge on pathway overlap, age heterogeneity, and intelligence correlations. Furthermore, within the structural stream, N5's Factor I loading (.59) is much weaker than N2's (.87), and within the developmental stream, their curve shapes differ qualitatively (monotonic vs. curvilinear). This is a grouping that survives two streams but is stressed internally.

**No other pair reaches two-stream convergence.** Most pairs converge in zero streams. This tells us that the Neuroticism domain has weak internal structure — most facets are not strongly connected to any specific partner.

**Proposed Within-Domain Groupings**

Based strictly on the convergence assessment:

**Grouping 1: {N1, N3}** — the anxiety-depression core.

  - Basis: Structural co-placement (S2) + genetic near-identity (rG=.90).
  - Developmental tension: N1 doesn't decline; N3 probably does. This means they share genetic and structural substance but respond to different environmental/developmental forces.
  - Confidence: Moderate-high for the structural/genetic grouping. The developmental divergence is real but can be explained by environmentally mediated differentiation of a shared genetic core (consistent with Røysamb's finding that rE between N1 and N3 is only .53, much lower than rG=.90).

**Grouping 2: {N2, N5}** — the declining/externalizing pair.

  - Basis: Shared decline direction + shared Factor I placement.
  - Genetic tension: Multiple signals show they are not biologically similar.
  - Confidence: Moderate. The grouping holds structurally and developmentally (direction only), but the genetic evidence actively undermines it. **This is the weakest proposed grouping.**

**Ungrouped: {N4, N6}**

  - N4 and N6 both sit at the structural boundary (near-equal factor loadings). They do not converge with each other developmentally, genetically, or structurally (beyond both being ambiguous). They are not a pair — they are two independent boundary facets.
  - N6 has developmental affinity with N1 (both don't decline / increase), giving it a D1 placement, but this is one-stream convergence only.
  - N4 has the weakest cross-stream connections of any N facet. It groups with nothing confidently.

**Step 4: Misbehaving Facets**

**N5 Impulsiveness — strongest misfit in the domain**

N5 is flagged across all three evidence streams:

  - **Developmental:** Monotonic trajectory unlike any other N facet; converges with C6 Deliberation. Strong signal.
  - **Structural:** Weakest N factor loading (.35–.48). Marginal within N space. Strong signal.
  - **Genetic:** Biologically isolated from grouping-mate N2 (no shared pathways, opposite intelligence correlation direction, different heritability age pattern). Moderate signal (individually weak studies, but convergent pattern).

**Cross-domain affinity:** N5 shows stronger developmental convergence with C-domain facets (especially C6) than with any N-domain facet. Multiple studies note this: Terracciano (2005, L), Brandt (2023, L), Jackson (2009, M). Jang (2001, L) authors explicitly suggest relocation to Extraversion (though their own molecular data partially contradicts this). The developmental and structural evidence points more toward C than E as the alternative home, but this is for Conversation 6 (cross-domain patterns).

**N2 Angry Hostility — bridges N and A**

N2 is not misplaced in the same way N5 is — it clearly loads on a Neuroticism factor. But it bridges domains:

  - Genetic correlations with A facets (especially A1 Trust) are large and replicated across three samples (Jang 2001, L).
  - HEXACO rotates this content entirely out of Emotionality and into Agreeableness.
  - Phenotypic cross-loading on A (−.45 to −.53) is substantial.

N2 is a legitimate N facet that also has deep roots in A space. Flagging for Conversation 6.

**N4 Self-Consciousness — the invisible facet**

N4 is not actively misbehaving — it's just underdetermined. Near-equal structural loadings, limited developmental data, 5-HTTLPR isolation from its grouping-mates. It has the least evidence-based connection to any specific grouping. This is a gap problem more than a misfit problem.

**N6 Vulnerability — late-life specialty**

N6's distinctive late-life trajectory and link to cognitive decline give it a different developmental flavor from other N facets. Its late-life increase may share more variance with C-domain decline (Alzheimer's/executive function) than with N-domain affect. Flagging for Conversation 6.

**Step 5: Overall Domain Coherence**

**Neuroticism is moderately incoherent as a domain.** It has the highest within-domain factor correlation (.64) of any Big Five domain, which sounds like good news for coherence — but this high correlation means the two-factor solution is poorly separated, and it masks substantial facet-level heterogeneity.

**What holds the domain together:**

  - Domain-level heritability is well-established (~.41–.50, twin studies; .08–.16 SNP h²). Briley & Tucker-Drob (2014, XL) shows domain-level genetic architecture is indistinguishable across Big Five traits.
  - Within-domain co-development is the highest of any domain (r_change = .33, Schwaba 2022, M).
  - The N1–N3 genetic near-identity (rG=.90) anchors at least part of the domain.

**What fractures it:**

  - N5 Impulsiveness behaves more like a C facet than an N facet developmentally, and is structurally marginal.
  - N2 Angry Hostility is genetically entangled with A as much as with N.
  - The two-factor structural solution has four of six facets loading ambiguously (N1 cross-loads; N4 and N6 are near-equal; N5 is weak). Only N2 and N3 have clean primary loadings.
  - Developmental trajectories split at least three ways (non-declining, declining, ambiguous), and this split does not cleanly align with either structural factor.

**Assessment:** Neuroticism is a real domain — facets share substantial genetic variance and moderate co-development. But its internal structure is closer to a loose confederation than a tight hierarchy. The two-factor solution captures real variance (the HEXACO mapping confirms the two factors land in different locations), but four of six facets don't sort cleanly, and the one facet that is most problematic (N5) has multi-stream evidence for misplacement.

The {N1, N3} core is the most coherent subunit. Everything else is noisy.

**Step 6: Evidence Gaps**

**What I don't have:**- **No genetic correlations among all six N facets.** Røysamb (2018) gives us N1↔N3 = .90, but that's it within N. We don't know rG for N1↔N2, N2↔N3, N2↔N5 (critically important), or any pair involving N4 or N6. Pilia (2006) computed a full genetic correlation matrix but reported it only as a clustering dendrogram, not as a full table. The supplementary tables (Table S2, S3) were not available in the extraction. **Retrieving Pilia's supplementary genetic correlation matrix would be transformative for this synthesis.

  - **No facet-level GWAS.** Schwaba (2025, XL) pooled up to 1.14M participants but analyzed only at the domain level. Many contributing cohorts collected facet-level data (NEO-PI-R, BFI-2). Facet-level GWAS from this consortium would directly test whether the proposed groupings share genetic architecture. This is the single biggest gap in the evidence base.**- N4 Self-Consciousness developmental data is thin.** Only two studies (Terracciano, Schwaba) provide NEO-PI-R N4 trajectories. Brandt (2023) could not estimate N4 due to negative latent variance. No BFI-2 or HEXACO facet maps cleanly to N4. This facet is empirically under-characterized.**- No multivariate genetic models with all six N facets.** Jang (1996, 1998) reported only univariate models. Jang (2001) modeled N and A jointly but did not report within-N genetic factor structure. Briley & Tucker-Drob (2012) found the common pathways model rejected for N, but used CPI facets, not NEO. We need a bivariate or multivariate twin analysis of the six NEO N facets to test whether genetic variance organizes into one, two, or more factors within N.**- No observer-report developmental data specifically for N facets.** Schwaba (2022) has NEO-FFI other-report but at domain level. Jackson (2009) has observer data but only for C. The self/observer discrepancy may matter for N as much as for C.**- No N5 Impulsiveness bivariate genetic correlation with C facets.** The cross-domain developmental convergence between N5 and C6 is strong, but we have no direct rG(N5, C6) estimate. This would be the cleanest test of whether N5 belongs in the N or C domain genetically.**- No direct test of whether a two-aspect model fits N better than a one-factor model genetically.** Jang (2002, referenced but not in the extraction corpus) reportedly found two genetic factors per domain, but I cannot verify the N-specific results.**- Childhood developmental data for N is sparse.** Brandes (2020) has ICID-S data (ages 9–13) but the instrument mapping to NEO N facets is approximate, and one key N facet (Negative Affect in girls) failed to converge.**What would change the picture:**

  - If Pilia's full genetic correlation matrix shows N1↔N3 rG >> N1↔N2 rG, that would strongly confirm the {N1, N3} core grouping and provide genetic evidence for the two-factor solution.
  - If facet-level GWAS shows N2 and N5 sharing more SNP-level genetic architecture with A and C facets respectively than with each other, that would confirm both cross-domain pulls and undermine the {N2, N5} grouping.
  - If N5↔C6 rG is high, the case for N5 misplacement becomes overwhelming.

**Appendix: Pilia et al. (2006) Supplementary Tables S2 and S3**

These tables were flagged in the Gaps section as potentially transformative. They were available in the project files. Their contents substantially affect several conclusions above, and I note the revisions below.

**Table S2: Genetic Correlation Clusters (rG > .50 cutoff)**

Pilia et al. clustered all 93 traits (30 NEO-PI-R facets + cardiovascular/hematological measures) by average pairwise genetic correlation, using rG > .50 as the inclusion threshold. The personality-relevant clusters are:

        **18****** **E1 Warmth, E3 Assertiveness, E6 Pos. Emotions, N1 Anxiety, N2 Angry Hostility, N3 Depression, N4 Self-Consciousness, N6 Vulnerability****** **.618****** **.233****** **.893******

**Critical observations for the N synthesis:**

**1. N facets do not form their own genetic cluster.** Instead, five of six N facets (N1, N2, N3, N4, N6) cluster with three E facets (E1, E3, E6) into a single large cross-domain cluster (Cluster 18). At the rG > .50 level, N facets are not genetically separable from these E facets. This is a domain-boundary finding: the N and E domains share substantial genetic architecture.

**2. N5 Impulsiveness is absent from every personality cluster.** N5 does not appear in Cluster 18 or in any other cluster. At the rG > .50 threshold, N5 has no genetic correlation above .50 with any other personality facet. It is genetically isolated from the entire NEO-PI-R. This is the strongest genetic evidence yet for N5's structural misplacement — it does not belong to N genetically, and it does not belong to any other domain either at this threshold.

**3. The five clustered N facets do not split into two sub-clusters.** N1, N2, N3, N4, and N6 all land in one cluster, together. There is no evidence here that {N2} separates from {N1, N3, N4, N6} at the genetic level. The min rG in Cluster 18 is .233 and the max is .893 — there is heterogeneity within the cluster, but the clustering algorithm did not find a subdivision. This is important: the structural two-factor solution (which separates anger/hostility content from anxiety/withdrawal content) may not be reflected in the genetic architecture.

**4. E4 Activity clusters with C4, not with other E facets.** rG = .775, a high genetic correlation. E4 is absent from Cluster 18 (the E/N cluster). This confirms the extraction notes' flagging of E4 as genetically misplaced in Extraversion — but this is cross-domain context, not N-specific.

**Table S3: Genetic Factor Structure (Procrustes-Rotated to Phenotypic Target)**

Pilia et al. extracted principal components from the genetic correlation matrix of all 30 facets and Procrustes-rotated them to the American normative five-factor structure. This tests whether the phenotypic Big Five structure is replicated at the genetic level. Factor congruence coefficients were high (all > .85), indicating overall replication.

**Critical observations:**

**1. N5 Impulsiveness has dramatically lower genetic N loading (.45) than all other N facets (.71–.87).** The gap between N5 and the next-lowest (N4 at .71) is .26 — larger than the entire range among the other five facets (.16). This is the genetic version of the structural finding from Jang (2001) that N5 has the weakest phenotypic N factor loading. The genetic data confirm it.

2. N5's largest cross-loading is on C (−.41). This is the strongest genetic cross-loading of any N facet onto any non-N factor. N5 shares genetic variance with Conscientiousness to a degree that rivals its N membership. The negative sign means the same genes that increase N5 Impulsiveness decrease Conscientiousness — consistent with N5 measuring (reversed) impulse control.

3. N5 also cross-loads on O (.36) and A (−.31). N5 is genetically diffuse — it loads meaningfully on four of five factors. No other N facet does this. It is the least genetically coherent N facet by a wide margin.

4. N2 Angry Hostility's genetic A cross-loading (−.35) confirms the Jang (2001) finding. The same genes that increase N2 decrease Agreeableness. This cross-loading is second only to N5's C cross-loading among N facets. It provides genetic-level evidence for N2 bridging the N–A boundary.

5. N6 Vulnerability has a large genetic C cross-loading (−.48). This is the largest cross-loading of any N facet other than N5. It means the same genes that increase Vulnerability also decrease Conscientiousness — consistent with N6's late-life trajectory paralleling C-domain decline (cognitive aging / executive function loss). This was flagged as a possibility in Step 4 but lacked direct genetic evidence. Table S3 provides it.

6. N3 Depression has a notable C cross-loading (−.32). Third-largest C cross-loading among N facets. N3 shares more genetic variance with C than N1 (.08) or N2 (−.01) do.

**7. The genetic N factor loadings do NOT split into two sub-groups.** If the structural two-factor solution (separating {N2, N5} from {N1, N3, N4, N6}) were reflected genetically, we would expect a pattern of differential cross-loadings distinguishing the two groups. Instead:

  - N2's signature cross-loading is on A (−.35).
  - N5's signature cross-loadings are on C (−.41), O (.36), and A (−.31).
  - N6's signature cross-loading is on C (−.48).
  - N3's is on C (−.32) and O (−.32).
  - N4's are on O (−.35) and C (−.34).
  - N1 has no substantial cross-loadings.

The cross-loading pattern does not partition into two groups. N2 and N5 do not share a distinctive genetic cross-loading profile. If anything, the facets that share the strongest C cross-loadings are {N5, N6, N4, N3} — which crosses the published aspect boundary.

**Revisions to the Synthesis**

These tables change several assessments:

**Clustering C (Genetic Evidence) — revised from "insufficient" to partially informative:**

The genetic clustering data (Table S2) show that {N1, N2, N3, N4, N6} form a single genetic cluster, with N5 isolated. This is a one-group-plus-isolate solution, not a two-group solution. The genetic factor structure (Table S3) confirms: the within-N genetic loadings range from .71 to .87 for five facets, with N5 at .45.

Revised Clustering C: **{N1, N2, N3, N4, N6} form one genetically coherent group. N5 is a genetic isolate.**

**Step 3 Convergence — N2–N5 pairing downgraded:**The convergence table previously rated N2–N5 at "2 of 3 streams agree, genetic dissents." With the Pilia genetic data, the genetic dissent is now much stronger: N2 loads .79 on the genetic N factor while N5 loads .45; N2 clusters with the other N facets while N5 does not cluster with anything; their cross-loading profiles diverge completely (N2→A; N5→C/O/A). The {N2, N5} grouping now has **one stream of support** (structural factor analysis only — and even there, N5's loading is the weakest) and **two streams actively dissenting** (developmental curve shape diverges; genetic architecture diverges). **Revised confidence: Weak.**

**Step 4 — N5 misplacement case strengthened to near-conclusive:**

N5 Impulsiveness is now flagged by:

  - Developmental evidence (monotonic trajectory, C6 convergence) — **Strong signal**
  - Structural evidence (weakest N factor loading phenotypically) — **Strong signal**
  - Genetic factor structure (weakest N loading at .45; largest C cross-loading at −.41) — **Strong signal** (Pilia 2006, XL quality)
  - Genetic clustering (isolated from all personality facets at rG > .50) — **Strong signal** (Pilia 2006, XL quality)
  - Pathway analysis (no shared pathways with other N facets) — Weak signal (Kim 2017, S quality)
  - Intelligence genetic correlation (opposite direction from N2) — Weak signal (Nikolašević 2021, S quality)

The convergence across all three tiers — Tier 1 developmental (strong), Tier 2 structural (strong), Tier 3 genetic (now strong with Pilia XL data) — makes N5's misplacement the most well-supported facet-level structural claim in the N domain.

**Step 4 — N6 Vulnerability cross-domain affinity with C now has genetic evidence:**

N6's genetic C cross-loading (−.48) is the largest of any N facet except N5. Combined with its late-life trajectory paralleling C decline, there is now two-stream evidence (developmental + genetic) that N6 shares variance with Conscientiousness. This was previously flagged speculatively; it now has direct genetic support from an XL-quality study.

**Step 5 — Domain coherence revised:**

The Pilia data present a paradox for domain coherence. On one hand, the genetic factor structure shows a clear N factor with five facets loading .71–.87 — the domain holds genetically. On the other hand, the genetic clustering lumps those five N facets with E facets into a single super-cluster, and N5 doesn't belong at all. The domain is genetically real but its boundaries are porous (especially toward E), and one member (N5) is genetically estranged.

**Step 6 — Gap partially filled:**

The biggest gap flagged in Step 6 was the absence of a genetic correlation matrix for N facets. Table S2 partially fills this: we know that N1–N4, N6 all have pairwise rG > .50 with at least some cluster members (the average is .618), but we still lack the individual pairwise values. The cluster membership tells us about the overall genetic neighborhood; it does not tell us whether, say, rG(N1, N3) >> rG(N1, N2) within the cluster. The Røysamb (2018) finding of rG(N1, N3) = .90 remains the only individual within-N pairwise estimate.

The full 30×30 genetic correlation matrix reportedly existed at a now-possibly-defunct URL (http://www.sph.umich.edu/csg/chen/public/sardinia). If accessible, it would resolve remaining within-cluster questions.

**Transparency Note**

I notice that my Groupings 1 and 2 ({N1, N3} and {N2, N5}) correspond to the two factors identified in DeYoung et al. (2007), minus the boundary facets N4 and N6 that loaded ambiguously. I want to flag that I may be pattern-matching to prior knowledge.

**Evidence that independently supports this grouping:**

  - N1–N3 genetic near-identity (rG=.90) — data-driven, not from DeYoung.
  - N1 and N3 both anchor the HEXACO Emotionality mapping (Ludeke 2019) — independent structural evidence.
  - N2 and N5 both decline developmentally while N1 does not — data-driven trajectory grouping.
  - N2 and N5 both load primarily on the same factor in DeYoung (2007) — but this IS the prior knowledge I'm trying to avoid importing.

**Evidence that cuts against this grouping:**

  - N2 and N5 are genetically dissimilar (pathway isolation, opposite intelligence correlations, different heritability age patterns).
  - The developmental grouping {N2, N5} is driven by direction of change only; curve shape and cross-domain affinity diverge.
  - N6 could plausibly group with N1 (developmental D1) rather than with the {N1, N3} pairing, which would create a different grouping.
  - A purely data-driven clustering might produce {N1, N3} + {N2 alone} + {N5 alone} + {N4, N6 unassigned} rather than the two-group solution.

The honest summary: the evidence independently supports the {N1, N3} core. The {N2, N5} grouping is more likely an artifact of structural factor analysis than a grouping supported by convergent evidence across streams. The genetic data actively undermine it. If I were building from the evidence alone, I would describe the internal structure of N as: one coherent core ({N1, N3}), one domain-bridging facet (N2→A), one domain-misfit facet (N5→C), and two underdetermined boundary facets (N4, N6).

**Post-appendix update:** The Pilia supplementary data (Table S2, Table S3) strengthen this conclusion. The genetic factor structure confirms N5's marginal N membership (.45 loading vs .71–.87 for the others), and the genetic clustering shows N5 doesn't belong to any personality cluster at all. The purely data-driven picture is now: {N1, N2, N3, N4, N6} are genetically coherent as a group (they cluster together, all load >.70 on the genetic N factor); the two-factor structural subdivision within this group is NOT supported at the genetic level; and N5 is a genetic orphan whose strongest cross-loadings point toward C.

**Neuroticism Domain Synthesis — v2**

**Orientation**

This synthesis covers the six NEO-PI-R Neuroticism facets (N1–N6) using evidence from nine studies provided in the v2 extraction files. No information from any prior synthesis is incorporated.

The evidence base here is heavily weighted toward **structural mapping** (Tier 2) and **genetic architecture** (Tier 3), with limited **developmental trajectory** evidence (Tier 1). Bleidorn et al. (2009) is the only study providing longitudinal developmental data with biometric decomposition. This asymmetry should be kept in mind: the developmental stream is underrepresented relative to the project instructions' evidence hierarchy.

Only one study in this corpus. All six N facets declined in Bleidorn (2009). N1's decline was part of a domain-wide pattern. No data on late-life trajectories (sample ends at ~59). **Confidence: Weak — single study, moderate quality, no replication.**

**Structural placement:**

  - Soto & John (2017, XL): BFI-2 Anxiety is the most factor-pure N facet — primary loading .89/.88 on N, no substantial cross-loadings. Correlates r=.70 with NEO Anxiety. Mapped to Withdrawal aspect.
  - Yamagata (2006, XL): Genetic factor loading on N: .86 (Canada), .79 (Germany), .70 (Japan). Clean primary loading across all three countries. No substantial genetic cross-loadings.
  - Jang (2002, L): Within-domain genetic factor analysis — Anxiety loads on BOTH genetic factors in Canada (.48 on Factor 1 "Hostility," .27 on Factor 2 "General distress"). In Germany, loads .34 on Factor 2 only. Does not anchor either within-domain genetic factor cleanly.

**Dissociation flags:**

  - Loads on both within-domain genetic factors in Canada (Jang 2002), which means it does not partition cleanly into either the "hostility" or "general distress" genetic subcomponent. This contrasts with the phenotypic/BFI-2 picture where Anxiety is the purest N facet.

Declined alongside all other N facets. No distinctive pattern within this single study. **Confidence: Weak — single study.**

**Structural placement:**

  - Soto & John (2017, XL): BFI-2 Emotional Volatility (which captures moodiness/instability, deliberately stripped of anger content) still correlates r=.54 with NEO Angry Hostility and r=.79 with BFAS Volatility. Mapped to Volatility aspect.
  - Yamagata (2006, XL): Genetic N loading: .79 (Canada), .76 (Germany), .56 (Japan). Consistent genetic cross-loading on low A: −.32 (Canada), −.41 (Germany), −.60 (Japan). In Japan, the genetic A cross-loading (−.60) approaches the genetic N loading (.56). This is the single most important N2 finding: genetically, N2 bridges N and low-A across three independent samples.
  - Jang (2002, L): **Anchors Genetic Factor 1** in both samples (loading .65 Canada, .66 Germany). In Germany, N2 is the ONLY facet loading on this factor — it IS the factor. In Canada, other facets also load on Factor 1, but N2 has the highest loading.

**Genetic correlations with other-domain facets:**

  - Yamagata (2006, XL): The genetic low-A cross-loading is replicated across three countries and strengthens from Canada to Japan. This is strong evidence that N2 genetically bridges the N–A boundary.

**Dissociation flags:**- **Genetic N–A bridging** across three samples (Yamagata 2006, XL). In the Japanese sample, N2 is almost as much an A facet as an N facet genetically. **Strong signal.**
  - **Anchors its own genetic factor** within N, separate from the "general distress" factor (Jang 2002, L). N2's genetic variance is partially independent from the other N facets.
  - Combined: N2 is genetically distinctive within N — it loads on a different genetic factor from N1/N3/N4/N6, and it has substantial genetic roots in the A domain.

Declined with all N facets. **Confidence: Weak — single study.**

**Structural placement:**

  - Soto & John (2017, XL): BFI-2 Depression has the weakest N primary loading (.73/.67) of any N facet AND the largest cross-loading of any BFI-2 facet: −.43/−.41 on Extraversion. Also has meaningful secondaries on C (−.21/−.28) and A (−.12/−.20). The authors describe it as capturing "the association of sadness with lack of energy and social confidence." Mapped to Withdrawal. Correlates r=.77 with BFAS Withdrawal, r=.71 with NEO Depression.
  - Yamagata (2006, XL): Genetic N loading: .80 (Canada), .82 (Germany), .82 (Japan). Clean primary loading across all three countries. No genetic cross-loadings ≥|.40|.
  - Jang (2002, L): Loads on Factor 2 "general distress" (Canada .43, Germany .45). Also loads on Factor 1 in Canada (.45) — a dual loading, similar to N1.

**Dissociation flags:**- **Massive phenotypic E cross-loading** (−.43, Soto & John 2017 XL). This is the largest cross-loading of any BFI-2 facet on any non-assigned domain. Depression is functionally a blended N–E construct at the phenotypic level. **Strong signal** for phenotypic dual nature.
  - However, the **genetic** picture is clean: N3 loads purely on the genetic N factor across all three countries (Yamagata 2006, XL). The E cross-loading appears to be phenotypic/environmental rather than genetic.
  - **Strongest cross-domain predictor** across all BFI-2 validation criteria (Zakrisson 2025, L) — predicts all 5 well-being dimensions and both attachment styles. This reflects its position as a hub connecting multiple domains.

**Tension between evidence streams:** Phenotypically, N3 is the most cross-domain N facet (E cross-loading). Genetically, it's among the most cleanly N-loading. This divergence suggests the N–E overlap in Depression is environmentally mediated, not genetically grounded.

  - Jang (2002, L): Loads on Factor 2 "general distress" (Canada .42, Germany .44). Does not load on Factor 1 "Hostility."

**Genetic specificity:**- Mitrović (2024, S): **N4 is the only N facet significantly associated with COMT V158M.** Met+ carriers (higher synaptic dopamine) score higher on N4. The other five N facets show no COMT association. This is a facet-specific molecular effect, not an aspect-level effect.

**Dissociation flags:**- **COMT isolation**: N4 shows a dopaminergic association that no other N facet shares (Mitrović 2024, S quality). This does not flag misassignment — N4 loads cleanly on genetic N — but it suggests N4 has unique molecular-genetic correlates beyond its domain/aspect membership. **Weak signal** (single candidate gene, small effects, S quality).
  - Within-domain genetic factor placement: N4 loads exclusively on the "general distress" factor (Jang 2002), grouping it with N1, N3, and N6 and separating it from N2.

**N5 Impulsiveness**

Its stability heritability is tied for lowest (a²=30 with N2, Bleidorn). Most strikingly, N5 is the **only N facet where developmental change is entirely non-shared environmental** — zero genetic influence on how it changes over time (Bleidorn 2009, S quality for biometric estimates but the pattern is distinctive).

**Developmental trajectory:** **Study****** **Quality****** **Age range****** **Finding******

        Bleidorn (2009)

        M

        18–59, 10 years

        Declined, but smallest magnitude among N facets (slope = −0.42). Change entirely environmental.

N5 declined with the domain, but weakly and via a different mechanism (purely environmental) than the other N facets (which all show genetic influence on change). **Confidence: Moderate signal** for etiological distinctiveness — the biometric estimate is from an underpowered study (S quality), but the pattern (N5 as etiological outlier) converges with stronger structural and genetic evidence.

**Structural placement:**

  - Soto & John (2017, XL): The BFI-2 deliberately excluded impulsivity content during development. There is no BFI-2 facet corresponding to N5 Impulsiveness. The authors note this content fell interstitially between domains.
  - Yamagata (2006, XL): Genetic N loading: **.45 (Canada), .37 (Germany), .51 (Japan)** — dramatically weaker than all other N facets (.70–.86). Genetic cross-loadings: C = −.53 (Canada), −.33 (Germany), −.52 (Japan). Also cross-loads on E in Germany (.46). N5's strongest genetic association in 2 of 3 samples is with low-C, not N.
  - Jang (2002, L): **66–85% of N5's genetic variance is facet-specific** — not shared with any other N facet through either common genetic factor. N5 does not load on Genetic Factor 1 ("Hostility") or Genetic Factor 2 ("General distress"). It is genetically independent of the rest of the N domain.

**Dissociation flags:**

This is the most heavily flagged facet. Evidence converges across all available streams:

  - **Weakest genetic N loading** across three independent countries (.45/.37/.51 vs. .70–.86 for other N facets). Yamagata 2006, XL quality. **Strong signal.**
  - **Strongest genetic C cross-loading** of any N facet (−.53/−.33/−.52). In Canada and Japan, N5 shares more genetic variance with C than it does uniquely with N. Yamagata 2006, XL. **Strong signal.**
  - **Genetically independent of other N facets within the domain.** 66–85% facet-specific genetic variance; no loading on either common genetic N factor. Jang 2002, L. **Strong signal.**
  - **Lowest implied heritability** in N (.24/.34) — consistent with being a poor member of its domain. Jang 2002, L. **Moderate signal.**
  - **Only N facet with zero genetic influence on developmental change** (a²=0; others a²=7–9). Bleidorn 2009, S for biometric. **Moderate signal** (underpowered study, but pattern is distinctive and converges).
  - **Excluded from BFI-2** as interstitial content that doesn't belong cleanly in N. Soto & John 2017, XL. **Moderate signal** (reflects expert structural judgment, not direct data).
  - **Impulse Control facet** (≈ reversed N5 + C6 content) shows the largest developmental age difference in the entire C domain (d₁₃ = 0.88, Jackson 2009, M). Indirect but consistent with N5 being functionally a C construct.

**Assessment:** N5 Impulsiveness is the clearest case for structural misplacement in the N domain. Every evidence stream available in this corpus — genetic factor loading, within-domain genetic factor structure, heritability level, biometric change decomposition, phenotypic structural judgment (BFI-2 exclusion), and indirect developmental evidence — points in the same direction: N5 is a marginal member of N whose genetic architecture is more aligned with C.

**Structural placement:**

  - Yamagata (2006, XL): Genetic N loading: .76 (Canada), .85 (Germany), .71 (Japan). Solid primary loading. Consistent genetic C cross-loading: −.37 (Canada), −.34 (Germany), −.57 (Japan). N6 genetically bridges N and low-C across all three countries.

**Step 2: Three Independent Clusterings**

**Clustering A — Developmental Trajectories Only**

**Available evidence is severely limited.** Only Bleidorn (2009, M quality for developmental claims) provides trajectory data. In that study, all six N facets showed significant linear decline over 10 years (ages 18–59). No facet showed a qualitatively different trajectory shape. The domain was described as declining "homogeneously."

The only developmental differentiation comes from the **biometric decomposition of change** (which I classify as genetic evidence, Tier 3, but which speaks to the mechanism of change):

  - {N1, N2, N3, N4, N6}: Change has both genetic (a²=7–9) and environmental components.
  - {N5}: Change is entirely environmental (a²=0, e²=14).

**Clustering A: Cannot be performed.** Developmental trajectory evidence in this corpus is insufficient to differentiate N facets. All six decline. The etiological mechanism of decline differs for N5, but trajectory shape and direction do not.

If forced to produce a grouping: {N1, N2, N3, N4, N6} vs. {N5}, but based on change mechanism, not trajectory.

**Clustering B — Structural Mapping Only**

Using BFI-2 factor loadings (Soto & John 2017, XL), BFAS aspect data (Haehner et al. 2025, L), and BFI-2 Swedish validation (Zakrisson 2025, L). Ignoring genetic and developmental data.

**BFI-2 convergence with BFAS aspects:**

  - Anxiety: r=.70 with NEO Anxiety → Withdrawal
  - Depression: r=.77 with BFAS Withdrawal, r=.71 with NEO Depression → Withdrawal
  - Emotional Volatility: r=.79 with BFAS Volatility, r=.54 with NEO Angry Hostility → Volatility

**BFAS aspect data (Haehner et al. 2025):**

  - Withdrawal–Volatility intercorrelation: r=.61 (highest within-domain aspect correlation in the Big Five)
  - External validity divergence: Withdrawal shows stronger LS (−.51 vs −.34) and SE (−.62 vs −.47) correlations than Volatility. Withdrawal associates with conservatism (.13); Volatility does not.

**Mapping BFI-2/BFAS back to NEO facets (using Soto & John 2017 Table 1 and convergence data):**

  - Withdrawal content: N1 Anxiety, N3 Depression, N4 Self-Consciousness, N6 Vulnerability
  - Volatility content: N2 Angry Hostility (though BFI-2 Emotional Volatility was deliberately stripped of anger — it captures moodiness/instability instead)
  - N5 Impulsiveness: **Excluded from BFI-2 entirely.** No structural home in the BFI-2's N space.

**Key structural observations:**

  - Depression has the weakest N loading and the largest cross-domain reach (E: −.43). It is structurally the most "blended" N facet.
  - The Withdrawal/Volatility structural split is supported by convergent/discriminant validity data (BFI-2 facets map cleanly to their respective BFAS aspects) and by differential external correlates (Haehner 2025).
  - N5 Impulsiveness is structurally orphaned — excluded by the BFI-2 developers as interstitial.

**Clustering from genetic evidence:**

**Group G1: Core N — {N1, N3, N4}**. These three facets load cleanly on the genetic N factor (.66–.86 across samples) with no substantial cross-loadings on any other genetic domain. They load on the "general distress" genetic factor within N (Jang 2002). They are genetically "pure Neuroticism."

**Group G2: N–A bridge — {N2}**. N2 loads on genetic N (.56–.79) but also cross-loads consistently on genetic low-A (−.32 to −.60 across three countries). Within the N domain, it anchors its own genetic factor separate from the general distress factor. N2 is genetically distinctive — part N, part (reversed) A.

**Group G3: N–C bridge — {N6}**. N6 loads solidly on genetic N (.71–.85) but has consistent genetic cross-loading on low-C (−.34 to −.57). It shares the "general distress" within-N genetic factor with N1/N3/N4 but has this additional C-domain genetic root.

**Group G4: Genetic isolate — {N5}**. N5 has weak genetic N loading (.37–.51), strong genetic C cross-loading (−.33 to −.53), and in one sample an E cross-loading (.46). Within the N domain, 66–85% of its genetic variance is facet-specific — it doesn't participate in either common genetic N factor. Its developmental change is entirely environmental. N5 is genetically marginal within N and genetically aligned with C.

**Assessment of genetic clustering confidence:**

  - G1 is well-supported: three facets, clean loadings, replicated across three countries (XL quality).
  - G2 and G3 are each defined by a distinctive cross-domain genetic pattern replicated across three countries (XL quality). These are not "groups" so much as individual facets with specific genetic bridge patterns.
  - G4 (N5 isolation) is the most robust finding: convergence across Jang 2002 (L), Yamagata 2006 (XL), and Bleidorn 2009 (S). Three independent studies, three different methods, same conclusion.

**Step 3: Convergence Assessment**

Clustering A could not be performed (insufficient developmental evidence). I therefore assess convergence between Clustering B (structural) and Clustering C (genetic) only, noting this is a two-stream comparison rather than the protocol's intended three.

        **Moderate-high.** Both streams group these together.

**What the convergence shows**

**{N1, N3, N4} form the best-supported core grouping.** Structural mapping (Withdrawal) and genetic architecture (core N, general distress factor) converge. N3's phenotypic E cross-loading (−.43) is a tension point, but this appears to be environmentally rather than genetically mediated (genetic N3 is clean). The grouping holds.

**N6 is part of this core but with a genetic caveat.** Structurally, N6 maps to Withdrawal. Genetically, it loads on the same within-domain factor as N1/N3/N4. But its consistent genetic C cross-loading (−.34 to −.57) gives it a dual nature that the other core members lack.

**N2 is structurally and genetically distinct from the core.** It maps to Volatility (structural) and anchors its own within-domain genetic factor while also bridging to A genetically.

**N5 is consistently rejected as a domain member.** Structural exclusion (BFI-2) + genetic isolation (facet-specific variance, weak N loading, C cross-loading) converge across two independent evidence streams and multiple studies.

**Proposed Within-Domain Groupings**

**Grouping 1: {N1, N3, N4, N6} — the distress/withdrawal core.**

  - Basis: Structural Withdrawal mapping (B) + Core genetic N / general distress factor (C).
  - Internal tension: N3 has phenotypic E overlap; N6 has genetic C overlap. N4 has unique COMT correlate. These are individual quirks, not clean sub-groupings.
  - Confidence: Moderate-high.

**Grouping 2: {N2} — the anger/antagonism facet.**

  - Basis: Anchors Volatility (structural) + anchors separate genetic factor within N + genetic A cross-loading.
  - N2 stands alone. It has no grouping partner in this corpus's evidence.
  - Confidence: High that N2 is distinct from Grouping 1.

**Not grouped: {N5} — the domain misfit.**

  - Does not belong in any N grouping. Genetic architecture is more C than N. Phenotypic structure (BFI-2 developers) agrees.

**Step 4: Misbehaving Facets**

**N5 Impulsiveness — strongest misfit**

Multi-stream evidence for misplacement:

  - Genetic factor loading .37–.51 on N vs. .70–.86 for other facets (Yamagata, XL)
  - Genetic C cross-loading −.33 to −.53 (Yamagata, XL)
  - 66–85% facet-specific genetic variance; no loading on either within-domain genetic factor (Jang 2002, L)
  - Only N facet with zero genetic influence on developmental change (Bleidorn 2009, S)
  - Excluded from BFI-2 as interstitial content (Soto & John 2017, XL)
  - Lowest implied heritability in N (Jang 2002, L)

Cross-domain affinity: Genetically, N5 points toward C. The Jackson (2009) Impulse Control facet (which conflates C6/N5 content) shows the largest developmental age differences in the C domain. Flag for Conversation 6.

**N2 Angry Hostility — domain-bridging**

Not misplaced in the same sense as N5 — N2 loads solidly on the genetic N factor (.56–.79). But it bridges domains:

  - Genetic A cross-loading replicated in three countries (−.32 to −.60, Yamagata XL)
  - Anchors its own within-N genetic factor, separate from the "general distress" factor (Jang 2002, L)
  - BFI-2 developers deliberately stripped anger content from Emotional Volatility during development (Soto & John 2017)

N2 is legitimately in N but has deep roots in A. Flag for Conversation 6.

**N6 Vulnerability — C-adjacent**

N6 loads well on genetic N (.71–.85) but has consistent genetic C cross-loading (−.34 to −.57). Within the N domain, it participates in the general distress factor. Its C overlap suggests Vulnerability's genetic basis partly captures difficulty with self-regulation under stress — a C-domain construct. Milder flag than N2 or N5; N6 is not misplaced, but its genetic profile is not purely N.

**N3 Depression — phenotypically blended**

N3's massive E cross-loading (−.43) makes it phenotypically the most multi-domain N facet. But genetically it is one of the purest N facets (.80–.82, no cross-loadings). The blending is environmental, not genetic. This is informative but does not flag structural misplacement.

**Step 5: Overall Domain Coherence**

**Neuroticism is a genetically coherent domain — with one exception.**

The genetic evidence paints a reasonably clear picture:

  - Five of six facets (N1–N4, N6) load .56–.86 on the genetic N factor across three countries (Yamagata, XL). The domain holds genetically.
  - Within the domain, two genetic factors emerge (Jang 2002): one driven by N2 (anger/hostility) and one carrying the remaining core facets (general distress). This within-domain structure is real but does NOT map cleanly onto the published Volatility/Withdrawal aspect structure. The "Hostility" factor is narrower than Volatility (N5 doesn't load on it); the "General distress" factor is broader than Withdrawal (in Canada, N1 loads on both factors).
  - N5 Impulsiveness is genetically marginal. It does not participate in either common genetic N factor and shares more genetic variance with C than with N.

**The Volatility/Withdrawal structural split is phenotypically supported but genetically complicated.** The BFI-2 and BFAS evidence clearly separate anxiety/depression/withdrawal content from anger/volatility content at the phenotypic level. But the genetic structure within N does not produce a clean two-factor solution matching this split:

  - The genetic split is "Hostility (N2)" vs. "everything else" — not "Volatility" vs. "Withdrawal."
  - N5 (the other proposed Volatility member) is genetically absent from the Hostility factor.
  - N1 loads on both genetic factors in Canada.

**What holds the domain together:**

  - Five facets load >.56 on the genetic N factor across three countries.
  - The within-domain aspect correlation is .61 (Haehner 2025, L) — the highest of any domain.
  - All six facets declined together over 10 years (Bleidorn 2009, M).

**What fractures it:**

  - N5 is genetically marginal and structurally orphaned.
  - N2 bridges N and A genetically.
  - N6 has a genetic C component.
  - The within-domain genetic factor structure does not match the published two-aspect structure.

**Assessment:** Neuroticism is a real domain with a genetically coherent core {N1, N3, N4, N6}, a distinctive anger component {N2} that bridges to A, and one member {N5} that probably doesn't belong. The published Volatility/Withdrawal aspect model captures a real phenotypic distinction but doesn't have clean genetic support — the genetic split is different (Hostility vs. general distress, not Volatility vs. Withdrawal).

**Step 6: Evidence Gaps**

**What I don't have in this corpus:**- **No robust developmental trajectory evidence.** Bleidorn (2009) is the only trajectory study, and all N facets declined uniformly. The developmental stream — rated Tier 1 in the project instructions — is essentially absent. I cannot construct Clustering A. Without developmental data showing divergent trajectories (e.g., N1 increasing while N2 decreases), I cannot assess whether the structural and genetic groupings hold up under the strongest evidence stream.**- No genetic correlations between specific N facet pairs.** Jang (2002) reports within-domain genetic factors, not pairwise rG values. Yamagata (2006) reports genetic factor loadings, not a correlation matrix. I know N5 is genetically isolated, but I don't know rG(N1, N3), rG(N2, N6), or any other specific pair.**- No direct test of whether a two-aspect model fits N better than a one-factor or three-factor model genetically.** Jang (2002) found two genetic factors, but these were extracted empirically, not compared against the published aspect assignments. The Volatility/Withdrawal model was not tested.**

  - No N1↔N3 genetic correlation. This would be the most informative single number for the synthesis — do the two structurally closest Withdrawal facets share near-identical genetic architecture?

  - No late-life developmental data.** Bleidorn covers 18–59. N6 Vulnerability is commonly reported to show distinctive late-life increases (linked to cognitive decline), but this corpus cannot speak to that.**- No cross-domain genetic correlations for N facets with specific E or C facets.** Yamagata (2006) gives genetic factor cross-loadings, but not rG between, say, N5 and C6 Deliberation specifically. These would test the cross-domain affinity claims.**- No observer-report or peer-report data.** All evidence is self-report. The environmental factor structure may be inflated by implicit personality theory bias (Yamagata explicitly flags this).**- N4 Self-Consciousness remains the thinnest-evidenced facet.** Its COMT association (Mitrović, S quality) is the only finding distinguishing it from the general distress core. More evidence is needed to determine whether N4 has genuine unique properties or is simply an unremarkable member of the {N1, N3, N4, N6} group.**Transparency Note**

I notice that my proposed groupings partially overlap with the published Volatility/Withdrawal aspect assignments from DeYoung (2007):

  - My Grouping 1 {N1, N3, N4, N6} matches the published Withdrawal aspect.
  - My Grouping 2 {N2} is one half of the published Volatility aspect (minus N5).
  - My rejection of N5 as a domain member is consistent with a well-known critique of its placement.

**Evidence that independently supports this pattern:**

  - The genetic factor structure (Jang 2002) independently extracted a "Hostility" factor (anchored by N2) and a "general distress" factor (carrying N1, N3, N4, N6) — without reference to the aspect model.
  - The genetic Big Five loadings (Yamagata 2006) independently show N5 as the weakest N member and N2 as the only N facet with consistent A cross-loading — without testing aspects.
  - The BFI-2 development (Soto & John 2017) independently mapped two of three N facets to Withdrawal and one to Volatility, while excluding N5 — without importing the BFAS structure wholesale.

**Evidence that cuts against or complicates this pattern:**

  - The within-domain genetic split is NOT Volatility vs. Withdrawal. It's "Hostility (N2 alone)" vs. "everything else minus N5." The published Volatility aspect includes N5, which genetically doesn't belong.
  - N1 Anxiety loads on BOTH genetic factors in Canada, which is inconsistent with a clean Withdrawal assignment.
  - N6 Vulnerability has a genetic C cross-loading that the other Withdrawal facets lack, suggesting the {N1, N3, N4, N6} grouping has internal heterogeneity at the genetic level.
  - The developmental evidence (the strongest stream per project instructions) cannot differentiate any groupings in this corpus — all facets declined together.

**The honest summary from this corpus alone:** The evidence supports (a) a genetically coherent core of five N facets, (b) a within-domain genetic split that partially but imperfectly maps to the Volatility/Withdrawal distinction, (c) N5 as a genetic non-member of the domain, and (d) N2 and N6 as facets with significant cross-domain genetic roots (A and C, respectively). The two-aspect model captures real variance but is an imperfect description of the genetic architecture.
