**Step 2: Three Independent Clusterings**

**Clustering A — Developmental Trajectories Only**

Using only trajectory shape, timing, life-event sensitivity, and rank-order stability patterns:

**Cluster A1: The Risers — {E3 Assertiveness}** E3 is the only E facet that consistently increases through midlife. Robust across Roberts 2006 (XL — Social Dominance >1 SD cumulative increase), Terracciano 2005 (L), Schwaba 2022 (M), Soto & John 2012 (M), Haehner 2025 (L). Higher rank-order stability than other E facets. This facet stands alone developmentally.

**Cluster A2: The Steep Decliners — {E4 Activity, E5 Excitement-Seeking}** Both decline substantially across the lifespan. E4 is the steepest decliner in the entire E domain (replicated 7+ times). E5 declines steeply but with different timing (concave — fast early, slowing) vs. E4 (convex — slow early, fast late). They share direction but not curvature.

*Subcaveat:* E4's decline is uniquely age-differential (steeper at older ages, Brandt 2023 L) and uniquely sensitive to retirement (Löckenhoff 2009 S). E5's decline is concentrated in youth. These may reflect different processes (physical vitality loss for E4; sensation-seeking decline for E5) despite the shared downward direction. Their pairing is moderate, not strong.

**Cluster A3: The Flat-to-Modestly-Declining — {E1 Warmth, E2 Gregariousness, E6 Positive Emotions}** E1: stable or slight increase through ~60, then declines. E2: flat or slight decline. E6: flat to slight decline. None shows the steep declines of Cluster A2 or the increase of E3. Within this cluster, E1 is the most stable (Brandt 2023: no change while E2 and E6 decreased), so the cluster has internal heterogeneity.

*Within-domain co-development:* r = .15 among E facets (Schwaba 2022, M) — tied with O for lowest of any domain. E facets develop largely independently.

**Developmental clustering verdict:**

  - E3 stands alone (rising)
  - E4 and E5 loosely cluster (falling, but different timing/curvature)
  - E1, E2, E6 loosely cluster (flat/modest decline, but E1 is more stable)
  - The published aspect boundary (Enthusiasm = E1, E2, E6 vs. Assertiveness = E3, E4, E5) is NOT recovered. The developmental data split E into {E3} vs. {E4, E5} vs. {E1, E2, E6}, which maps loosely onto the aspect split for the flat/declining group but badly fractures the Assertiveness group.

**Clustering B — Structural Mapping Only**

Using only factor-analytic loadings, cross-taxonomy placement, and network-based structural evidence:

**Cluster B1: Enthusiasm — {E1 Warmth, E2 Gregariousness, E6 Positive Emotions}** All three load on the Enthusiasm aspect in DeYoung 2007. E1 and E6 are the core markers; E2 Gregariousness is the prototypical sociability facet. Cross-taxonomy: map to HEXACO Sociability, Social Self-Esteem, Liveliness. Roberts' Social Vitality. BFI-2: Sociability + Energy Level (Soto & John 2017 mapped both to Enthusiasm).

Ludeke 2019 (L): Enthusiasm is a broad construct spread across multiple HEXACO X facets. β = .52 predicting HEXACO Extraversion.

**Cluster B2: Assertiveness Core — {E3 Assertiveness}** E3 is the clearest single-facet marker of the Assertiveness aspect. Maps cleanly to HEXACO Social Boldness, Roberts' Social Dominance, BFI-2 Assertiveness. Ludeke 2019: Assertiveness is a tighter, more unitary construct than Enthusiasm, captured primarily by HEXACO Social Boldness. β = .44 predicting HEXACO Extraversion.

**Structurally Homeless — {E5 Excitement-Seeking}** Peripheral in DeYoung 2007. Excluded from BFI-2 entirely (Soto & John 2017). No clean HEXACO equivalent. Phenotypically disconnected from other E facets (Røysamb 2018: r = .05 with life satisfaction, vs. .13–.30 for all other E facets). This facet has no secure structural home.

**Structurally Contested — {E4 Activity}** Factor analysis: loads on Assertiveness (DeYoung 2007). But Roberts 2006 (XL) classified it under Social Vitality, not Social Dominance. Soto & John 2017 placed BFI-2 Energy Level under Enthusiasm. The structural disagreement across taxonomies is itself the finding. E4's content ("bursting with energy," "fast pace of living") sits at the intersection of motivational energy (Assertiveness?) and vitality/enthusiasm (Enthusiasm?).

**Structural clustering verdict:**

  - {E1, E2, E6} = Enthusiasm (strong)
  - {E3} = Assertiveness core (strong)
  - E4 is contested between Enthusiasm and Assertiveness across taxonomies
  - E5 is structurally homeless
  - The published 3+3 aspect split (Enthusiasm = E1, E2, E6; Assertiveness = E3, E4, E5) is recovered for Enthusiasm but not cleanly for Assertiveness. The "Assertiveness aspect" is really just E3 plus two disputed facets.

**Clustering C — Genetic Evidence Only**

Using only heritability estimates, genetic correlations, genetic clustering, and GWAS results:

**The genetic evidence for within-E clustering is weak and inconsistent.** Key findings:

  - *Additive/nonadditive split crosscuts aspects:* E1, E3, E5 are nonadditive (Jang 1996, M); E2, E4, E6 are additive. This does NOT follow the aspect boundary. However, nonadditive effects failed to replicate in Jang 1998 (L, larger sample) — the split may be noise.**

  - *E4 Activity clusters genetically with C facets:* The strongest genetic signal in the E domain. Pilia 2006 (XL): E4's genetic correlations place it closer to C than to other E facets, confirmed by genetic factor analysis. E4's age-heterogeneity pattern (h² concentrated in young) matches C facets exactly.

  - *E6 Positive Emotions is genetically domain-general:* rG = .57 with Sociability (≈Enthusiasm), .62 with Energy (≈Assertiveness) — no aspect preference (Eid 2003, M). rG = .61 with E4 Activity (Røysamb 2018, M). Unique genetic correlation with intelligence (Nikolašević 2021, S). E6 looks like a general E marker, not an Enthusiasm-specific one.

  - *Residual heritability is uniform:* Range .23–.36, all AE models (Jang 1998, L). Aspect-level means differ only slightly (Enthusiasm: .27; Assertiveness: .31). No strong dissociation.

  - *Common genetic factor fits E:* Both Briley & Tucker-Drob 2012 (M) and Johnson & Krueger 2004 found the common pathways model adequate for E, meaning a single genetic factor can account for E's genetic structure. But within this, Gregariousness has 60% facet-specific genetic variance (Briley 2012).

  - *Eid 2003 (M):* Genetic structure does NOT cleanly partition into two clusters corresponding to Enthusiasm and Assertiveness. A single common genetic factor (anchored by Positive Affect) with specific factors fits the data. 61–71% of genetic variance in each sub-facet is specific.

Genetic clustering verdict:** Genetic evidence is insufficient to support the published two-aspect clustering. The clearest genetic signal is that E4 Activity does not belong with other E facets — it clusters with C. Beyond that, the genetic evidence suggests a single general genetic factor for E plus substantial facet-specific variance, not two genetically distinct aspects. This is consistent with the structural finding that Assertiveness (as a tight construct) lives at the HEXACO Social Boldness level, while Enthusiasm is a broader composite — but the genetic data cannot distinguish these groupings.

**Step 3: Convergence Assessment**

        E6 domain-general, E2 has high specific genetic variance

**Summary of convergence:**The Enthusiasm grouping {E1, E2, E6} achieves **two-stream convergence** (developmental + structural). The genetic evidence is ambiguous — it neither clearly supports nor contradicts the grouping.

The published Assertiveness grouping {E3, E4, E5} achieves **zero-stream convergence**. E3, E4, and E5 diverge on all three evidence streams. This is the weakest published aspect grouping in any domain I've examined.

**Proposed within-domain groupings (evidence-driven):**

**Group 1: Enthusiasm — {E1, E2, E6}** (two-stream convergence: developmental + structural)

  - Internal coherence: moderate. E1 is slightly more stable than E2/E6 in some studies.
  - This grouping is real but not tight. Within-domain co-development r = .15 suggests even these three facets develop somewhat independently.

**Group 2: Assertiveness-Proper — {E3}** (strong developmental + structural signal, but it's a singleton)

  - E3 is the only facet that consistently increases through midlife.
  - Clean structural mapping to Social Dominance / HEXACO Social Boldness.
  - Cannot form a "group" with its published aspect-mates because those mates behave completely differently.

**Misbehaving facets that resist grouping:**- **E4 Activity:** Structurally and genetically misplaced. Developmentally aligns with Social Vitality (declining) not Social Dominance (increasing). Genetically clusters with C. Item content may confound personality with physical vitality. This facet does not belong with E3 on any evidence stream. Its strongest affinity is with C facets (genetic) or with E2/E6 (developmental direction, very loosely).
  - **E5 Excitement-Seeking:** Structurally homeless. Excluded from BFI-2. Phenotypically disconnected from E in external criterion studies. Highest facet-specific heritability in E. This facet is a candidate for removal from E entirely.

**Step 4: Misbehaving Facets**

**E4 Activity — strongest cross-domain affinity signal in E**

E4 is flagged by every study that examines it closely:

  - **Developmental:** Steepest decline, only age-differential facet, only retirement-sensitive E facet. Pattern matches Social Vitality (Roberts 2006, XL), not Assertiveness/Social Dominance.
  - **Genetic:** Clusters with C facets (Pilia 2006, XL). Age-heterogeneity pattern matches C. Does not cluster with aspect-mates E3 or E5.
  - **Structural:** Classification disagreement across three major taxonomies (Assertiveness in DeYoung; Social Vitality in Roberts; Enthusiasm-adjacent in BFI-2).
  - **Measurement concern:** Item content ("often feel as if I'm bursting with energy") may increasingly measure physical vitality rather than personality in older adults. Multiple authors flag this (Terracciano 2005, Brandt 2023, Löckenhoff 2009).

**Cross-domain affinity:** E4 → C (genetic clustering, age-heterogeneity pattern). This is flagged for Conversation 6.

**E5 Excitement-Seeking — candidate for domain removal**

  - Excluded from BFI-2 (Soto & John 2017).
  - Peripheral loading in DeYoung 2007.
  - No clean HEXACO equivalent.
  - r = .05 with life satisfaction while all other E facets r = .13–.30 (Røysamb 2018, M).
  - Highest specific heritability in E (.36 residual, .52 of reliable variance; Jang 1998, L).
  - Developmental trajectory unlike E3 (opposite curvature despite same aspect assignment).

E5's content (sensation-seeking, risk-taking, excitement-craving) overlaps more with impulsivity dimensions than with social dominance or warmth. It may be better placed near N5 Impulsiveness or as a standalone construct.

**E6 Positive Emotions — domain-general marker, not aspect-specific**

E6 is genetically correlated equally with Enthusiasm- and Assertiveness-adjacent constructs. Its age-heterogeneity pattern matches E4 and C (not its aspect-mate E1). Its unique genetic correlation with intelligence sets it apart from E1 and E2. E6 appears to be a general Extraversion marker — the "positive affect" core of the domain — rather than a facet that sits specifically on the Enthusiasm aspect.

**Step 5: Overall Domain Coherence**

**Extraversion is a structurally real domain with a severely incoherent aspect structure.**

The domain-level signal is genuine:

  - Common pathways model fits E genetically (Briley 2012, M; Johnson & Krueger 2004).
  - E has the highest domain-level rank-order stability in the Brandes (2020) child sample (r = .72).
  - Domain-level heritability is consistent (~.50 across studies).

But the published Assertiveness aspect {E3, E4, E5} is not recoverable from any evidence stream other than factor analysis in the original DeYoung 2007 sample. Developmentally, genetically, and in external criterion validity, E3, E4, and E5 behave as three unrelated facets. The "Assertiveness aspect" appears to be a factor-analytic artifact held together by what these facets are not (not warm, not gregarious, not emotionally positive) rather than by what they share.

The Enthusiasm aspect {E1, E2, E6} is more coherent — developmental and structural convergence — but even here, the genetic evidence is ambiguous, and E6 behaves more like a domain marker than an aspect-specific facet.

**The best-supported structure from this evidence base is:**- A core **Sociability/Warmth** group {E1, E2} with moderate coherence
  - A **Positive Emotionality** domain marker {E6} that belongs to E but not specifically to either aspect
  - An **Assertiveness/Social Dominance** singleton {E3} with strong developmental identity but no genetically validated group membership
  - A **Physical Vitality/Energy** facet {E4} with strong cross-domain affinity to C
  - A **Sensation-Seeking** facet {E5} that is structurally peripheral to E

This is not a clean two-aspect structure. It's more like one moderate cluster (E1+E2), one domain marker (E6), and three quasi-independent facets (E3, E4, E5).

**I notice this somewhat resembles the conclusion one might draw from the BFI-2 structure** (Sociability, Energy Level, Assertiveness as three separate facets rather than two aspects). I want to flag that I may be pattern-matching to that framework. The evidence that independently supports a three-way rather than two-way split: (a) the developmental trajectories don't cleanly bifurcate into two groups, (b) the genetic evidence supports a single common factor plus specifics rather than two aspect-level factors, (c) the cross-taxonomy mapping shows three distinguishable content clusters (sociability, energy/vitality, dominance/assertiveness). The evidence against a three-way split: (a) E6 Positive Emotions doesn't fit cleanly into any of the three, (b) the factor-analytic evidence consistently yields two, not three, intermediate factors.

**Step 6: Evidence Gaps**

  - **No genetic correlations between E1, E2, E3, E5, and E6 in the same biometric model.** Røysamb (2018) only modeled E4 and E6. Eid (2003) used NEO-FFI composites, not NEO-PI-R facets. A full 6×6 genetic correlation matrix among E facets does not exist in this corpus.**- Pilia (2006) supplementary Table S3** — genetic factor structure of all 30 facets — is cited as confirming E4's placement with C. If retrievable, it would provide genetic factor loadings at the facet level for all E facets, not just the E4→C finding reported in the text.**- No within-person developmental data specifically testing the aspect structure.** Haehner 2025 (L) uses BFAS aspects directly but only has a 2-year window. No study with all 30 NEO facets has tested whether E1+E2+E6 co-develop more than E1+E3 or E2+E5.**- No genetic data on E5 Excitement-Seeking beyond univariate h².** No study reports E5's genetic correlations with other E facets, other personality facets, or external criteria at the genetic level.**- The E4 Activity measurement confound is unresolved.** Multiple studies flag that E4 items may measure physical vitality rather than personality in older adults, but no study directly tests this (e.g., by controlling for objective health measures or comparing observer vs. self-report on E4 specifically).**- No study in this corpus reports E-facet developmental trajectories using fixed-effects models.** Seifert 2023 (XL) used FEM but only at the item level with non-NEO instruments. FEM estimates for all 30 NEO facets would be transformative.**- E6 Positive Emotions' structural status is unresolved.** Is it an Enthusiasm facet, a general E marker, or something else? The genetic evidence points to "general E marker" but no study directly tests this against the aspect model.**- No adolescent developmental data at the NEO-PI-R facet level.** Ringwald 2024 uses BFI-2 facets; Brandes 2020 uses ICID-S. The 14–25 age range — where some of the most dramatic E changes occur — lacks NEO-level facet resolution.**Appendix: Pilia et al. (2006) Supplementary Tables S2 and S3**

These tables were cited throughout the extractions as missing but critical. They are now available in the project files. Source quality: **XL** (N = 6,148, extended pedigree design, all 30 NEO-PI-R facets). The data below are Sardinian, so generalizability caveats apply, but the sample size and design make these the best available estimates of genetic factor structure at the facet level.

**Implications for the E-Domain Profile**

These data substantially revise the genetic evidence stream for Extraversion. Key changes to the synthesis:

**1. E4 Activity is genetically a C facet — now confirmed quantitatively**E4's primary genetic factor loading is C = **.65**, exceeding its E loading of .47. It is the only E facet whose largest genetic loading is on a non-E factor. Table S2 confirms: E4 clusters genetically with C4 Achievement Striving (rG = .775), not with any E facet.

This upgrades the E4 dissociation from "strong based on text description" to **strong based on direct quantitative evidence at XL quality**. E4 Activity is genetically a Conscientiousness facet that phenotypically loads on Extraversion. The main profile's conclusion that E4 has cross-domain affinity to C is now confirmed at the highest available evidence level.

**2. E3 Assertiveness is genetically a three-domain facet**

E3 loads E = .51, O = .44, C = .42. All three are substantial. Genetically, E3 is not a clean Extraversion facet — it is an agentic composite drawing from Extraversion (social boldness), Openness (intellectual engagement), and Conscientiousness (goal-directed effort). This converges with Ludeke 2019 (L) finding that HEXACO C-Diligence correlates with Assertiveness, Industriousness, Intellect, and low Withdrawal simultaneously.

This has implications for the Assertiveness "aspect": the construct DeYoung (2007) labeled Assertiveness may be genetically broader than any single Big Five domain, tapping a cross-domain agency factor. Flag for Conversation 6.

**3. E1 Warmth cross-loads genetically on A (.45)**

E1's A loading (.45) is nearly two-thirds of its E loading (.72). Genetically, Warmth is a hybrid E/A facet. This converges with: (a) E1's original placement as an A facet in Costa & McCrae (1985), (b) the Brandes (2020, L) finding that warmth/prosociality content migrates between E and A in child development, (c) the general observation that warmth/prosociality sits at the E–A boundary.

This moderately weakens the Enthusiasm grouping's genetic coherence. E2 Gregariousness (.71 E, .17 A) is a clean E facet; E1 Warmth (.72 E, .45 A) is a hybrid. Two of three Enthusiasm facets are not pure E genetically (E1 cross-loads on A; E6's genetic profile is discussed below).

4. E5 Excitement-Seeking loads genetically on low A (−.43)

E5's second-largest genetic loading is −.43 on Agreeableness — antagonism. Genetically, E5 is a high-E/low-A composite. This gives it genetic affinity with the HEXACO rotation (where sensation-seeking content distributes across E and low-H/low-A). It also converges with E5's exclusion from the BFI-2 and its phenotypic disconnection from other E facets — it is capturing a genetically distinct behavioral tendency (approach toward exciting/risky stimuli combined with low concern for others' reactions) that only partially overlaps with Extraversion.

**5. E6 Positive Emotions has moderate but not primary cross-loadings**

E6 loads E = .67, with secondary loadings on low N (−.40) and O (.39). The low-N loading is the largest N cross-loading among E facets, consistent with E6 being the affective/emotional core of E that inversely tracks negative emotionality. The O loading is consistent with the "approach motivation" interpretation (positive emotions facilitate exploration/openness). E6 does not genetically cross-load on A or C, distinguishing it from E1 (A cross-load) and E3/E4 (C cross-loads).

This partially resolves the main profile's question about E6's structural status. Genetically, E6 is an E facet with N and O coloring — consistent with being a domain-general E marker that captures the affective valence dimension, not an Enthusiasm-specific facet.

**6. E2 Gregariousness is the genetically cleanest E facet**

E2 loads .71 on E, with the next-largest loading being −.35 on N. No substantial cross-loadings on O, A, or C. This makes E2 the genetically purest marker of the Extraversion factor. Its substantial facet-specific genetic variance (60% in Briley 2012) reflects specificity *within* E, not leakage to other domains.

**7. Cluster 18 reveals genetic entanglement of E with N**

Three E facets (E1, E3, E6) cluster genetically with five N facets (N1, N2, N3, N4, N6) at rG > .50. This is not an E-internal cluster — it is a cross-domain cluster. The E facets that are most genetically intertwined with other constructs are not intertwined with each other in isolation; they are intertwined with Neuroticism facets, forming a large general-adjustment/wellbeing genetic cluster.

Notably absent from Cluster 18: E2 Gregariousness (genetically clean E), E4 Activity (genetically with C), E5 Excitement-Seeking (genetically with low A). The three "missing" E facets are each in a different genetic neighborhood. There is no genetic cluster consisting of E facets only.

**8. No E-internal genetic cluster exists at rG > .50**

Table S2 contains no cluster consisting exclusively of two or more E facets. The E facets that cluster genetically do so with facets from N, C, or A — not with each other. This is the strongest possible evidence against genetically coherent E aspects. At the genetic level, Extraversion is a phenotypic composite: its facets share enough genetic variance to produce a recognizable E factor (congruence coefficient = .89), but they do not form tight within-E genetic groupings.

**Revised Clustering C (Genetic Evidence) — incorporating Tables S2 and S3**

The original Clustering C stated: "Genetic evidence is insufficient to support the published two-aspect clustering." The supplementary data strengthen this conclusion and make it more specific:

**There are no within-E genetic clusters.** Every E facet's strongest genetic affiliations are cross-domain:

  - E1 → A (.45)
  - E2 → clean E (no strong cross-loading)
  - E3 → O (.44) and C (.42)
  - E4 → C (.65, primary loading)
  - E5 → low A (−.43)
  - E6 → low N (−.40) and O (.39)

The published Enthusiasm grouping {E1, E2, E6} has moderate genetic coherence in the sense that all three load ≥.67 on the E factor. But their secondary genetic profiles diverge completely: E1 → A, E2 → nothing, E6 → low-N and O. They are united on E and nowhere else.

The published Assertiveness grouping {E3, E4, E5} has poor genetic coherence. E3 loads on three domains (E/O/C), E4's primary loading is C, and E5's secondary loading is low-A. They do not share a genetic profile beyond being E-relevant.

**Revised genetic clustering verdict:** The genetic architecture of Extraversion is organized around a single broad E factor — visible in Table S3's E column — with every facet additionally influenced by one or more non-E genetic dimensions. The two-aspect structure has no genetic support. The single-factor-plus-specifics model from Eid (2003, M) is now confirmed at XL quality by Table S3.

**Revised Step 5 Assessment (incorporating supplementary data)**

The main profile's overall assessment — "Extraversion has a genuine domain-level signal sitting on top of a broken aspect structure" — is confirmed and sharpened by the supplementary data.

The domain-level genetic signal is real: factor congruence = .89, and all six E facets load ≥.47 on the genetic E factor. But the aspect-level structure has zero genetic support: no E-internal genetic clusters exist, and every facet's secondary genetic profile points to a different non-E domain.

The Enthusiasm grouping {E1, E2, E6} retains two-stream convergence (developmental + structural), but genetic evidence is now actively ambiguous-to-negative rather than simply absent. The Assertiveness grouping {E3, E4, E5} remains at zero-stream convergence, now with positive genetic evidence against it (E4's primary genetic loading is C; E3 is an agentic cross-domain composite; E5 is a high-E/low-A construct).

**Revised Evidence Gaps (removing resolved items)**

Gap 1 from the original list ("No genetic correlations between E facets in the same biometric model") is now **partially resolved** by Tables S2 and S3. We have the genetic factor structure and clustering, though not the raw pairwise genetic correlation matrix.

Gap 2 ("Pilia Table S3") is **resolved**.

Remaining gaps from original list: 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8 are unchanged.

**New gap identified:** The raw 30×30 pairwise genetic correlation matrix from Pilia (2006) was reportedly available at http://www.sph.umich.edu/csg/chen/public/sardinia. Tables S2 and S3 summarize this matrix (via clustering and factor analysis), but the full matrix would allow testing specific pairwise questions — e.g., is E1–E2 rG higher than E1–E3 rG? The summary tables cannot answer such questions.

**Extraversion — Supplementary Synthesis (v2 Studies Only)**

This synthesis is produced exclusively from the studies in the v2 extraction files. It does not incorporate any findings from the original synthesis.

**Step 2: Three Independent Clusterings**

**Clustering A — Developmental Trajectories Only**

**Evidence base is thin.** Only one study (Bleidorn 2009, M) provides developmental data in this corpus, covering ages 18–59 over ~10 years in a German twin sample.

**Cluster A1: Increasers — {E3 Assertiveness, E4 Activity}** Both increased significantly over 10 years. E3 had the largest slope variance of any E facet. E4's increase was smaller.

**Cluster A2: Decreasers — {E2 Gregariousness, E5 Excitement-Seeking}** Both decreased significantly. E5's developmental direction is opposite to its Assertiveness aspect-mates E3 and E4.

**Cluster A3: Stable — {E1 Warmth, E6 Positive Emotions}** Neither showed significant mean-level change.

**Developmental clustering verdict:** The aspect boundary is not recovered. Assertiveness-assigned facets {E3, E4, E5} split: E3 and E4 increase while E5 decreases. E5 clusters developmentally with Enthusiasm-assigned E2. The Enthusiasm group {E1, E2, E6} splits: E2 decreases while E1 and E6 are stable.

*Major caveat:* This clustering rests on a single M-quality study. It should be treated as suggestive only.

**Clustering B — Structural Mapping Only**

Using factor-analytic loadings, cross-taxonomy placement, and network-based evidence:

**Cluster B1: Enthusiasm Core — {E1, E2, E6}** All three are mapped to Enthusiasm in DeYoung 2007. BFI-2 maps Sociability and Energy Level (which absorbs E6 content) to Enthusiasm (Soto & John 2017, XL). E2 Gregariousness is the most factor-pure E facet (Soto & John 2017; Yamagata 2006). E1 Warmth has a secondary A loading but its primary home is E-Enthusiasm. E6 Positive Emotions loads on Enthusiasm with secondary N and O genetic loadings.

Within-domain aspect correlation: Enthusiasm ↔ Assertiveness r = .49 (Haehner 2025, L) — moderate, suggesting partially but not fully overlapping constructs.

Enthusiasm's unmodified CFA fit was poor in German (CFI = .74; Haehner 2025, L), suggesting the aspect may mix heterogeneous content (sociability vs. positive affect).

**Cluster B2: Assertiveness-Proper — {E3}** E3 maps cleanly to Assertiveness across all taxonomies. BFI-2 Assertiveness correlates .85 with BFAS Assertiveness (Soto & John 2017, XL). Has secondary structural cross-loadings on C and O but primary home is E-Assertiveness.

**Structurally Contested — {E4 Activity}** DeYoung 2007: Assertiveness. BFI-2: Energy Level → Enthusiasm. BFI-2 Energy Level's between-domain correlation with C-Productiveness exceeded its within-E correlation with Assertiveness in 2 of 3 Swedish samples (Zakrisson 2025, L). Cross-taxonomy disagreement is the structural finding.

**Structurally Homeless — {E5 Excitement-Seeking}** Excluded from BFI-2 (Soto & John 2017, XL). Peripheral in DeYoung 2007. No BFAS aspect assignment in Soto & John's crosswalk table (Table 1 — dash for E5's BFAS alignment).

**Structural clustering verdict:** {E1, E2, E6} = Enthusiasm (strong, though E1 has A affinity and E6 is domain-general). {E3} = Assertiveness core (strong). E4 is contested between Enthusiasm, Assertiveness, and C. E5 is homeless. The published 3+3 aspect split is not cleanly recoverable — the "Assertiveness aspect" beyond E3 itself dissolves.

**Clustering C — Genetic Evidence Only**

**Evidence base is much richer than the developmental stream.** Three studies contribute: Jang 2002 (L), Yamagata 2006 (XL), and Mitrović 2024 (S). Lewis & Bates 2014 (M) adds HEXACO data.

**Key finding 1: Two genetic factors exist within E, but they do NOT map onto the published aspects.**

Jang 2002 (L) found two common genetic factors per domain, including E. The E factors are:

  - Factor 1 "Sociability": led by E2 (.71/.69), with moderate loadings from E1, E5, E6, E3. E4 loads weakly.
  - Factor 2 "Agentic": in Germany, led by E3 (.48) and E4 (.46). In Canada, led by E1 (.50) and E6 (.47). Cross-sample instability in which facets define this factor.

The German Factor 2 loosely resembles Assertiveness. The Canadian Factor 2 does not — it is led by Warmth and Positive Emotions, which are Enthusiasm facets. The two-factor genetic structure is real (the single-factor model was rejected), but its content shifts across cultures.

**Key finding 2: E4 Activity has a replicable genetic cross-loading on C.**

Yamagata 2006 (XL): E4's genetic C loading was .48 / .53 / .54 across Canada, Germany, Japan. This is stronger than its phenotypic C loading (.39/.39/.38) and stronger than its E loading in some samples. The cross-loading replicates across three independent national twin samples. This is the single most robust cross-domain genetic finding in the entire corpus.

**Key finding 3: E3 Assertiveness is genetically diffuse.**

Yamagata 2006 (XL): E3 loads genetically on E (.45–.55), low-N (−.19 to −.43), low-A (−.26 to −.44), and C (.30–.39). It touches four domains genetically. E3's genetic E loading is only moderate — it is not a "pure" genetic E facet.

**Key finding 4: E5 Excitement-Seeking is mostly genetically unique.**

Jang 2002 (L): 68–70% of E5's genetic variance is facet-specific. It loads only on Factor 1 (the sociability/gregariousness factor), and weakly. Most of E5's genetic basis is not shared with any other E facet.

**Key finding 5: COMT V158M cleanly distinguishes Enthusiasm from Assertiveness facets.**

Mitrović 2024 (S): E1, E2, E6 all associated; E3, E4, E5 not associated. This is the published aspect boundary exactly. However, this is a single candidate gene with small effects (d = 0.16–0.21) and no replication.

**Key finding 6: HEXACO Extraversion shows a genetic sub-cluster consistent with Enthusiasm.**

Lewis & Bates 2014 (M): Liveliness and Social Self-Esteem share genetic variance beyond the common E factor (cross-path a = .44). These are positive-affect-loaded facets, loosely mapping to Enthusiasm. Social Boldness (≈ Assertiveness) is genetically separate.

**Genetic clustering verdict:** Two genetic factors exist within E (Jang 2002, L — replicated across two countries). However, these factors do not stably map onto the published aspects. Factor 1 is a broad sociability factor. Factor 2 shifts in content across cultures. E4 Activity's strongest genetic signal is its cross-loading on C, not its within-E placement. E5 is mostly genetically unique. E3 is genetically diffuse across four domains. The COMT finding provides weak molecular support for the Enthusiasm/Assertiveness distinction, and the HEXACO data provide moderate support for a positive-affect sub-cluster within E.

On balance: There is genetic evidence for *some* within-E differentiation, but it does not cleanly recover the published two-aspect structure.

**Summary:**The strongest convergence signal: **E2 and E3 are separable across all three streams.** E2 is the prototypical Enthusiasm facet (declines, defines sociability genetic factor, COMT-associated). E3 is the prototypical Assertiveness facet (increases, genetically multi-domain, COMT-unassociated). Their dissociation is the cleanest evidence for within-E structure.

The published Enthusiasm grouping {E1, E2, E6}: structural convergence is strong; developmental convergence is partial (E1 and E6 cohere but E2 diverges); genetic convergence is weak (they don't load on the same single genetic factor).

The published Assertiveness grouping {E3, E4, E5}: zero convergence for E3–E5 and E4–E5 across any stream. E3 and E4 converge developmentally and partially genetically in the German sample, but E4's primary genetic signal is its C cross-loading.

**Step 4: Misbehaving Facets**

**E4 Activity — genetic C facet with an E phenotype**

Genetic C cross-loading .48–.54 across three countries (Yamagata 2006, XL). BFI-2 Energy Level (absorbing E4 content) pulls toward C in 2 of 3 Swedish samples (Zakrisson 2025, L). BFI-2 deliberately reclassified E4-like content from Assertiveness to Enthusiasm. Not COMT-associated (unlike Enthusiasm facets). In Bleidorn 2009, E4 increased (like E3, its Assertiveness aspect-mate), which is the one piece of evidence favoring its current assignment in this corpus.

Cross-domain affinity: E4 → C. Flag for cross-domain synthesis.

**E5 Excitement-Seeking — structurally peripheral, genetically unique**

Excluded from BFI-2 (Soto & John 2017, XL). 68–70% of genetic variance is facet-specific (Jang 2002, L). Developmental direction opposite to Assertiveness aspect-mates E3 and E4 (Bleidorn 2009, M). Not COMT-associated. E5's genetic basis is largely independent of other E facets.

**Strongest domain-removal candidate in E.**

**E3 Assertiveness — genetically multi-domain**

Phenotypically clean Assertiveness marker. But genetically loads on four domains: E, low-N, low-A, C (Yamagata 2006, XL). BFI-2 Assertiveness has cross-loadings on C (.23/.24) and O (.24/.24) (Soto & John 2017, XL). E3 may represent a cross-domain "agency" construct rather than a pure E facet.

Cross-domain affinity: E3 → low-N, low-A, and C. Flag for cross-domain synthesis.

**E1 Warmth — E/A genetic boundary facet**

Genetic A loading = .45 alongside E loading of .72 (Yamagata 2006, XL). Loads on both within-E genetic factors (Jang 2002). Phenotypically mapped to Enthusiasm, but genetically half-A.

**Step 5: Overall Domain Coherence**

**Extraversion has a recoverable domain-level genetic factor, but its internal aspect structure is not genetically stable across cultures.**

Evidence for domain-level coherence:

  - Yamagata 2006 (XL): genetic E factor replicates across Canada, Germany, Japan with congruence coefficients of .92–.96. The genetic five-factor structure is "genetically crisp."
  - Lewis & Bates 2014 (M): common genetic E factor fits the HEXACO Extraversion data (with modifications).
  - All E facets load ≥.45 on the genetic E factor (Yamagata 2006).

Evidence against clean aspect-level structure:

  - Two genetic factors exist within E (Jang 2002, L — single-factor model rejected), but their content shifts across cultures.
  - No E-internal genetic cluster at rG > .50 exists (Pilia S2 data from project files).
  - The COMT molecular data (Mitrović 2024, S) provide the only evidence cleanly supporting the Enthusiasm/Assertiveness split at a biological level, but at very low quality (single candidate gene, small effects).
  - E4's genetic signal is dominated by its C cross-loading, not its E placement.
  - E5 is 68–70% genetically unique.
  - E3 is genetically spread across four domains.

**Haehner 2025 (L) provides the best external validity data:** Assertiveness and Enthusiasm are only moderately correlated (r = .49). They diverge on political orientation (Assertiveness slightly conservative; Enthusiasm neutral). But they are similar in life satisfaction and self-esteem correlations — unlike most other within-domain aspect pairs (e.g., Industriousness dwarfs Orderliness on LS; Withdrawal dwarfs Volatility on SE).

**The Enthusiasm aspect has better unidimensional CFA fit than most other aspects** in the Haehner data — but it was notably poor without modifications in German (CFI = .74), suggesting it mixes sociability and positive-affect content that don't form a clean unidimensional construct.

**Bottom line from these studies:** Extraversion's domain-level factor is genetically real and cross-culturally stable. The two-aspect structure has modest structural support, one piece of molecular evidence, and no stable genetic support across cultures. The strongest within-E signal is the E2–E3 dissociation (sociability vs. assertiveness) — the rest is noise, boundary-crossing, or facet-specific variance.

**Step 6: Evidence Gaps**

  - **Developmental evidence is severely underpowered in this corpus.** Only Bleidorn 2009 (M, N = 344, ages 18–59) provides trajectory data. All developmental claims rest on this single study.**- No within-E genetic correlation matrix is available from these studies.** Jang 2002 provides factor loadings but not the raw 6×6 genetic correlation matrix among E facets. Pilia S2/S3 (from project files) fills this gap but is not in the current extraction.**- The cross-cultural instability of E's genetic Factor 2 (Jang 2002) is unresolved.** In Germany it resembles Assertiveness; in Canada it does not. No study in this corpus explains why.**- No study directly tests whether two-aspect genetic models fit E better than one-factor models.** Jang 2002 found two factors needed, but did not test whether those factors align with the published aspects. A targeted confirmatory test (Enthusiasm factor vs. Assertiveness factor) has not been reported.**- COMT finding (Mitrović 2024) is unreplicated.** The clean Enthusiasm/Assertiveness molecular split on COMT is the only direct biological evidence for the aspect boundary, but it's a single candidate-gene study with small effects.**- No BFAS study reports cross-loadings of items onto non-assigned aspects.** Haehner 2025 tested each aspect unidimensionally but did not fit a full 10-aspect model or report how items relate to non-assigned aspects.**- E5 Excitement-Seeking's domain membership is untested with multivariate genetic models that include non-E facets.** Its 68–70% genetic specificity within E (Jang 2002) raises the question of whether that specific variance is shared with facets in other domains (e.g., N5 Impulsiveness). The within-domain-only design of Jang 2002 cannot answer this.**

*Agreeableness*********

**Agreeableness Domain Synthesis: A1–A6**

**Step 1: Facet Profile Table**

**A1 Trust** **Dimension****** **Evidence****** **Source(s)****** **Quality****** **Confidence****** **Heritability******

        h² = .30 additive (Jang 1996); raw h² = .31–.38 cross-culturally (Jang 1998); residual h² = .25–.31 AE (Jang 1998). Sex heterogeneity: higher h² in females (Pilia 2006).

        Jang 1996 (M); Jang 1998 (L); Pilia 2006 (XL)

        Moderate raw h² supported across studies. Residual h² indicates heritable specific variance beyond the A factor.

        **Moderate signal****** **Developmental trajectory******

        Inverted-U: increases until ~60, then declines. Most pronounced reversal of any A facet (Terracciano 2005). Increases from 30–70 in MSEM (Schwaba 2022). Inverted-U rank-order stability (Brandt 2023). Trustfulness trends positive across 40 years (Soto & John 2012).

        Terracciano 2005 (L); Schwaba 2022 (M); Brandt 2023 (L); Soto & John 2012 (M)

        Inverted-U replicated across studies. The late-life decline is the distinctive feature — possibly reflects cognitive evaluation of others' motives rather than affective warmth.

        **Moderate signal****** **Structural placement******

        Dual-loading: .42/.42 on both Compassion and Politeness in DeYoung (2007). Standalone facet in BFI-2 with no aspect alignment (Soto & John 2017).

        DeYoung 2007; Soto & John 2017

        Two independent structural analyses agree: Trust does not belong cleanly to either aspect. It marks the A domain, not a sub-grouping.

        **Strong signal****** **Genetic correlations******

        Large negative rG with N facets (−.53 to −.75; Jang 2001). Significant 5-HTTLPR association (h² = .019; Jang 2001). Behaves like partially inverted Neuroticism at the genetic level.

        Jang 2001 (L)

        Three independent samples strengthen the A1–N genetic overlap finding.

        **Moderate signal****** **Cross-taxonomy******

        No clean HEXACO home. Trust content distributes across HEXACO domains. In BFI-2, left without aspect assignment.

        Ludeke 2019 (L); Soto & John 2017

        Consistent: Trust is taxonomically orphaned.

        **Moderate signal****** **Dissociation flags******

        (1) Dual-loading in factor analysis. (2) Inverted-U trajectory unlike linear Politeness facets or flat A3/A5. (3) Genetically resembles inverted-N more than other A facets. (4) Standalone in BFI-2.

        Multiple

        A1 Trust is the most structurally anomalous A facet. Every evidence stream flags it as not fitting cleanly within A.

**A2 Straightforwardness**

        **Dimension****** **Evidence****** **Source(s)****** **Quality****** **Confidence****** **Heritability******

        h² = .47 additive (Jang 1996); raw h² = .31 combined (Jang 1998); residual h² = .25 AE (Jang 1998). Genetic outlier in Pilia (2006): very large non-additive component (narrow .134, broad .413 — largest gap in A domain), near-3× sex difference (males .067, females .183).

        Jang 1996 (M); Jang 1998 (L); Pilia 2006 (XL)

        Heritable with specific genetic variance beyond the A factor. Pilia shows a distinctive genetic architecture (large dominance, strong sex effect).

        **Moderate signal** (raw h²); **weak signal** (architecture details, single study)

        **Developmental trajectory******

        Clear linear increase across adulthood (Terracciano 2005). Increases 30–70 (Schwaba 2022). Non-monotonic rank-order stability (Brandt 2023).

        Terracciano 2005 (L); Schwaba 2022 (M); Brandt 2023 (L)

        Linear increase replicated. Rank-order pattern is more complex.

        **Moderate signal****** **Structural placement******

        Loads on Politeness (DeYoung 2007). Politeness maps primarily to HEXACO Honesty-Humility (r = .51; Ludeke 2019). A2 content captures Honesty-Humility variance — so its steep developmental increase is expected from the HH age trend.

        DeYoung 2007; Ludeke 2019 (L); Ashton & Lee 2016 (L)

        Clean Politeness placement.

        **Strong signal****** **Genetic correlations******

        None specifically reported for A2 relative to other facets.

        —

        Gap.

        —

        **Cross-taxonomy******

        Maps to HEXACO Honesty-Humility content (sincerity, straightforwardness).

        Ludeke 2019 (L); Ashton & Lee 2016 (L)

        Consistent.

        **Moderate signal****** **Dissociation flags******

        (1) Genetic outlier in Pilia: large non-additive component and sex difference unlike other Politeness facets. (2) Non-monotonic rank-order, unlike A4's declining stability.

        Pilia 2006 (XL); Brandt 2023 (L)

        Genetic architecture is distinctive, but this could reflect A2 tapping deception-detection or social desirability processes that happen to be genetically distinctive while still properly within A.

        Jang 1996 (M); Jang 1998 (L); Pilia 2006 (XL)

        Whatever makes Altruism distinct from the A factor is NOT genetically influenced — it's environmentally shaped. This is one of only 4 CE-residual facets.

        **Moderate signal****** **Developmental trajectory******

        Flat or very modest increase. Already at "healthy ideal" by age 30, ceiling effect (Schwaba 2022). Weak convex curve (Terracciano 2005). All A facets increase in children (Brandes 2020).

        Flatness is likely ceiling artifact, not structural distinctiveness. Hard to interpret developmentally.

        DeYoung 2007; Soto & John 2017

        Like A1, A3 is a domain marker. DeYoung (2007) explicitly noted no good NEO markers for Compassion specifically.

        Not specifically reported for A3 in the available studies.

        HEXACO Altruism interstitial facet shares variance with H, Emotionality, and A. Ashton & Lee (2016) show the Altruism facet drives Big Five A's upward age trend.

        Altruism is cross-domain content.

        (1) Dual-loading (.65/.64) — domain marker, not aspect marker. (2) Lowest raw h² in A. (3) No heritable specific variance. (4) Flat developmental trajectory (ceiling).

        Multiple

        A3 has weak genetic specificity and ambiguous structural placement. Its raw heritability is entirely inherited from the A factor, with nothing genetically unique to the facet itself.

        Maps to HEXACO Flexibility (compliance, accommodation). Ashton & Lee (2016) distinguish Flexibility (monotonic increase) from Forgivingness/Patience (U-shaped), suggesting the compliance content and anger-control content of HEXACO-A have different developmental courses.

        Ashton & Lee 2016 (L)

        A4 content (compliance) develops differently from anger-control content (which maps more to reversed Volatility).

        Multiple

        A4 is the best-behaved Politeness facet: clean structural loading, clear developmental trajectory, moderate heritability. The Pilia age-h² overlap with A3 is a cross-aspect signal but from a single study.

        **Dimension****** **Evidence****** **Source(s)****** **Quality****** **Confidence****** **Heritability******

        NO genetic variance in Jang (1996): CE model, c² = .33, e² = .67. rDZ (.36) > rMZ (.30) — reversed MZ/DZ pattern. Extreme cross-cultural instability: h² = .00 in Canada, h² = .46 in Germany (Jang 1998). Residual CE: no heritable specific variance (c² = .26, e² = .74; Jang 1998).

        Jang 1996 (M); Jang 1998 (L)

        The most genetically anomalous A facet. Reversed twin correlations and wildly different heritability across cultures suggest Modesty is substantially shaped by culture/environment, not genes. The authors explicitly discuss it as a candidate "characteristic adaptation" rather than "basic tendency."

        **Moderate signal** (that it's environmentally shaped)

        **Developmental trajectory******

        Flat: barely changes across adulthood (Terracciano 2005; Schwaba 2022). U-shaped rank-order stability (Brandt 2023). 90% of longitudinal participants showed trivial or small increases — almost no individual variability (Soto & John 2012). One of only 2 facets with gender × age interaction (Terracciano 2005).

        Terracciano 2005 (L); Schwaba 2022 (M); Brandt 2023 (L); Soto & John 2012 (M)

        Flat trajectory replicated across studies. Minimal individual variability in change — unusual among personality facets.

        **Strong signal****** **Structural placement******

        Loads on Politeness (DeYoung 2007). Politeness maps to HEXACO Honesty-Humility. A5 content (humility, self-effacement) is core HH content.

        DeYoung 2007; Ludeke 2019 (L)

        Clean Politeness placement structurally.

        **Strong signal****** **Genetic correlations******

        None reported.

        —

        Gap.

        —

        **Cross-taxonomy******

        Maps to HEXACO Honesty-Humility (Modesty facet). Ashton & Lee (2016) found HH increases ~1.0 SD — but A5 is flat in Big Five studies. This discrepancy may reflect measurement differences or the fact that the NEO A5 items tap a narrower, more culturally variable construct than the HEXACO Modesty facet.

        Ashton & Lee 2016 (L)

        A5's developmental flatness in NEO studies contrasts with the steep HH trend in HEXACO, despite mapping to similar content. Puzzling.

        **Conflicted signal****** **Dissociation flags******

        (1) Reversed MZ/DZ twin correlations. (2) Cross-cultural heritability instability (0 vs .46). (3) Flat while aspect-mates A2 and A4 increase. (4) No heritable specific variance. (5) Minimal individual variability in change. (6) HH content should predict steep increase, but it doesn't.

        Multiple

        A5 Modesty is the most problematic facet in the A domain. It dissociates from its Politeness aspect-mates developmentally, its genetic architecture is culture-dependent, and it has no heritable specific variance. It looks more like a culturally shaped behavioral pattern than an endogenous disposition.

**A6 Tender-Mindedness**

        **Dimension****** **Evidence****** **Source(s)****** **Quality****** **Confidence****** **Heritability******

        d² = .45, nonadditive (Jang 1996, but nonadditive effects didn't replicate in Jang 1998). Raw h² = .38 (Jang 1998). Residual h² = .28 AE (Jang 1998).

        Jang 1996 (M); Jang 1998 (L)

        Moderate, heritable with specific genetic variance.

        **Moderate signal****** **Developmental trajectory******

        Increase then decline (convex), similar to A1 Trust but less pronounced decline (Terracciano 2005). Increases 30–70 (Schwaba 2022). HEXACO Sentimentality (≈ A6) increases ~0.33 SD from teens to 60 (Ashton & Lee 2016).

        Terracciano 2005 (L); Schwaba 2022 (M); Ashton & Lee 2016 (M)

        Upward trend replicated. Convex shape is consistent across studies.

        **Moderate signal****** **Structural placement******

        Loads on Compassion (DeYoung 2007). In HEXACO, Sentimentality (≈ A6) is under Emotionality domain, not Agreeableness — suggesting A/N boundary content.

        DeYoung 2007; Ashton & Lee 2016 (L)

        Clean Compassion loading in Big Five space, but HEXACO rotation puts it in Emotionality. This is a structural signal that A6 captures emotional reactivity content that belongs partly to the N/Emotionality space.

        **Moderate signal****** **Genetic correlations******

        Small, inconsistent rG with N facets (−.54 to +.26, highly variable across samples; Jang 2001). No 5-HTTLPR association. Genetically dissimilar from A1 Trust despite both being Compassion facets.

        Jang 2001 (L)

        The A1–A6 genetic dissociation within Compassion is notable. Trust behaves like inverted-N genetically; Tender-Mindedness does not.

        **Moderate signal****** **Cross-taxonomy******

        Maps to HEXACO Emotionality–Sentimentality. Developmental trajectory (upward) aligns with Sentimentality trend and diverges from HEXACO Emotionality–Anxiety (downward) and Fearfulness (flat).

        Ashton & Lee 2016 (L)

        A6 develops like a prosocial-emotional facet, not like an anxiety/fear facet. Its HEXACO Emotionality placement reflects shared emotional-reactivity content, but its developmental trajectory is A-like (increasing) not N-like (decreasing).

        **Moderate signal****** **Dissociation flags******

        (1) Genetically dissimilar from A1 Trust within Compassion. (2) Sits at A/N boundary in HEXACO rotation. (3) Sentimentality content shares more with prosocial emotion than with withdrawal/anxiety.

        Jang 2001 (L); Ashton & Lee 2016 (L)

        A6 is the cleanest Compassion facet by structural loading, but its HEXACO position and genetic dissimilarity from A1 suggest "Compassion" as a genetic unit is shaky.

        —

**Step 2: Three Independent Clusterings**

**Clustering A — Developmental Trajectories Only**

Evidence used: Terracciano 2005 (L), Schwaba 2022 (M), Brandt 2023 (L), Soto 2011 (M), Soto & John 2012 (M), Ringwald 2024 (L), Seifert 2023 (XL), Ashton & Lee 2016 (M), Löckenhoff 2009 (M/S), Brandes 2020 (L), Haehner 2025 (L).

**Cluster D1 — Linear increasers: {A2 Straightforwardness, A4 Compliance}** Both show clear, monotonic linear increases across adulthood. A4 is the steeper of the two. A4 is uniquely retirement-sensitive. Both are Politeness facets. This is the tightest developmental pairing in the A domain.

**Cluster D2 — Convex (rise-then-plateau-or-decline): {A1 Trust, A6 Tender-Mindedness}** Both show increases that peak and then decline (or flatten) in later life. A1's decline is more pronounced and earlier than A6's. They share a trajectory shape but differ in magnitude of the late-life reversal. Both are assigned to Compassion. However, A1's late-life decline may reflect a different mechanism (increasing wariness/cynicism — cognitive evaluation) than A6's trajectory (emotional reactivity).

**Isolate D-i — A3 Altruism** Flat, already at ceiling by age 30 (Schwaba 2022, M). Weak convex shape (Terracciano 2005, L). Cannot be cleanly clustered because the flatness is likely a ceiling artifact, not a structural signal. If the ceiling weren't there, A3 might track with D2. But we can't know that.

**Isolate D-ii — A5 Modesty** Flat, minimal individual variability in change. Aspect-mates A2 and A4 both increase; A5 does not. Developmental behavior is completely unlike the rest of Politeness. One of the most developmentally inert personality facets in the entire NEO system.

**Additional developmental signals:**

  - Within-domain co-development is low: mean r_change = .21 (Schwaba 2022, M), and A facets have the lowest same-domain change correlations of any Big Five domain (avg r = .28; Soto & John 2012, M).
  - Haehner (2025, L): Compassion intercepts decline from ~40; Politeness intercepts slightly increase. Correlated change between the two aspects is the weakest of any within-domain pair (r = .20, but CI is enormous: −.47 to .86).
  - Seifert (2023, XL): The most dramatic within-domain item-level divergence in the entire paper is in Agreeableness. Kindness items increase; forgiving and effortful-prosocial items decrease or flatten. This split does NOT map onto Compassion vs. Politeness — it cross-cuts the aspect boundary.
  - Brandt (2023, L): A facets showed the most variable rank-order stability patterns of any domain. The diversity does not map cleanly onto aspect boundaries.

**Clustering B — Structural Mapping Only**

Evidence used: DeYoung (2007), Soto & John (2017), Ludeke (2019, L), Hansen (2025, M), Ashton & Lee (2016, L), Briley & Tucker-Drob (2012, M).

**Cluster S1 — Compassion anchor: {A6 Tender-Mindedness}** The only facet with a clean, unambiguous Compassion loading. In HEXACO, maps to Emotionality–Sentimentality (A/N boundary), but developmental trajectory confirms A-domain placement.

**Cluster S2 — Politeness: {A2 Straightforwardness, A4 Compliance, A5 Modesty}** All three load on Politeness in DeYoung (2007). Politeness maps primarily to HEXACO Honesty-Humility (r = .51; Ludeke 2019). When Compassion and Politeness jointly predict H-H, Compassion β drops to .07 — Politeness owns the HH overlap.

**Domain markers, no aspect home: {A1 Trust, A3 Altruism}**

  - A1 Trust: .42/.42 dual-loading on both aspects (DeYoung 2007). Standalone in BFI-2 with no aspect assignment (Soto & John 2017).
  - A3 Altruism: .65/.64 dual-loading on both aspects (DeYoung 2007). DeYoung explicitly noted no good NEO markers for Compassion specifically (as distinct from Politeness). A3 marks the A domain.

**Cross-taxonomy structural finding:** Compassion and Politeness land in entirely different HEXACO domains (Ludeke 2019, L). This is strong evidence that they are separable constructs. But the NEO facets that are supposed to define Compassion — A1, A3, A6 — don't actually define it cleanly. Two of three (A1, A3) are domain markers, and only A6 is a clean Compassion marker. The "Compassion aspect" as measured by the BFAS uses different items, not the NEO facets.

Hansen (2025, M) provides an external-criterion test: BFI-2 Compassion (≈ A1+A3+A6 content) correlates with musical sensibility (r = .19), while BFI-2 Respectfulness and Trust (≈ Politeness content) are near zero. Clean Compassion-vs-Politeness split on an affective/aesthetic criterion.

**Clustering C — Genetic Evidence Only**

Evidence used: Jang 1996 (M), Jang 1998 (L), Jang 2001 (L), Pilia 2006 (XL), Hansen 2025 (M), Schwaba 2025 GWAS (XL domain-level).

**Assessment: Genetic evidence does not support clean within-domain clustering.**

The genetic patterns crosscut published aspect boundaries in every direction tested:

  - *Residual heritability* (Jang 1998, L): {A1, A2, A4, A6} have heritable specific variance (AE) vs. {A3, A5} have no heritable specific variance (CE). One CE facet per published aspect — the split is symmetrical across the aspect boundary, not aligned with it.**

  - *Age-heterogeneity in h²* (Pilia 2006, XL): {A3, A4} share a pattern of higher genetic variance in younger individuals, declining with age. But A3 is Compassion and A4 is Politeness. Their cross-aspect pairing doesn't support either aspect grouping.

  - *Sex-heterogeneity in h²* (Pilia 2006, XL): {A1, A2} both show significantly higher h² in females. But A1 is Compassion and A2 is Politeness.

  - *Etiological type* (Jang 1996, M — but nonadditive effects didn't replicate in Jang 1998, L): Additive {A1, A2, A3} vs. nonadditive {A4, A6} vs. no genetics {A5}. Cross-cuts aspect boundary. However, this distinction is probably fragile — Jang 1998's larger cross-cultural sample found no nonadditive effects for any facet.

  - *Within-Compassion genetic dissociation* (Jang 2001, L): A1 Trust has large negative genetic correlations with N facets (rG = −.53 to −.75). A6 Tender-Mindedness does not (rG inconsistent, ranging −.54 to +.26). If Compassion were a genetically coherent unit, A1 and A6 should share genetic correlation profiles. They don't.

  - *Domain-level genetic anomaly* (Schwaba 2025 GWAS, XL): Agreeableness is the most genetically anomalous of all Big Five domains — lowest h²_SNP, lowest cross-geography genetic correlation (.65 vs .86–.99 for others), lowest cross-instrument genetic correlation (.68 vs .82–.97), only domain without prefrontal cortex enrichment. This raises the question of whether the domain itself has a coherent genetic basis.

  - *Twin-correlation dissociation* (Hansen 2025, M): BFI-2 Compassion (A1) shows a D-pattern (MZ .36, DZ .14) while the A domain shows a C-pattern (MZ .46, DZ .27). This suggests Compassion-content and Politeness-content facets may have different genetic architectures — but it's from one study with BFI-2 facets, not NEO.

Bottom line:** The genetic evidence provides no basis for within-domain clustering. Patterns of heritability, genetic architecture type, age-heterogeneity, and sex-heterogeneity all crosscut the Compassion/Politeness boundary. The only consistent genetic signal is negative: A5 Modesty is genetically anomalous (no heritable specific variance, cross-cultural instability), and A3 Altruism also lacks heritable specific variance. These two isolates are the clearest genetic pattern, and they come from different aspects.

**Step 3: Convergence Assessment**

For each facet pair, I assess whether they cluster together in zero, one, two, or three evidence streams (Developmental, Structural, Genetic).

        — No clear pattern

        **2 of 3****** **Moderate confidence.** Best-supported pairing in the domain.

        A2–A5

        ✗ A2 increases, A5 flat

        ✓ Both Politeness

        ✗ Totally different profiles

        **1 of 3******

        Structural-only. Weak.

        A4–A5

        ✗ A4 increases, A5 flat

        ✓ Both Politeness

        ✗ A4 heritable, A5 not

        **1 of 3******

        Structural-only. Weak.

        A1–A6

        ~✓ Both convex (loose)

        Mixed: A1 dual-loads, A6 Compassion

        ✗ Genetically dissimilar (Jang 2001)

        **~1 of 3******

        Developmental similarity is loose. Structural and genetic diverge. Weak.

        A1–A3

        ✗ A1 inverted-U, A3 flat

        ~✓ Both dual-loaders

        ✗ Different h² profiles

        **~1 of 3******

        They share the property of being domain markers. But that's a structural observation, not a clustering. Weak.

        A3–A6

        Mixed: A3 dual-loads, A6 Compassion

        ✗ A3 CE residual, A6 AE

        ✗ Different aspects

        — No data

        A1–A4

        ✗ A1 inverted-U, A4 linear

        ✗ Different aspects

        **0 of 3******

        No convergence.

**Summary of convergence:**- The only pairing with multi-stream support is **{A2, A4}** — they cluster on both developmental trajectories and structural placement. They are the core of whatever "Politeness" is.
  - All other pairings are supported by at most one evidence stream.
  - The published Compassion aspect {A1, A3, A6} has essentially no multi-stream convergence. A1 and A3 are domain markers that dual-load, A6 is the sole clean Compassion marker, and genetically A1 and A6 are dissimilar. This is a structurally supported grouping that receives no developmental or genetic reinforcement.
  - The published Politeness aspect {A2, A4, A5} has structural convergence but A5 breaks from the group on both developmental and genetic evidence.

**Proposed within-domain groupings (based on convergence, not on holistic intuition):**- **{A2 Straightforwardness, A4 Compliance}** — the tight Politeness core. Two-stream convergence (developmental + structural). These are the behavioral-compliance facets that increase linearly across adulthood and load cleanly on Politeness.**- {A6 Tender-Mindedness}** — the Compassion anchor. Sole clean Compassion marker structurally, with a developmental trajectory (upward, then decline) consistent with prosocial-emotional content. But it's alone — there's no well-supported grouping to put it in.**- {A1 Trust}** — domain marker with cross-domain affinity. Dual-loads on both aspects, genetically resembles inverted-N, developmentally distinctive (inverted-U with late-life decline). Structurally and genetically, it has as much affinity with N facets as with A facets.**- {A3 Altruism}** — domain marker with weak specificity. Dual-loads, lowest raw h² in A, no heritable specific variance. Whatever A3 measures beyond the A factor is environmentally shaped.**- {A5 Modesty}** — the genuine outlier. Flat developmentally, no heritable specific variance, cross-culturally unstable heritability, reversed MZ/DZ pattern. Does not belong to any within-domain cluster on developmental or genetic grounds.**Self-check on pattern-matching:** I notice that my grouping isolates A5 Modesty, which is a finding consistent with some published critiques of the Politeness aspect. I want to flag that I may be pattern-matching to published arguments. The evidence that independently supports A5's isolation: (1) flat trajectory while A2 and A4 increase (Terracciano L, Schwaba M); (2) no heritable specific variance (Jang 1998 L); (3) reversed MZ/DZ correlations (Jang 1996 M); (4) cross-cultural heritability instability (Jang 1998 L). The evidence against isolating A5: its structural loading on Politeness is clean (DeYoung 2007). One evidence stream (structural) says it belongs; two streams (developmental, genetic) say it doesn't.

**Step 4: Misbehaving Facets**

**Facets that don't fit their within-domain grouping:**

**A5 Modesty** — the most problematic facet in the A domain. Structurally assigned to Politeness, but developmentally inert (flat, minimal individual variability) while its aspect-mates increase steadily. Genetically anomalous: no heritable specific variance, reversed twin correlations, cross-cultural instability. This facet behaves more like a culturally shaped behavioral norm than an endogenous personality trait.

**A1 Trust** — structurally homeless. Dual-loads .42/.42 on both aspects. Standalone in BFI-2. Genetically, it behaves like partially inverted Neuroticism (large negative rG with N facets). Developmentally, its late-life decline distinguishes it from the other increasing A facets. A1 may be better understood as a cognitive-evaluative facet (beliefs about others' intentions) that correlates with A and anti-N for phenotypic reasons, rather than a core A facet.

**A3 Altruism** — structurally a domain marker (.65/.64 dual-loading), not an aspect marker. No heritable specific variance — whatever makes it unique beyond the A factor is environmentally shaped. Developmentally ambiguous due to ceiling effects.

**Facets with stronger cross-domain affinity than within-domain cohesion:**

A1 Trust → Neuroticism (inverted). Genetic correlations of −.53 to −.75 with N facets (Jang 2001, L). Significant 5-HTTLPR association. At the genetic level, Trust looks more like low-Neuroticism than like Agreeableness. This is the strongest cross-domain affinity signal in the A domain.

A6 Tender-Mindedness → Emotionality boundary. In HEXACO rotation, Sentimentality (≈ A6) lands in Emotionality, not Agreeableness. This suggests A6 captures emotional-reactivity content that belongs partly to the N/Emotionality space. However, A6's developmental trajectory (increasing) is A-like, not N-like (decreasing), so the cross-domain affinity is taxonomic/structural, not developmental.

A5 Modesty → possible HH construct (cross-domain). A5's content maps to HEXACO Honesty-Humility, which shows dramatically different developmental behavior (~1.0 SD increase) than A5 itself (flat). The discrepancy may indicate that the NEO A5 items tap a narrower, more culturally variable construct than what HH captures, or that the HEXACO HH age trend is inflated by desirability. Flag but do not resolve — the evidence is insufficient. (Flagging for Conversation 6.)

**Step 5: Overall Domain Coherence**

**Agreeableness is the least internally coherent of the Big Five domains on the evidence reviewed here.**

Multiple independent signals converge on this:

  - **Genetic incoherence.** Schwaba (2025, XL) found Agreeableness has the lowest h²_SNP, lowest cross-geography genetic correlation, lowest cross-instrument genetic correlation, and is the only domain without prefrontal cortex gene expression enrichment. The domain-level genetic architecture is less consistent across contexts than any other Big Five trait. Within the domain, heritability patterns (additive vs. nonadditive, age-heterogeneity, sex-heterogeneity) crosscut the aspect boundary in every direction.**- Developmental incoherence at the facet level.** A facets showed the most variable rank-order stability patterns of any domain (Brandt 2023, L). Within-domain co-development is the weakest (mean r_change = .21; Schwaba 2022, M). Same-domain change correlations are the lowest (avg r = .28; Soto & John 2012, M). Item-level divergence within A is the most dramatic in the Seifert (2023, XL) study — items within the same domain literally change in opposite directions.**- Structural ambiguity at the aspect level.** Two of six facets (A1, A3) are domain markers that dual-load on both aspects, not aspect markers. DeYoung (2007) explicitly noted no good NEO markers for Compassion specifically. Soto & John (2017) resolved this by making Trust standalone and effectively redefining Compassion with non-NEO items. The two-aspect solution requires departing from the NEO facet set.**- Within-aspect incoherence.** Neither published aspect holds together across evidence streams. Compassion: A1 and A6 are genetically dissimilar (Jang 2001, L); A3 has no heritable specific variance. Politeness: A5 dissociates from A2 and A4 on both developmental trajectory and genetic profile.**What does hold together:** The tightest unit is {A2, A4} — behaviorally compliant facets that increase linearly across adulthood and load cleanly on Politeness. Beyond that, the domain is more a loose confederation than a structured hierarchy.

**Assessment: Agreeableness is a domain held together more by its content overlap (prosocial concern, non-antagonism) than by shared developmental dynamics, shared genetics, or clean internal structure. The Compassion/Politeness distinction is real as a structural observation (they land in different HEXACO domains), but it maps poorly onto the NEO facets that are supposed to populate it.**

**Step 6: Gaps**

**Evidence I do not have:**- **Genetic correlations between A facets.** No study in this corpus reports the full 6×6 genetic correlation matrix for A1–A6. Jang 1996 and 1998 are univariate. Jang 2001 reports A1 and A6 correlations with N facets, not with each other. Pilia 2006 has a cluster dendrogram but the full matrix is in a supplementary table not available in the PDF. This is the single biggest gap — without it, I cannot test whether aspect-mates share more genetic variance than non-aspect-mates within A.**- NEO facet-level GWAS.** Schwaba 2025 GWAS is domain-level only. Facet-level GWAS from this consortium would be transformative but does not exist yet.**- A2 Straightforwardness genetic correlations with other facets.** A2's distinctive genetic architecture (Pilia 2006) is flagged but I have no cross-facet genetic correlations to contextualize it.**- Longitudinal observer-report data for A facets.** All developmental trajectory data for A is self-report. Jackson (2009) had observer data but only for Conscientiousness. Self-report A trajectories could partly reflect increasing social desirability with age.**- A5 Modesty: why is it flat when HH content shows steep increase?** The HEXACO HH age trend (~1.0 SD) and A5's flatness are in tension. No study directly tests whether this reflects item content differences, cultural variability, or desirability confounds.**- Childhood-to-adulthood developmental continuity for A facets.** Brandes (2020) covers ages 9–13 with a non-NEO instrument. No study bridges the adolescent A-facet data (Ringwald 2024, BFI-2) with the adult NEO-PI-R data (Terracciano, Brandt).**- Within-person co-development of A facets.** Haehner (2025) reports aspect-level correlated change (Compassion ↔ Politeness r = .20), but the CI is −.47 to .86 — functionally uninformative. No well-powered test of whether A facets co-develop within aspects vs. across aspects.**- ~~Pilia (2006) supplementary tables.** Table S3 (genetic factor structure of all 30 facets) and the full genetic correlation matrix would directly address questions 1 and 2 above. The paper states these were available online. If retrievable, they would substantially improve the genetic evidence for A and every other domain.~~ **RESOLVED — see Appendix A.

**Appendix A: Pilia et al. (2006) Supplementary Tables — Genetic Correlation Clusters and Genetic Factor Structure**

**Source**Pilia, Chen, Scuteri et al. (2006), Supplementary Tables S2 and S3. Study quality: **XL** for heritability; **L** for genetic correlations. N = 6,148 in 711 extended pedigrees, Sardinian founder population, all 30 NEO-PI-R facets.

These tables were flagged as the single biggest gap in the main synthesis (Gap #1). They are now available and substantially revise Clustering C (Genetic Evidence Only) and the convergence assessment.

**Table S2: Genetic correlation clusters (rG > .50 threshold)**

The paper computed bivariate genetic correlations among all 93 phenotypes measured (30 NEO facets + cardiovascular, hematological, and anthropometric traits) and reported clusters of traits whose average within-cluster rG exceeded .50.

**A1 Trust does not appear in any A-facet cluster.** It does not reach rG > .50 with any other A facet. It is genetically isolated within the Agreeableness domain.

**Key observation:** A1 Trust's genetic isolation within A is consistent with its phenotypic dual-loading (.42/.42), its cross-domain genetic correlations with N facets (Jang 2001), and its standalone status in BFI-2. At the genetic level, Trust is not part of either within-A grouping.

**Table S3: Genetic factor structure (Procrustes-rotated)**

These are principal components extracted from the genetic correlation matrix of all 30 NEO-PI-R facets, Procrustes-rotated to the American normative phenotypic five-factor structure. This directly tests whether the genetic structure mirrors the phenotypic structure.

**What this changes in the synthesis**

**1. Clustering C is now informative — and it contradicts the published aspects.**The main synthesis concluded "Genetic evidence does not support clean within-domain clustering" and "provides no basis for within-domain clustering." This was based on univariate heritability patterns only. With the bivariate genetic correlations now available, the genetic evidence actively supports a within-domain clustering — but one that **crosscuts the published Compassion/Politeness boundary.**

**Revised Clustering C — Genetic Evidence:**

**Cluster G1: {A3 Altruism, A4 Compliance}** — rG = .564. One facet from each published aspect. Content interpretation: "prosocial behavioral compliance" — doing good things (A3) and going along with others (A4). Both also share substantial C cross-loadings in the genetic factor structure (A3 at .39, A4 near zero but developmentally they share age-h² decline patterns per Pilia 2006 main paper).

**Cluster G2: {A2 Straightforwardness, A5 Modesty, A6 Tender-Mindedness}** — avg rG = .574 (range .493–.661). Again crosses the aspect boundary: A2 and A5 are Politeness; A6 is Compassion. Content interpretation is less obvious — perhaps "social self-restraint" (not being deceptive, not being arrogant, being emotionally sensitive to others). A2 and A5 share Honesty-Humility content; A6 adds emotional sensitivity.

**Isolate G-i: A1 Trust** — does not cluster with any A facet at rG > .50. Lowest genetic A-factor loading (.55). Largest cross-domain genetic loadings of any A facet (N = −.41, E = .36). Genetically, Trust belongs to the A domain only weakly.

**2. The convergence assessment shifts.**

With Clustering C now populated, here is the updated convergence table for the facet pairings that gain or lose support:

        ✗ Different aspects

        ✓ rG = .564

        **1 of 3** (new)

        0/3

        Genetic evidence creates a new cross-aspect pairing

        A2–A5

        ✗ A2 increases, A5 flat

        ✓ Both Politeness

        ✓ Same genetic cluster

        Promoted — but developmental still disagrees

        A2–A6

        0/3

        No change, genetic now actively disagrees

**3. Revised assessment of A5 Modesty.**

The main synthesis identified A5 as "the genuine outlier" — developmentally flat, no heritable specific variance, reversed MZ/DZ correlations. The genetic clustering data partially rehabilitate A5: it clusters genetically with A2 and A6 (rG in the .493–.661 range) and has a strong genetic A-factor loading (.76). Whatever is wrong with A5 developmentally and in terms of its specific (residual) heritability, the variance it *does* carry genetically is solidly Agreeableness variance, and it shares genetic variance with other A facets.

This creates a more nuanced picture: A5 may be a facet whose **shared** (domain-level) variance is genetically normal, but whose **specific** (facet-unique) variance is almost entirely culturally shaped. Its developmental flatness may reflect the culturally shaped specific component dominating the phenotypic trajectory, while its genetic correlations with A2 and A6 reflect the shared A-factor component.

**4. A6 Tender-Mindedness has a major Openness cross-loading.**

A6 loads .73 on genetic A but .53 on genetic O. This is nearly a dual-loading at the genetic level. No other A facet comes close to this on O (next highest is A1 at .19). This converges with A6's HEXACO placement in Emotionality (which captures emotional sensitivity/aesthetic-emotional content overlapping with O) and should be flagged for Conversation 6.

**5. A1 Trust's cross-domain status is confirmed at the genetic level.**

A1 was already flagged as having strong N-affinity (rG = −.53 to −.75 with N facets per Jang 2001). The genetic factor structure confirms: A1 loads −.41 on genetic N and .36 on genetic E, with only .55 on genetic A. Among the 30 NEO facets, A1 Trust is one of the most genetically "spread" across factors. Its primary genetic home is A, but barely.

**6. The overall domain coherence assessment does not change.**If anything, it strengthens. The genetic clustering data reveal a within-domain structure, but that structure crosscuts the published aspects. Combined with the developmental and structural evidence already assembled, the picture remains: **Agreeableness is a domain whose internal structure depends on which evidence stream you consult.** The structural stream says Compassion/Politeness. The genetic stream says {A3, A4} vs. {A2, A5, A6} vs. A1-isolated. The developmental stream says {A2, A4} vs. {A1, A6 loosely} vs. A3-flat vs. A5-flat. None of these align.

**Remaining gaps after this appendix**

Gap #1 (full genetic correlation matrix for A facets) is now **partially resolved.** We have the cluster structure and factor loadings, but not the full 6×6 pairwise rG matrix. We know which pairs exceed .50 and we know the factor structure, but we don't know the exact rG for pairs that fall below .50 (e.g., A1–A2, A1–A4, etc.). The cluster analysis tells us these are below .50, but not by how much.

Gap #2 (genetic factor structure) is **fully resolved** for all 30 facets. Table S3 provides the complete Procrustes-rotated genetic factor loadings. This is usable for all five domain syntheses, not just Agreeableness.

**Step 2: Three Independent Clusterings**

**Clustering A — Developmental Trajectories Only**

Evidence used: Bleidorn et al. (2009, M developmental). This is the only study in this corpus with NEO-PI-R facet-level developmental data for A.

**Cluster D1 — Significant increasers: {A1 Trust, A2 Straightforwardness, A4 Compliance, A6 Tender-Mindedness}**

All four show significant mean-level increases across 10 years. However, the etiology of change is radically different: A1 genetic, A2 nonshared environmental, A4 shared environmental, A6 shared environmental. They cluster on *direction* but not on *mechanism*. This is a loose cluster at best.

**Non-changers: {A3 Altruism, A5 Modesty}**

Both flat. A3 likely reflects ceiling effects; A5 appears genuinely inert.

**Assessment:** The developmental evidence in this corpus is thin (one study, M quality, underpowered biometrics). The 4-vs-2 split does not align with Compassion/Politeness (which would predict {A1, A3, A6} vs {A2, A4, A5}).

**Clustering B — Structural Mapping Only**

Evidence used: Soto & John (2017, XL), Zakrisson et al. (2025, L), Haehner et al. (2025, L).

**Cluster S1 — Compassion: {A3 Altruism, A6 Tender-Mindedness}**

BFI-2 Compassion correlates .70 with BFAS Compassion, .67 with NEO Altruism, .50 with NEO Tender-Mindedness (Soto & John 2017). Haehner (2025): Compassion drives well-being correlations; Politeness is near-zero.

**Cluster S2 — Politeness: {A2 Straightforwardness, A4 Compliance}**

BFI-2 Respectfulness correlates .66 with BFAS Politeness, .52 with NEO Compliance, .48 with NEO Straightforwardness. Compassion–Politeness within-domain r = .34 (Haehner 2025) — low, confirming these are substantially distinct constructs. Politeness has lowest reliability of any BFAS aspect (α = .62).

**Structural orphan: A1 Trust**

No BFAS aspect assignment (Soto & John 2017). BFI-2 A facets intercorrelate ~.50 evenly — no 2-cluster structure. Trust items bleed into N (Zakrisson 2025). A congruence with US = .75 in Swedish data (notably low).

**Structural orphan: A5 Modesty**

Excluded from BFI-2 entirely.

**Clustering C — Genetic Evidence Only**

Evidence used: Jang et al. (2002, L), Yamagata et al. (2006, XL structural / L genetic correlations), Bleidorn et al. (2009, S heritability).

**Yamagata (2006) — Genetic factor loadings on genetic A:** **Facet****** **Canada****** **Germany****** **Japan****** **Key cross-loadings******
