        Partially converge (both peripheral to Intellect)

        Partially converge (both on genetic factor 2)

        **0–1 of 3******

**Convergence summary**

**Three-stream convergence: {O2 Aesthetics, O3 Feelings}.**

This is the only facet pair in the O domain where all three evidence streams agree. Both are developmentally stable. Both load on the Openness structural aspect. Both load on the same genetic factor (factor 1 in Jang 2002, genetic core in Yamagata 2006). This is a confident pairing.

**Two-stream convergence: {O1, O2} and {O1, O3}** (structural + genetic, developmental dissents).

O1 loads on the Openness aspect structurally and on the Openness genetic factor. But it declines while O2 and O3 are stable. The developmental dissent weakens but doesn't break the structural-genetic alignment. The declining trajectory may reflect O1's content specificity (imagination/daydreaming) rather than structural misplacement.

**One-stream convergence only for all Intellect-side pairings.**

No pairing among {O4, O5, O6} achieves more than one-stream convergence. O4 and O5 share a genetic factor (weakly) but dissociate developmentally. O5 and O6 are both stable but O5 cross-loads genetically and O6 is mostly independent. The "Intellect aspect" as a unit has much weaker convergent support than the "Openness aspect."

**Proposed within-domain groupings (evidence-driven)**

**Group 1 — Openness core: {O2 Aesthetics, O3 Feelings}** Three-stream convergence. Stable developmentally. Clean structural Openness markers. Anchor the genetic Openness factor. This is the most confident grouping in the O domain.

**Group 1 extended — {O1 Fantasy}** Two-stream convergence with O2 and O3 (structural + genetic). O1 belongs genetically and structurally with the Openness core, but its developmental trajectory (declining) dissociates it. O1 is a good but imperfect member of this group.

**Group 2 — Intellect-adjacent: {O5 Ideas}** O5 is the structural anchor of the Intellect aspect and maps cleanly to BFI-2 Intellectual Curiosity. But genetically it is semi-independent (44–55% specific variance, cross-loads across genetic factors). Its developmental stability places it with the non-declining group but doesn't specifically cluster it with the Intellect-assigned facets (since O4 declines while O5 doesn't). O5 stands somewhat alone — a coherent construct with weak domain integration.

**Peripheral / unclassifiable: {O4 Actions, O6 Values}** Both excluded from BFI-2. O4 has the most cross-culturally unstable genetic loading (Yamagata XL) and declines. O6 has near-zero heritability, the highest facet-specific genetic variance, and content (social attitudes) distinct from the rest of the domain. Neither has strong convergent support for placement in either aspect.

**Step 4: Misbehaving Facets**

**O4 Actions — genetically homeless**

O4 is the most structurally and genetically problematic O facet. The evidence is convergent across streams:

  - Excluded from BFI-2 (Soto & John 2017, XL)
  - Genetic loading drops from .65 → .43 → .37 across Canada/Germany/Japan (Yamagata 2006, XL). Fails to reach .40 on any genetic factor in Japan.
  - In the Jang 2002 German sample, O4 loads 100% on genetic factor 2 (Intellect-like), zero on factor 1 (Openness-like). This is a clean genetic placement on Intellect — but in phenotypic structure, DeYoung 2007 assigned O4 to Openness (.54 loading). So there is a **phenotypic-genetic mismatch**: O4 loads on Openness phenotypically but on Intellect genetically (in the German data).
  - Declines developmentally while its genetic factor-mates (O5, O6) are stable.

O4's content (willingness to try new activities, variety-seeking) is more behavioral than cognitive or perceptual. It may be a behavioral-engagement facet that sits at the boundary of the O domain rather than in its core.

**Flag for Conversation 6:** O4's behavioral content may overlap with E-domain facets (activity, novelty-seeking). Does it cluster genetically with E or C content across domain boundaries?

**O6 Values — genetically adrift**

O6 has the weakest genetic integration of any O facet:

  - Near-zero implied heritability from twin correlations (Jang 2002, L). MZ correlations (.35/.34) barely exceed DZ (.30/.31).
  - 51–59% facet-specific genetic variance — most of whatever genetic influence exists is not shared with the domain.
  - Excluded from BFI-2 (Soto & John 2017, XL).
  - Content (tolerance, open-mindedness, willingness to re-examine values) is social-attitudinal, not cognitive or perceptual.
  - Elevated DZ correlations suggest shared environment effects (Jang 2002, L), which is atypical for adult personality.
  - Jackson 2009 (M): Conventionality (≈ reverse O6) has a unique late-onset developmental trajectory unlike any standard C facet. This supports the view that O6/Conventionality content straddles domain boundaries (C/reverse-O).

O6 is the strongest candidate for domain removal or reclassification in the entire O domain.

**Flag for Conversation 6:** O6's social-attitudinal content may cluster with A-domain facets. Its unique developmental behavior (late-onset via Conventionality proxy) suggests it may capture a dimension not well-represented in either the O or C taxonomy.

**O5 Ideas — coherent construct, weak domain integration**

O5 is not misbehaving in the sense of being misplaced — it clearly defines the Intellect aspect. But it is genetically semi-independent of its domain:

  - 44–55% facet-specific genetic variance (Jang 2002, L)
  - Cross-loads on the Openness genetic factor in the Canadian sample (.52) rather than loading cleanly on the Intellect factor
  - Intellect aspect is poorly captured by alternative taxonomies (Ludeke 2019 from other extractions reports R² = .42 by all HEXACO domains)
  - Haehner 2025 (L): Openness-Intellect aspect correlation = .29 — these two aspects are nearly independent

O5 may be a coherent construct that happens to be housed within O because it correlates phenotypically with aesthetic/experiential openness, rather than because they share deep biological architecture. Its high genetic specificity is consistent with this interpretation.

**O3 Feelings — content at the domain boundary**

O3 sits cleanly within the Openness genetic factor and structural aspect, but its content (emotional awareness, sensitivity) overlaps N-domain affect and A-domain empathy. Yamagata 2006 (XL) shows mild E and N genetic cross-loadings for O3, consistent with the content concern. Bleidorn 2009 (M) shows it's developmentally stable — it stays with O2 on trajectory.

O3 is not misplaced by the evidence, but its content makes it a candidate for cross-domain affinity. **Flag for Conversation 6.**

**Step 5: Domain Coherence Assessment**

**Openness has the clearest within-domain structural split of any Big Five domain — but the two sides of the split are unequal in their evidential support.**

The Openness aspect {O1, O2, O3} has multi-stream convergence:

  - Structural: clean loadings on Openness
  - Genetic: anchor the genetic Openness factor (Jang 2002, L; Yamagata 2006, XL)
  - The {O2, O3} core achieves three-stream convergence (adding developmental stability)
  - Aspect correlation with Intellect = .29 (Haehner 2025, L), confirming strong separability
  - External correlates diverge sharply from Intellect (SE, political orientation; Haehner 2025, L)

The Intellect aspect {O4, O5, O6} is fragmented:

  - Only O5 is a clean structural marker
  - O4 and O6 are both excluded from BFI-2 as too peripheral
  - Genetically: O4 loads cleanly on the Intellect factor in one sample but is cross-culturally unstable; O5 cross-loads; O6 is mostly independent
  - Developmentally: O4 declines while O5 and O6 are stable
  - No pairing within {O4, O5, O6} achieves more than one-stream convergence

**The domain as a whole:** The five-factor genetic structure replicates for O, but with the lowest factor congruence coefficient of any domain (.87 in Pilia 2006 data referenced elsewhere; Yamagata 2006 shows the weakest O4 loadings). O has the worst CFA fit of any BFI-2 domain (Zakrisson 2025, L). Only metric (not scalar) measurement invariance holds cross-culturally.

**Assessment:** O is a domain with one well-supported aspect (Openness: {O2, O3} core, O1 extended) and one fragmented aspect (Intellect: O5 stands alone, O4 and O6 are peripheral). The Intellect aspect as currently constituted is not a genetically or developmentally coherent unit — it is held together primarily by structural factor analysis. The two aspects are the most separable in the Big Five (r = .29), which is both the strongest evidence that the split is real and a warning that these might be better understood as two weakly related constructs sharing a domain label than as two halves of a coherent whole.

**Comparison note:** Without the full corpus from the first synthesis, I cannot directly compare O to other domains on the same scale. Within this corpus, the Jang 2002 paper reports that the O domain shows the cleanest aspect-aligned genetic factor structure of any domain — the German Openness/Intellect genetic split was the best match to the later-published aspect model. This is notable.

**Step 6: Evidence Gaps**

**Critical gaps**- **Developmental evidence is thin.** Only one study (Bleidorn 2009, M for phenotypic, S for biometric) provides facet-level developmental data for all six O facets. This study covers ages 18–59 only, with a small and predominantly female sample. Late-life trajectories, adolescent trajectories, and replication across samples are all missing from this corpus.**- No genetic correlation matrix between O facets.** Jang 2002 reports genetic factor loadings within O, but not pairwise genetic correlations between O facets. Knowing that O2 and O5 load on different genetic factors doesn't tell us their direct genetic correlation — it could still be substantial if both factors are correlated.**- No cross-domain genetic models for O facets.** Jang 2002 and Yamagata 2006 both analyze O facets only within the O domain (6 × 6). They cannot detect whether O3 Feelings shares genetic variance with N-domain facets, or whether O4 Actions shares genetic variance with E-domain facets. Cross-domain multivariate genetic models would be needed.**- O6 Values heritability needs resolution.** The near-zero implied heritability from Jang 2002 twin correlations is notable but the sample may be underpowered for this specific facet. Elevated DZ correlations suggest shared environment — if real, this would make O6 unique among O facets and most NEO facets generally. Replication in a larger sample is needed.**- No observer-rated or peer-rated developmental data for O facets.** All evidence in this corpus is self-report.**Moderate gaps**

  - BFI-2 Creative Imagination ≠ NEO Fantasy. The .38 convergent correlation means these are substantially different constructs. Findings about Creative Imagination cannot be directly attributed to O1 Fantasy. The structural orphaning of Creative Imagination from both BFAS aspects raises the question: if Soto & John's redefined construct doesn't map onto either aspect, does NEO Fantasy actually map as cleanly as DeYoung 2007 suggested, or was the .79 loading contingent on the specific item content?**- HEXACO genetic structure for O is underresolved.** Lewis & Bates 2014 (M) found a single common genetic factor adequate for HEXACO O, but with only 2–3 items per facet, the precision is too low to detect sub-domain structure. The HEXACO result neither confirms nor refutes the two-factor genetic finding from Jang 2002.**- No facet-level GWAS for O.** Molecular genetic evidence at the facet level is limited to the COMT null finding (Mitrović 2024, S). Whether O facets have distinct molecular genetic signatures is unknown.**What I have no evidence for in this corpus**

  - Genetic correlations between O facets and non-O facets
  - O facet responses to life events
  - Whether the Openness/Intellect factor correlation (.29 in Haehner 2025) varies by age, sex, or culture
  - Item-level genetic architecture for any O facet
  - Whether O facets show differential heritability by sex (Jang 2002 regressed out sex before analysis)
  - Late-life (60+) developmental trajectories at the O facet level

**Transparency Notes**

**Pattern-matching flag**

The German genetic factor structure from Jang 2002 maps onto the DeYoung (2007) aspect model with notable precision — {O1, O2, O3} on factor 1, {O4, O5, O6} on factor 2. I want to flag that this alignment was noted in the extraction itself ("This genetic split was found 5 years before DeYoung published the aspect model"), and I may be giving it extra weight because of the temporal independence. However, the Canadian data from the same study are messier (O5 cross-loads on factor 1), and both O5 and O6 have very high facet-specific variance. The clean German result may be the exception rather than the rule.

**Where groupings diverge from published models**

My proposed groupings place the Openness aspect {O2, O3 core; O1 extended} on strong multi-stream footing but describe the Intellect aspect as fragmented. The published aspect model treats {O4, O5, O6} (with O4 assignment debated) as a coherent Intellect unit. The evidence in this corpus does not support that coherence — O4 is cross-culturally unstable, O5 is semi-independent, and O6 is genetically adrift. The Intellect aspect may be a construct that is real in phenotypic factor space but not backed by a unitary genetic architecture.

**The Openness domain's structural problem**

Multiple lines of evidence converge on O being the most structurally problematic Big Five domain: worst CFA fit (Zakrisson 2025), lowest factor congruence in genetic structure (Yamagata 2006), lowest within-domain aspect correlation (.29, Haehner 2025), only metric (not scalar) cross-cultural invariance. The domain holds together as a single genetic factor (Yamagata 2006, XL), but the two-aspect substructure is asymmetric — one well-supported half (Openness) and one fragmented half (Intellect). Whether "Openness to Experience" is best understood as one domain with internal differentiation or as two loosely correlated constructs is a question this corpus raises but cannot settle.

*Conscientiousness*********

**Conscientiousness Domain Profile (C1–C6)**

**NEO-PI-R Facets**

  - C1 Competence
  - C2 Order
  - C3 Dutifulness
  - C4 Achievement Striving
  - C5 Self-Discipline
  - C6 Deliberation

**Step 1: Facet Profile Table**

**C1 Competence**

**Heritability estimates:**

  - Jang (1996): h² = .34 (additive, AE model). MZ r = .41, DZ r = .14.
  - Jang (1998): Raw h² = .41 (combined Canada + Germany, AE). Residual h² = .11 — the *lowest* of any AE-model C facet. h²/r_tt = .25, meaning only 25% of reliable specific variance is genetic. Retest reliability of residual = .44 (lowest in C domain).
  - Luciano (2006): Broad h² = .35 (A = .00, D = .35, CIs on A and D individually include zero). MZ r = .40, DZ r = .10.
  - Pilia (2006): Narrow h² = .186; broad h² not separately reported but C domain narrows from .316 to .107 in older group. C1 follows the typical C-domain pattern: genetic variance concentrated in younger individuals.

**Heritability summary:** Raw heritability is consistently moderate (~.34–.41) across three independent samples. *However*, residual heritability (Jang 1998) is very low (.11) — C1's heritability is almost entirely attributable to the common C factor, with very little genetically unique specific variance. **Moderate signal** for raw heritability; **strong signal** for low genetic specificity.

**Developmental trajectory:**

  - Terracciano (2005): Dramatic peak-and-decline. Rises through midlife, declines in late life (convex trajectory). One of the larger effects in the C domain. [L quality]
  - Schwaba (2022): Increases across ages 30–70 (significant at p < .01). [M quality]
  - Brandt (2023): Could not be tested for mean-level age moderation (negative latent variance). Rank-order stability: increasing with age (opposite of cumulative continuity). [L quality]
  - Soto & John (2012): CPI Industriousness composite (which partially includes C1 content) increases. [M quality]
  - Seifert (2023): No facet-level data, but work-contextualized C items (which overlap with C1 self-efficacy content) declined in old age. [XL quality for domain-level]
  - Brandes (2020): Achievement Orientation (≈ C4, partially C1) increased in girls ages 9–13, flat in boys. [L quality; childhood only, non-NEO instrument]

**Trajectory summary:** C1 shows a peak-and-decline pattern — increases through midlife, declines later. The trajectory resembles self-esteem curves more than monotonic maturation. **Strong signal** for mid-life peak (Terracciano + Schwaba converge); **moderate signal** for late-life decline (primarily Terracciano, which is heavily weighted toward 60+).

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loads .75 on Industriousness, .30 on Orderliness. Clean primary loading on Industriousness.
  - Soto & John (2017): BFI-2 Productiveness facet captures some C1 content.
  - Luciano (2006): C1 correlates about equally with its Industriousness aspect-mates (C4: .58, C5: .59) and its Orderliness non-mates (C2: .43, C3: .53). Phenotypically more "general C" than specifically Industriousness.
  - Røysamb (2018): C1 Competence's life satisfaction correlation (r = .30) exceeds the C domain's (r = .28) and matches E6 Positive Emotions. C1 loses unique predictive power when N and E facets are controlled — its wellbeing association is captured by N (reversed) and E.

**Genetic correlations with other domains:**

  - Luciano (2006): Multivariate model shows C1 shares a small but real genetic overlap with IQ (general A factor loading = .15 on C1; ~2% of C1 variance from shared genetic factor). C1 also shares a nonadditive genetic (D) factor with C3 Dutifulness (.55 loading on C1, .40 on C3) that is independent of cognitive ability.
  - Nikolašević (2021): rG = +0.16 with fluid intelligence (APM). Only C facet with a notable positive genetic correlation with g. Does not survive FDR correction alone, but replicates Luciano's finding in a different sample and culture (Serbian vs. Australian). **Moderate signal** (replicated across two independent samples).

**Cross-taxonomy mapping:**

  - Ludeke (2019): At the aspect level, Industriousness predicts HEXACO C with β = .48. HEXACO C-Diligence correlates with Industriousness, Orderliness, Intellect, Assertiveness, and low Withdrawal — suggesting Diligence contains broader motivational/agentic content.
  - Ashton & Lee (2016): HEXACO Diligence (≈ C4, partially C1) shows continued rise after age 25 (~0.2 SD).

**Dissociation flags:**- **C1 shares genetic variance with IQ unlike its Industriousness aspect-mates.** C4 and C5 show zero correlation with cognitive measures; C1 correlates .15–.27 (Luciano 2006; Nikolašević 2021 replicates). This is the most consistent cross-domain signal for C1. **Moderate signal, strengthened by replication.**
  - **C1's residual heritability is barely above zero.** At .11, it is the lowest of any AE-model C facet (Jang 1998). What makes C1 *distinct from C* is overwhelmingly environmental in origin. Its Industriousness aspect-mate C5 has residual h² = .28 — nearly 3× higher. **Moderate signal.**
  - **C1's developmental trajectory (peak-and-decline) differs from C4 (flat) and C5 (late-life decline).** Within Industriousness, three different curve shapes. **Moderate signal.**
  - **C1's wellbeing profile looks more like N/E than C** (Røysamb 2018). Suggestive but based on phenotypic associations only.

**C2 Order**

**Heritability estimates:**- Jang (1996): No genetic variance (CE model; c² = .24, e² = .76). MZ r = .32, DZ r = .26. **Does not replicate.**
  - Jang (1998): Raw h² = .51 (combined; .58 in Canada, .44 in Germany). AE model in both samples. This reverses the 1996 finding completely.
  - Luciano (2006): Broad h² = .18 (lowest of any C facet). MZ r = .21, DZ r = .15.
  - Pilia (2006): Narrow h² = .277 in young, drops to .093 in old. Follows the typical C-domain age pattern.
  - Jang (1998) residual: h² = .26. AE model. h²/r_tt = .40 — moderate proportion of reliable specific variance is genetic. Retest reliability = .65.

**Heritability summary:** The Jang 1996 "no genetic variance" finding is **definitively not replicated** — Jang 1998, Luciano 2006, and Pilia 2006 all show genetic influence. However, estimates vary widely: .18 (Luciano) to .58 (Jang 1998 Canada). **Unstable across studies.** The residual heritability (.26) is moderate — C2 has meaningful genetic specificity beyond the C factor.

**Developmental trajectory:**

  - Terracciano (2005): Weak decline across adulthood. Small effect. [L quality]
  - Schwaba (2022): Flat (below healthy ideal at both 30 and 70; fails to "mature"). [M quality]
  - Brandt (2023): No significant mean-level change. Rank-order stability: lowest between ages 50–60, then increasing — U-shaped, opposite of cumulative continuity. [L quality]
  - Soto et al. (2011): ~7 T-score point increase from adolescence to 65, but only ~1 T-score point in adulthood (25→65). The adult portion is essentially flat. [M developmental, L structural]
  - Jackson (2009): Roberts' Orderliness (≈ C2) shows d₁₃ = 0.07 (self-report) — essentially zero cumulative age difference. Replicated in Study 2 and across ethnic subgroups. [M quality]
  - Soto & John (2012): CPI Orderliness non-significant in both cross-sectional and longitudinal samples. [M quality]
  - Ashton & Lee (2016): HEXACO Organization (≈ C2) shows continued slow rise (~0.2 SD from 25 to 60s). HEXACO Perfectionism (also C2-adjacent) flatlines after teenage increase. [M developmental, L structural]
  - Haehner (2025): Orderliness aspect shows stable intercepts paired with within-person increases. Less dynamic than Industriousness. [L quality]
  - Seifert (2023): "Like order" and "pay attention to details" items are among the more stable C items in old age. [XL quality]

**Trajectory summary:** C2 Order is **developmentally flat in adulthood**. This is one of the most replicated findings in the C domain — confirmed across Terracciano, Schwaba, Brandt, Jackson (2 studies), Soto & John (2012), and Soto et al. (2011). The adult flatness contrasts sharply with the increases seen in C3 Dutifulness and C6 Deliberation (its Orderliness aspect-mates). **Strong signal.**

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loads .81 on Orderliness, .17 on Industriousness. Clean Orderliness marker.
  - Luciano (2006): C2 correlates weakly with IQ (.09, .02, .06 across three cognitive measures — nonsignificant).
  - Nikolašević (2021): rG = −0.28 with fluid intelligence — the *most negative* genetic correlation with g of any C facet. Survives FDR correction.

**Cross-taxonomy mapping:**

  - Ludeke (2019): Orderliness maps almost one-to-one to HEXACO Organization.

**Dissociation flags:**- **C2 is developmentally flat while its Orderliness aspect-mates (C3, C6) increase.** This is the most consistent within-Orderliness dissociation. **Strong signal** (replicated across 5+ studies).
  - C2 has a strongly negative genetic correlation with intelligence (rG = −0.28) while C3 Dutifulness (also Orderliness) is near zero, and C6 Deliberation is mildly negative (−0.10). C2's genetic relationship to g is more extreme than its aspect-mates. **Weak signal** (single study, small twin sample).

**C3 Dutifulness**

**Heritability estimates:**

  - Jang (1996): h² = .44 (additive, AE model). The highest of any C facet in this sample. MZ r = .44, DZ r = .12.
  - Jang (1998): Raw h² = .40 (combined). Residual h² = .28 (AE model). h²/r_tt = .65 — the *highest* proportion of reliable specific variance attributable to genetics in the entire 30-facet dataset. Retest reliability of residual = .43.
  - Luciano (2006): Broad h² = .32 (A = .00, D = .32). Multivariate model: shares a D (dominance) factor with C1 (.40 loading on C3, .55 on C1) independent of IQ. Also has a specific A factor (.46) explaining 21% of variance — unique genetic determinants not shared with C1 or IQ.
  - Pilia (2006): Follows typical C age pattern (h² higher in young, lower in old).

**Heritability summary:** Consistently moderate (~.32–.44) across studies. C3 is distinctive for having the *highest genetic specificity* of any C facet — its specific variance (what makes it different from the C factor) is more heritable than any other C facet's specific variance. **Strong signal.**

**Developmental trajectory:**

  - Terracciano (2005): Increases modestly, then declines in late life. [L quality]
  - Schwaba (2022): Increases steeply (~+0.50 SD from 30 to 70) — one of the steepest positive slopes in the entire study. [M quality]
  - Brandt (2023): No significant mean-level change detected. [L quality]
  - Ashton & Lee (2016): HEXACO Diligence (partially C3-adjacent) rises slowly. [M developmental]
  - Ringwald (2024): BFI-2 Responsibility (≈ C3) increases significantly, ages 14–23. [L quality]

**Trajectory summary:** Mixed. Schwaba finds a steep increase; Terracciano finds modest increase then decline; Brandt finds nothing. The discrepancy between Schwaba (steep positive) and Brandt (null) is notable — both use NEO-PI-R, but different samples and age ranges. Ringwald confirms increases in adolescence/young adulthood. **Moderate signal** for increase, with inconsistency in timing and magnitude.

**Structural placement:**- DeYoung (2007): Loads .63 on Industriousness, .50 on Orderliness. **Dual-loading** — this is the C facet most clearly straddling the aspect boundary.
  - Soto & John (2017): Reconceived as standalone "Responsibility" facet in BFI-2, with no BFAS aspect alignment. This decision explicitly acknowledges C3's between-aspect position.
  - Luciano (2006): C3 has modest but consistent IQ correlations (.11–.15) — the only C facet besides C1 with significant associations with all three cognitive measures.

**Cross-taxonomy mapping:**

  - Ludeke (2019): At aspect level, HEXACO C-Diligence contains broader motivational content beyond pure C.

**Dissociation flags:**- **C3 straddles the Industriousness/Orderliness boundary.** DeYoung (2007) loadings: .63/.50. Soto & John (2017) spun it off as standalone "Responsibility." Luciano (2006) genetic model shows C3 shares D variance with C1 (Industriousness) but also has substantial unique genetic sources. **Strong signal** from converging structural and genetic evidence.
  - **C3 has the highest genetic specificity of any C facet** (Jang 1998: h²/r_tt = .65). This is more than double C1's (.25) and substantially above C2's (.40). C3 is genetically the *most distinct* C facet. **Moderate signal** (single study, but large cross-cultural sample).

**C4 Achievement Striving**

**Heritability estimates:**

  - Jang (1996): h² = .42 (additive, AE model). MZ r = .42, DZ r = .08.
  - Jang (1998): Raw h² = .46 (highest in C domain for raw scores). Residual: CE model (c² = .26, e² = .74) — **no heritable specific variance**. C4's heritability is entirely attributable to the common C factor.
  - Luciano (2006): Broad h² = .36 (A = .01, D = .35). MZ r = .38, DZ r = .06.
  - Pilia (2006): Follows typical C age pattern.

**Heritability summary:** Raw heritability is consistently moderate (~.36–.46). But the residual finding from Jang 1998 is important: C4's specific variance — what makes Achievement Striving distinct from broad C — has no genetic basis. It is entirely environmental. **Strong signal** for the raw/residual discrepancy (large cross-cultural sample).

**Developmental trajectory:**

  - Terracciano (2005): Barely changes. Nearly flat trajectory across adulthood. [L quality]
  - Schwaba (2022): Flat (below healthy ideal but not increasing). [M quality]
  - Brandt (2023): No significant mean-level change. Rank-order stability: consistently higher than the C domain across all ages. [L quality]
  - Jackson (2009): Roberts' Industriousness (≈ C4/C5) increases young→middle (d₁₂ = 0.42) then plateaus. Curvilinear: rises then levels. [M quality]
  - Soto & John (2012): CPI Industriousness composite increases. [M quality]
  - Seifert (2023): "Am exacting in my work" (LISS, C4-like content) showed pronounced decline in old age. [XL quality]

**Trajectory summary:** C4 is **developmentally flat or shows only modest early-adult increases**. Among the Industriousness facets, C4 is the most stable. Jackson (2009) finds Industriousness increasing early then plateauing; Terracciano, Schwaba, and Brandt find C4 specifically is flat. Seifert's item-level data suggests work-contextualized items (C4-adjacent) decline in old age. **Moderate signal** for general flatness with possible early-adult increase and late-life decline of work-contextualized content.

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loads .75 on Industriousness, .24 on Orderliness. Clean Industriousness marker.
  - Luciano (2006): C4–C5 have the highest inter-facet correlation in the C domain (.67). C4 is uncorrelated with IQ/achievement.

**Dissociation flags:**- **C4 has no heritable specific variance** (Jang 1998, CE residual). Its Industriousness aspect-mate C5 has residual h² = .28. C4's specificity is purely environmental while C5's is substantially genetic. **Moderate signal.**
  - **C4 is developmentally flat while C5 shows late-life decline and C1 shows peak-and-decline.** Three Industriousness facets, three different trajectory shapes. **Moderate signal.**

**C5 Self-Discipline**

**Heritability estimates:**- Jang (1996): No genetic variance (CE model; c² = .34, e² = .66). MZ r = .30, DZ r = .37 (DZ > MZ — one of only two facets with this pattern). **Does not replicate.**
  - Jang (1998): Raw h² variable. Canada: ACE model (c² present); Germany: AE model. Combined shows genetic influence. Residual h² = .28 (AE model). h²/r_tt = .54.
  - Luciano (2006): Broad h² = .49 (D = .49; the *highest* and only statistically significant D estimate of any C facet). MZ r = .53, DZ r = .03. **Maximally divergent from Jang 1996** — from the least heritable to the most heritable C facet.
  - Pilia (2006): Follows typical C age pattern (h² higher in young).

**Heritability summary:** **Highly unstable across studies.** Jang 1996 found zero heritability; Luciano 2006 found the highest heritability of any C facet (D = .49). Jang 1998 found intermediate values with cross-cultural variability. The 1996 CE finding is now clearly sample-dependent — Jang 1998 and Luciano both confirm genetic influence. But the magnitude is unreliable. The age difference (Jang M ≈ 31; Luciano M ≈ 20) may contribute, as Briley & Tucker-Drob (2014) show heritability is somewhat higher at younger ages. **Weak/conflicted signal** for any specific heritability estimate; **strong signal** that C5 is heritable (contra Jang 1996).

**Developmental trajectory:**

  - Terracciano (2005): Steep late-life decline — one of the larger negative effects in the C domain. [L quality]
  - Schwaba (2022): At ceiling (above healthy ideal by age 30); flat because already high. [M quality]
  - Brandt (2023): Decreased over time. Rank-order stability: peaked around 45–49, then declined most strongly after age 64. [L quality]
  - Soto et al. (2011): Self-Discipline increases ~11 T-score points from adolescence to 65 (vs. ~7 for Order). Adult portion (25→65): ~6 T-score points increase. [M developmental, L structural]
  - Soto & John (2012): CPI Self-Discipline increases longitudinally (G₁₀ = 0.128*). **Note conflict:** This is opposite Terracciano's late-life decline. May reflect instrument differences (CPI vs. NEO), age ranges, or sample composition. [M quality]
  - Jackson (2009): Roberts' Industriousness (≈ C4/C5 blend) increases young→middle then plateaus. [M quality]
  - Seifert (2023): Work-contextualized C items decline in old age. [XL quality]

**Trajectory summary:** C5 shows **increases through young/middle adulthood, followed by late-life decline**. The late-life decline is well-supported (Terracciano, Brandt, Seifert's work items). The Soto & John (2012) positive longitudinal finding is an outlier — likely instrument-specific (CPI vs. NEO). The developmental pattern parallels an inverted-U: up through midlife, down after ~65. **Strong signal** for the overall pattern, though the Soto & John (2012) discrepancy warrants noting.

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loads .86 on Industriousness, .18 on Orderliness. Clean Industriousness marker — actually the *strongest* Industriousness loading.
  - Luciano (2006): C4–C5 have the highest inter-facet correlation (.67), supporting co-assignment.
  - Soto & John (2012): Change correlations: Self-Discipline (C) ↔ Rumination (N) = −.48 — the strongest cross-domain change correlation in the entire dataset.

**Cross-domain affinities:**

  - N5 Impulsiveness convergence: C5's late-life decline (toward lower self-control) converges with N5's lifelong decline (toward lower impulsiveness). Both involve impulse regulation. This is noted in multiple extractions. Terracciano (2005) explicitly links C6 Deliberation's increase with N5's decline; C5 shares the impulse-control content but moves in the opposite direction (declining self-discipline vs. declining impulsivity — note that these are not contradictory: lower self-discipline and lower impulsivity can coexist if they tap different aspects of regulation).
  - Löckenhoff (2009): Health covariates affected N5 and C6 — better physical health predicted decreases in N5 and increases in C6. C5 showed a weak decline with retirement that did not survive supplemental analysis.

**Dissociation flags:**- **C5 heritability is maximally unstable** — from h² = 0 (Jang 1996) to h² = .49 (Luciano 2006). No other C facet shows this magnitude of cross-study reversal. **Strong signal** for instability.
  - **C5 and C6 share etiological profiles across the aspect boundary** in Jang 1996 (both CE models) and share impulse-control content. The Jang 1996 etiological clustering does not replicate (both are heritable in later studies), but the content overlap remains. **Weak signal** (etiological basis did not replicate).
  - **C5 declines in late life while C4 (its closest Industriousness-mate, r = .67) is flat.** Within-Industriousness dissociation in developmental trajectory. **Moderate signal.**

**C6 Deliberation**

**Heritability estimates:**- Jang (1996): No genetic variance (CE model; c² = .23, e² = .77). MZ r = .22, DZ r = .18. **Does not replicate.**
  - Jang (1998): Raw: AE model combined. Residual: CE model (c² = .18, e² = .82) — **no heritable specific variance**. C6's heritability is entirely attributable to the common C factor. Retest reliability of residual = .71 (highest in C domain) — low heritability is not an artifact of unreliability.
  - Luciano (2006): Broad h² = .34. MZ r = .33, DZ r = .06.
  - Pilia (2006): **C6 is the exception** in the C domain — its h² does NOT decline with age (ratio 0.75, vs. near-halving for C1–C5). Largest non-additive genetic component of any C facet (narrow .093 vs. broad .364).

**Heritability summary:** Raw heritability is modest (.22–.34 across studies that detect genetic effects). The Jang 1996 CE finding does not replicate. But the residual finding from Jang 1998 is important: like C4, C6 has no heritable specific variance. Its raw heritability comes entirely from the C factor. The Pilia 2006 finding that C6 is the only C facet without age-related heritability decline gives it a distinctive genetic architecture within the domain. **Moderate signal** for modest raw heritability; **moderate signal** for zero genetic specificity and distinctive age pattern.

**Developmental trajectory:**- Terracciano (2005): Increases +1.70 T/decade — the **steepest increase of any C facet** and one of the largest effects in the entire 30-facet dataset. Monotonic. [L quality]
  - Schwaba (2022): Increases (significant). [M quality]
  - Brandt (2023): Increased over time. Rank-order stability: *increasing* with age (opposite of cumulative continuity). [L quality]
  - Jackson (2009): Roberts' Impulse Control (≈ C6 + N5 content) shows d₁₃ = 0.88 — the **largest cumulative age difference of any C facet** in self-report, and 0.59 in observer report. Monotonic increase across the full lifespan. Replicated across two studies, two methods, and ethnic/SES subgroups. [M quality]
  - Ashton & Lee (2016): HEXACO Prudence (≈ C6) shows the steepest rise of any C facet (~0.4 SD from 25 to 60s). [M developmental]
  - Seifert (2023): General conscientiousness items (not work-contextualized) hold steadier in old age. [XL quality]
  - Haehner (2025): Within-person Orderliness increases. [L quality]
  - Ringwald (2024): BFI-2 Organization (which blends C2 and C6 content) showed minimal/no change, ages 14–23. [L quality — note the Organization facet blends Orderliness content that is developmentally flat with Deliberation content that increases, possibly canceling out]

**Trajectory summary:** C6 Deliberation shows the **strongest, most consistent increase of any C facet across the lifespan.** This is replicated in Terracciano (2005), Schwaba (2022), Brandt (2023), Jackson (2009, 2 studies), and Ashton & Lee (2016). The monotonic increase parallels the inverse of N5 Impulsiveness. **Strong signal.**

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): Loads .66 on Orderliness, .41 on Industriousness. Primarily Orderliness but with a substantial secondary on Industriousness.
  - Luciano (2006): C6 has the weakest inter-facet correlations in the C domain (.35–.46), with its lowest correlation being with C4 Achievement Striving (.35 — opposite aspect). C6 and C4 on opposite sides of the aspect boundary show the weakest correlation, consistent with aspect separation.

**Cross-domain affinities:**- **N5 Impulsiveness convergence (strong signal):** C6 increases monotonically; N5 decreases monotonically. Both change in the direction of reduced impulsivity. This convergence is explicitly noted by Terracciano (2005, p. 10), replicated by Brandt (2023), and further supported by Löckenhoff (2009) finding that health covariates affect both N5 and C6. The developmental convergence across at least three independent studies constitutes a **strong cross-domain signal**.
  - Jang (1996): C6 and N5 do not share etiological profiles in the 1996 data (C6 = CE, N5 = AE). But this etiological divergence is based on the fragile Jang 1996 CE finding for C6, which does not replicate.

**Dissociation flags:**- **C6 diverges dramatically from its Orderliness aspect-mates in development.** C6 increases +1.70 T/decade (Terracciano); C2 is flat or slightly declining; C3 increases modestly. The magnitude gap between C6 and C2 is enormous. Jackson (2009) quantifies it: Impulse Control d₁₃ = 0.88 vs. Orderliness d₁₃ = 0.07. **Strong signal** (replicated across 5+ studies, 2 methods).
  - **C6's genetic architecture diverges from its Orderliness aspect-mates.** Pilia (2006): C6 is the only C facet without age-related heritability decline. Jang (1998): C6 residual is CE (no genetic specificity) while C2 and C3 residuals are AE (heritable specificity). **Moderate signal** (two independent genetic findings).
  - **C6 has stronger developmental affinity with N5 Impulsiveness (different domain) than with C2 Order (same aspect).** This is the clearest cross-domain developmental convergence in the C domain. **Strong signal.**

**Step 2: Three Independent Clusterings**

**Clustering A — Developmental Trajectories Only**

Using only direction, timing, and shape of age-graded change. Ignoring all heritability and structural data.

**Pattern 1: The Increasers (mature across adulthood)**

  - C3 Dutifulness: increases (Schwaba steep; Ringwald in adolescence; Terracciano modest then declines late)
  - C6 Deliberation: increases monotonically and steeply — the dominant maturational signal in C

These two share a consistent upward trajectory. C6's is steeper and more monotonic; C3's is more variable across studies but directionally positive. They are both Orderliness facets, but the gap between them in magnitude is notable.

**Pattern 2: The Flat/Modest (little adult change)**

  - C2 Order: flat in adulthood. The most replicated null finding in C.
  - C4 Achievement Striving: flat or very modest change. The most stable Industriousness facet.

These two share developmental inertia. Both resist the normative maturation pattern. They sit on opposite sides of the aspect boundary.

**Pattern 3: The Peak-and-Decline**

  - C1 Competence: rises through midlife, declines in late life (convex, resembling self-esteem)
  - C5 Self-Discipline: increases through midlife, declines substantially in late life (inverted-U, with steeper late decline than C1)

These two share the inverted-U shape. Both are Industriousness facets. C5's decline is steeper and better replicated than C1's.

**Within-cluster cohesion:**

  - The Increasers (C3 + C6) cohere moderately — same direction, different magnitude
  - The Flat pair (C2 + C4) cohere well — both stably inert
  - The Peak-and-Decline pair (C1 + C5) cohere moderately — same general shape, different steepness

**Cross-cluster note:** C6 Deliberation's monotonic increase has stronger developmental affinity with the *inverse* of N5 Impulsiveness than with anything in its own domain. The C6–N5 convergence is the most distinctive developmental signal involving any C facet.

**Clustering B — Structural Mapping Only**

Using only factor loadings, cross-taxonomy placement, phenotypic intercorrelations, and network structure. Ignoring all developmental and genetic data.

**Cluster I: Industriousness core**

  - C4 Achievement Striving: .75 on Industriousness, .24 on Orderliness
  - C5 Self-Discipline: .86 on Industriousness, .18 on Orderliness (strongest Industriousness marker)

C4 and C5 have the highest inter-facet correlation in C (.67, Luciano 2006). Clean structural cluster.

**Cluster II: Orderliness core**

  - C2 Order: .81 on Orderliness, .17 on Industriousness (strongest Orderliness marker)

C2 is structurally the purest Orderliness facet. Its lowest inter-facet correlation is with C4 (.35, Luciano 2006) — the purest Industriousness facet. The C2–C4 pair maximally differentiates the two aspects.

**Boundary facets:**

  - C1 Competence: .75/.30 — primarily Industriousness but with broader "general C" phenotypic correlations. Correlates about equally with both aspect groups. Its content overlaps with self-perceived capability/intelligence, pulling it toward the O/Intellect boundary.
  - C3 Dutifulness: .63/.50 — the clearest **dual-loading** facet. Soto & John (2017) explicitly spun it off as standalone "Responsibility." Structurally straddling.
  - C6 Deliberation: .66/.41 — primarily Orderliness but with a substantial Industriousness secondary. Its content (careful decision-making, impulse control) is more about behavioral regulation than tidiness/organization.

**Structural summary:** The cleanest structural grouping is {C4, C5} (Industriousness core) versus {C2} (Orderliness core). C1, C3, and C6 are all boundary facets to varying degrees. C3 is the most clearly dual, and C6 is structurally Orderliness-primary but content-wise distinct from C2.

**Cross-taxonomy:** Ludeke (2019) finds both C aspects translate cleanly to HEXACO space, with Orderliness ≈ HEXACO Organization and Industriousness being broader (connecting to Extraversion and Intellect). This supports the Industriousness/Orderliness distinction as structurally real.

**Clustering C — Genetic Evidence Only**

Using only heritability estimates, genetic correlations, genetic architecture type, and GWAS results. Ignoring all developmental and structural data.

**Preliminary note:** The genetic evidence for C facets is more conflicted than for most other domains. The Jang 1996 "heritable half / non-heritable half" pattern completely failed to replicate in Jang 1998 and Luciano 2006. This means the most dramatic genetic clustering signal turned out to be noise.

**What survives replication:**

*Raw heritability:* All six C facets are heritable in replicated studies. No facet is consistently non-heritable. The range is moderate (.18–.51 depending on study and facet). No clean clustering emerges from raw heritability — all facets are in the same ballpark.

*Residual heritability (Jang 1998) — what is genetically specific to each facet:*

  - **Heritable specificity:** C2 Order (.26), C3 Dutifulness (.28), C5 Self-Discipline (.28) — these have genetically distinct specific variance
  - **No heritable specificity:** C4 Achievement Striving (CE), C6 Deliberation (CE) — their heritability is entirely from the common C factor
  - **Barely heritable specificity:** C1 Competence (.11) — technically AE, but near the floor

This creates a different clustering: {C2, C3, C5} have genetically unique specific variance; {C1, C4, C6} do not (or barely). This grouping **does not follow the aspect boundary** — it crosscuts it (C5 clusters with C2/C3, not with its aspect-mates C1/C4).

*Genetic correlations with intelligence:*

  - C1 Competence: positive rG with IQ (Luciano 2006, Nikolašević 2021 — replicated)
  - C2 Order: negative rG with g (Nikolašević 2021; −0.28, survives FDR)
  - Other C facets: near zero

This separates C1 (positive g-overlap) from C2 (negative g-overlap) from {C3, C4, C5, C6} (near zero).

*Age heterogeneity in heritability (Pilia 2006):*

  - C1–C5: all show h² concentrated in younger individuals (typical C pattern)
  - C6 Deliberation: h² does NOT decline with age; largest non-additive component

This separates C6 from all other C facets.

*Domain-level genetic architecture (Briley & Tucker-Drob 2012):*

  - Common pathways model rejected for C domain. Genetic effects on C facets cannot be adequately represented as flowing through a single domain latent variable. ~57% global genetic variance, ~43% specific. The misfit was concentrated on the Industriousness facet (Self-Discipline in CPI).

**Genetic clustering assessment:** The genetic evidence is **too fragmented and inconsistent to support a clean two-cluster solution.** The most replicated findings are:

  - C1 Competence shares genetic variance with intelligence; other C facets do not. (Moderate signal — replicated)
  - C6 Deliberation has a distinctive age-related heritability pattern. (Moderate signal — single study but large N)
  - The aspect boundary is NOT reflected in genetic architecture. Residual heritability crosscuts aspects; age patterns crosscut aspects; the only clean genetic pair is C4–C5 (both Industriousness, neither has heritable specificity or both do depending on which study you weight).

**Honest assessment: Genetic evidence is insufficient to support a clean within-domain clustering.** Individual facets show distinctive profiles, but these don't resolve into two stable groups.

**Step 3: Convergence Assessment**

For each facet pair, I assess whether they cluster together across all three evidence streams.

**Facet-pair convergence matrix** **Pair****** **Dev (A)****** **Structural (B)****** **Genetic (C)****** **Convergence******

        C4–C5

        Different (C4 flat; C5 inverted-U)

        Same cluster (Industriousness core)

        Mixed (C4 no specific h²; C5 has specific h²)

        **1/3** — structural only

        C4–C1

        Different (C4 flat; C1 peak-decline)

        Same cluster loosely (both Industriousness, but C1 is boundary)

        Different (C1 has IQ overlap, C4 doesn't; both lack specific h²)

        **0.5/3** — partially structural

        C1–C5

        Same cluster (both peak-decline)

        Same cluster (Industriousness)

        Different (C1 near-zero specific h²; C5 moderate specific h²)

        **2/3** — developmental + structural

        C2–C3

        Different (C2 flat; C3 increases)

        Different subgroups (C2 is pure Orderliness; C3 is dual-loading)

        Same (both have moderate heritable specificity)

        **1/3** — genetic only

        C2–C6

        Very different (C2 flat; C6 steepest increase)

        Same cluster (both primarily Orderliness)

        Different (C6 has no specific h²; C2 does; C6 has unique age pattern)

        **1/3** — structural only

        C3–C6

        Same direction (both increase), but magnitude differs

        Both load on Orderliness (C3: .50; C6: .66)

        Different (C3 highest specific h²; C6 zero specific h²)

        **1.5/3** — developmental direction + partial structural

        C1–C3

        Different trajectory shapes

        C3 is boundary; C1 is primarily Industriousness

        Share D factor (Luciano 2006)

        **1/3** — genetic only (single study)

        C5–C6

        Opposite directions (C5 declines late life; C6 increases)

        Opposite aspects

        Both had CE models in Jang 1996 (did not replicate)

        **0/3** — no convergent clustering

**Summary of convergence**

**No facet pair shows three-stream convergence.**

**Two-stream convergence (moderate confidence):**

  - C1 and C5: developmental shape (peak-and-decline) + structural (Industriousness). Genetic stream dissents (different specific heritability profiles).

**One-stream or partial convergence (low confidence):**

  - C4 and C5: structural only (Industriousness core). Developmental and genetic streams dissent.
  - C2 and C6: structural only (Orderliness loadings). Developmental and genetic streams dissent.
  - C3 and C6: developmental direction + partial structural. Genetic stream dissents.
  - C2 and C3: genetic similarity in heritable specificity. Developmental and structural streams dissent.

**Zero convergence:**

  - C5 and C6: opposite on every dimension. These are the most dissimilar pair in the C domain despite both involving impulse-regulation content.

**Proposed within-domain groupings**

Given the weak convergence, any grouping must be stated with appropriate humility. The evidence best supports:

**Grouping 1: The standard Industriousness/Orderliness split is weakly supported.**

  - Industriousness {C1, C4, C5}: Held together primarily by factor loadings (DeYoung 2007). Developmental trajectories diverge (three different shapes). Genetic profiles diverge. Phenotypic intercorrelations are higher within this group than across groups (C4–C5 = .67 is the strongest pair), but C1 correlates broadly.
  - Orderliness {C2, C3, C6}: Held together primarily by factor loadings (DeYoung 2007), but C3 is genuinely dual-loading. Developmental trajectories diverge dramatically (flat vs. modest increase vs. steep increase). Genetic profiles diverge.

**Grouping 2: An alternative developmental clustering crosscuts the aspects.**

  - Peak-and-Decline {C1, C5}: Both Industriousness facets, but the grouping is driven by trajectory shape, not factor loadings. Both involve self-evaluative content (competence, self-discipline) that may peak mid-life.
  - Flat/Inert {C2, C4}: Both resist maturation. Opposite aspects. Linked by their developmental stubbornness.
  - Increasers {C3, C6}: Both increase across adulthood. C3 is dual-loading; C6 is Orderliness-primary. Their shared developmental trajectory does not correspond to a shared aspect.

**Grouping 3: A genetic clustering further crosscuts.**

  - Genetically specific {C2, C3, C5}: Have heritable specific variance
  - Genetically domain-dependent {C1, C4, C6}: Heritability comes from the common C factor, with little or no unique genetic basis

**My assessment:** The Industriousness/Orderliness structural split is real — it is supported by factor analysis and cross-taxonomy mapping — but it does not capture the developmental or genetic organization of the domain. The C domain is better described as having **weak internal structure** where the developmental and genetic evidence both crosscut the factor-analytic grouping. If forced to name two within-domain groups, the structural evidence gives the cleanest answer: {C4, C5} vs. {C2}. But C1, C3, and C6 are all genuinely boundary facets that do not sit cleanly in either group.

I notice this grouping partially resembles the published Industriousness/Orderliness aspect model. I want to flag that I may be anchored on this model. The independent evidence for the two-group split comes primarily from the factor loadings themselves and from Ludeke's cross-taxonomy mapping. The developmental and genetic evidence do not independently recover this grouping.

**Step 4: Misbehaving Facets**

**C3 Dutifulness — the straddler**C3 is the most structurally misplaced facet in the C domain. Its factor loadings (.63/.50) are the closest to equal of any C facet. Soto & John (2017) pulled it out as standalone "Responsibility." Its genetic profile (highest specific heritability, shares D variance with C1 across the aspect boundary) suggests it is partially independent of both aspects. **C3 belongs to the C domain but does not cleanly belong to either aspect.**

**C6 Deliberation — cross-domain developmental affinity**C6's developmental trajectory (steep monotonic increase) has more in common with the inverse of N5 Impulsiveness (N domain) than with its Orderliness aspect-mates C2 (flat) and C3 (modest increase). Three independent studies (Terracciano 2005, Brandt 2023, Jackson 2009) confirm the C6–N5 developmental convergence. C6 also has a unique genetic age profile (Pilia 2006) and no heritable specificity (Jang 1998). **C6 is an Orderliness facet by factor loading, but its developmental and genetic behavior is more aligned with impulse-control content that crosses the N–C boundary.** Flag for Conversation 6.

**C1 Competence — the cognitive overlap**C1 shares genetic variance with IQ (Luciano 2006, Nikolašević 2021 — replicated cross-culturally). No other C facet does this. Its content includes self-perceived capability that overlaps with O5 Ideas / Intellect. Its wellbeing profile (Røysamb 2018) is more like N/E than like C. Its residual heritability is near-zero. **C1 may capture self-evaluative content that is as much about the O/Intellect boundary as about Conscientiousness proper.** Flag for Conversation 6.

**C2 Order and C4 Achievement Striving — the developmentally inert**

Both resist maturation. This pairs them developmentally despite being in different aspects. Neither has any other strong cross-domain signal. These are well-behaved domain members that simply don't change — which in itself is a structural signal (whatever drives C maturation acts on other facets, not these).

**Step 5: Domain Coherence Assessment**

**Overall coherence: Low to moderate.**

Conscientiousness is structurally a real domain — factor analysis consistently recovers it, and it has moderate within-domain co-development (mean r_change = .31 in Schwaba 2022, second-highest after N). The domain-level heritability is consistent (~.44–.49 across studies).

But beneath the domain level, the internal structure is fragmented:

  - **Developmental trajectories split three ways** (flat, increasing, inverted-U) that do not align with the two-aspect boundary.
  - **Genetic architecture crosscuts the aspects.** Residual heritability groups {C2, C3, C5} together and {C1, C4, C6} together — neither grouping maps to Industriousness/Orderliness.
  - **The Industriousness/Orderliness distinction is supported by factor analysis and cross-taxonomy mapping**, but not independently recovered by developmental or genetic evidence.
  - **Three facets (C1, C3, C6) are boundary facets** to varying degrees — C1 toward Intellect/O, C3 straddling both aspects, C6 toward the impulsivity dimension shared with N5.
  - **Briley & Tucker-Drob (2012) rejected the common pathways model** for C — genetic effects on C facets cannot be adequately represented as flowing through a single latent variable.

The domain holds together phenotypically, but the standard two-aspect model captures only the structural evidence stream. The developmental and genetic evidence suggest a more complex internal organization. C is best described as a **loose confederation** with a phenotypic core (general conscientiousness, captured by the domain factor) and substantial facet-level specificity that crosscuts the published aspects.

One important framing note: the domain-level coherence statistic (Schwaba's within-domain r_change = .31) is moderate and real. Facets within C co-develop more than facets across domains. The domain is not arbitrary. But the internal structure — *how* facets relate to each other within C — is not well-captured by a single split.

**Step 6: Evidence Gaps**

**Missing evidence**- **No study in this corpus reports genetic correlations between C facets directly.** Pilia (2006) computed all pairwise genetic correlations and reported them as a dendrogram and supplementary table — but the supplementary table is not in the PDF and the full matrix is not described. This is the single most informative piece of missing data. If retrieved, it would tell us whether C2/C3/C6 share genetic variance (supporting Orderliness as a genetic unit) or whether the genetic clusters crosscut the aspects.**- No facet-level GWAS data.** Schwaba et al. (2025) is domain-level only despite many cohorts having facet-level data. Facet-level or aspect-level GWAS from this consortium would be transformative.**- No study tests the aspect structure directly at the genetic level.** No study asks: "Are two genetic factors needed within C, and do they correspond to Industriousness and Orderliness?" Jang et al. (2002, referenced but not in the corpus) reportedly found two genetic factors per domain using NEO-PI-R facets, but those data are not directly available.**- C3 Dutifulness lacks a strong developmental trajectory estimate.** The findings are inconsistent: Schwaba (steep increase), Brandt (null), Terracciano (modest then decline). Better-powered longitudinal data specifically for C3 would resolve this.**- C1 Competence's cross-domain genetic correlations are tested only with IQ/g.** Its potential overlap with O/Intellect facets has not been examined at the genetic level (e.g., bivariate genetic models of C1 with O5 Ideas).**- Observer-report developmental data are sparse for C facets.** Jackson (2009) has observer data but uses non-NEO facets. Schwaba (2022) has NEO-FFI other-report but at a coarser level (domains + 14 broad facets, not 30 NEO facets). The self/observer discrepancy Jackson found (self-reports detect Industriousness changes earlier than observers do) has not been replicated with NEO-PI-R facets.**- Childhood C development is represented by only one study** (Brandes 2020) using a non-NEO instrument with mother-report only. Whether the adult-pattern dissociations (C6 increasing, C2 flat) are present before adolescence is unknown.**- No data on C6 Deliberation's genetic correlations with N5 Impulsiveness.** The developmental convergence is strong, but no study tests whether C6 and N5 share genetic variance. This would be the most important bivariate genetic test for the C domain.**Unresolvable ambiguities with current evidence**

  - **Is C6's steep developmental increase a Conscientiousness phenomenon or an impulsivity phenomenon?** The factor loading says C; the developmental trajectory says impulse control (shared with N5). Without genetic correlations between C6 and N5, this cannot be resolved.**- Is C1's IQ overlap a measurement artifact (self-perceived intelligence inflating self-reported competence) or a genuine structural signal?** The genetic evidence (Luciano, Nikolašević) says the overlap is real at the genetic level, not just self-report bias. But the effect is small (~2% shared variance).**- What is C3 Dutifulness's actual developmental trajectory?** The studies disagree. More data needed.**- Does the Industriousness/Orderliness split have genetic reality?** Factor analysis says yes. Genetic architecture says "not cleanly." The Pilia (2006) supplementary tables (see Appendix) partially address this — see below.**Appendix: Pilia et al. (2006) Supplementary Tables S2 and S3**

These tables were flagged in Step 6 as the single most important missing data for the C domain. Both are now available. They report genetic correlations and genetic factor structure from the SardiNIA extended pedigree sample (N = 6,148).

**Table S2: Genetic Correlation Clusters (rG > .50 threshold)**

Table S2 groups all 93 traits (30 personality facets + cardiovascular + hematological measures) into clusters where the average within-cluster genetic correlation exceeds .50. The personality-relevant clusters are:

**Cluster 8: {A3 Altruism, A4 Compliance}** Average rG = .564.

**Cluster 9: {C4 Achievement Striving, E4 Activity}** Average rG = .775.

**Cluster 11: {A2 Straightforwardness, A5 Modesty, A6 Tender-Mindedness}** Average rG = .574. Min = .493, Max = .661.

**Cluster 15: {O1 Fantasy, O2 Aesthetics, O3 Feelings, O5 Ideas}** Average rG = .751. Min = .684, Max = .810.

**Cluster 17: {C1 Competence, C2 Order, C3 Dutifulness, C5 Self-Discipline, C6 Deliberation}** Average rG = .623. Min = .436, Max = .778.

**Cluster 18: {E1 Warmth, E3 Assertiveness, E6 Positive Emotions, N1 Anxiety, N2 Angry Hostility, N3 Depression, N4 Self-Consciousness, N6 Vulnerability}** Average rG = .618. Min = .233, Max = .893.

**C-domain findings from Table S2**

**Five of six C facets cluster together genetically: {C1, C2, C3, C5, C6}.** Average rG = .623 — moderate-to-strong genetic coherence. The minimum within-cluster rG is .436 and the maximum is .778, indicating meaningful but uneven genetic overlap.

**C4 Achievement Striving does NOT cluster with the other C facets.** Instead, C4 clusters with E4 Activity (rG = .775). This is a *cross-domain* genetic cluster — C4 is genetically more similar to E4 Activity than to any of its C domain siblings at this clustering threshold.

**N5 Impulsiveness does not appear in any cluster.** It falls below the rG > .50 threshold with all other traits. N5 is genetically isolated — it does not cluster with its N domain-mates (Cluster 18), nor with C facets (Cluster 17), nor with E4/C4 (Cluster 9). This is consistent with N5's anomalous position flagged throughout the profile.

**C6 Deliberation clusters with the other C facets, not with N5.** Despite the strong developmental convergence between C6 and the inverse of N5, they do not share sufficient genetic variance to cluster together. The C6–N5 developmental convergence appears to be environmentally mediated, not genetically rooted. This partially resolves the ambiguity noted in Step 6 ("Is C6's steep developmental increase a C phenomenon or an impulsivity phenomenon?") — genetically, it's a C phenomenon.

**Implications for the Industriousness/Orderliness question**

The clustering does NOT separate C facets into Industriousness and Orderliness groups. Instead, five of six C facets form a single genetic cluster, and the sixth (C4) leaves the domain entirely to pair with E4. This means:

  - **The Industriousness/Orderliness distinction has no support at the rG > .50 clustering level.** The within-domain genetic correlations do not split along the aspect boundary.
  - **C4 Achievement Striving is the genetic outlier.** Its rG = .775 with E4 Activity exceeds its genetic similarity to its own domain. This converges with the main profile's finding that C4's specific variance has no heritable basis (Jang 1998 residual = CE). C4's genetic variance may come substantially from a motivational/energetic factor shared with E4, not from a Conscientiousness-specific factor.
  - **The five-facet C genetic cluster is relatively coherent** (average rG = .623), suggesting a real genetic core to Conscientiousness — but one that excludes C4.

**Table S3: Genetic Factor Structure (Procrustes-Rotated Principal Components)**

Table S3 reports genetic factor loadings from a principal components analysis of the 30 × 30 genetic correlation matrix, Procrustes-rotated to target the American normative phenotypic five-factor structure. Factor congruence coefficients are high (all > .85), indicating the genetic structure largely replicates the phenotypic structure.

**C factor loadings for all C facets** **Facet****** **C loading****** **Primary factor****** **Other notable loadings******

        C1 Competence

        **.62******

        C

        N: −.41, E: .36

        C2 Order

        **.69******

        C

        O: −.35

        C3 Dutifulness

        **.80******

        C

        A: .35

        C4 Achievement Striving

        **.78******

        C

        A: −.36

        C5 Self-Discipline

        **.86******

        N: −.35
