        **.64******

        C

        O: −.43, N: −.39

All six C facets load primarily on the genetic C factor. The genetic factor structure replicates the phenotypic structure — no C facet has its primary genetic loading on a different domain. Variable congruence coefficients (VCC) are very high for all C facets (.79–.98).

**Within-C genetic loading pattern**

The highest genetic C loadings are:

  - C5 Self-Discipline: .86
  - C3 Dutifulness: .80
  - C4 Achievement Striving: .78
  - C2 Order: .69
  - C6 Deliberation: .64
  - C1 Competence: .62

This ordering does NOT split cleanly into Industriousness vs. Orderliness. The top three are {C5, C3, C4} — two Industriousness facets and one boundary/Orderliness facet. The bottom three are {C2, C6, C1} — one Orderliness core, one Orderliness secondary, and one Industriousness facet.

The genetic loading pattern tracks **general Conscientiousness saturation** (how much each facet captures the common C factor genetically) rather than an aspect-level distinction. C5 Self-Discipline is the genetic core of C; C1 Competence and C6 Deliberation are the genetic periphery.

**Cross-domain genetic loadings of C facets**

Several C facets have notable secondary genetic loadings on other factors:

  - **C1 Competence**: N = −.41, E = .36. Genetically, C1 is partially a low-N, high-E construct. This converges with Røysamb (2018) finding that C1's wellbeing profile is captured by N and E facets, and with Luciano (2006) finding C1 shares genetic variance with IQ. C1 sits at a genetic intersection of C, low-N, and E.
  - **C6 Deliberation**: O = −.43, N = −.39. Genetically, C6 is partially a low-O, low-N construct. The −.43 on Openness is the largest cross-loading of any C facet. This is notable because the phenotypic cross-loading of C6 is typically described in terms of impulse control (N5-adjacent), but the genetic cross-loading points toward low-Openness (rigidity, closed-mindedness) as much as low-Neuroticism.
  - **C4 Achievement Striving**: A = −.36. Genetically, C4 is partially a low-A (competitive/disagreeable) construct. This is consistent with the Table S2 finding that C4 clusters genetically with E4 Activity — both may tap a competitive, energetic drive that is partially antagonistic.
  - **C3 Dutifulness**: A = .35. The only C facet with a positive A loading. C3 shares genetic variance with Agreeableness — consistent with its structural position straddling the aspect boundary and Soto & John's (2017) reconceptualization as "Responsibility."
  - **C2 Order**: O = −.35. Like C6, Order's genetic cross-loading is on low Openness.
  - **C5 Self-Discipline**: N = −.35. Genetically overlaps with low-Neuroticism, consistent with the broader impulse-control theme.

**E4 Activity's genetic placement**

Confirming the main paper's text and Table S2: E4 Activity loads .65 on the genetic C factor — *higher than its loading on the genetic E factor* (.47). E4 is genetically more a Conscientiousness facet than an Extraversion facet. Its genetic C loading (.65) exceeds that of C1 Competence (.62) and C6 Deliberation (.64). This is the single most dramatic cross-domain genetic misplacement in the entire 30-facet dataset. Flag for Conversation 6.

**N5 Impulsiveness's genetic placement**

N5 loads .45 on genetic N (the lowest of any N facet), .36 on genetic E, −.41 on genetic C, and −.31 on genetic A. N5 has the most diffuse genetic loading profile of any N facet — it is genetically as much a low-C, high-E construct as a high-N construct. The −.41 on genetic C is substantial but *negative*, while C6 Deliberation's C loading is *positive* (.64). N5 and C6 are on opposite poles of the genetic C factor, which is consistent with their opposite-direction developmental trajectories (N5 declining, C6 increasing) being expressions of the same underlying genetic dimension.

**Summary: What the Pilia supplements change**

These data partially resolve three questions from Step 6:

  - **Does the Industriousness/Orderliness split have genetic reality?** **No, not at the genetic correlation clustering level.** Five of six C facets form a single genetic cluster. The genetic factor loadings do not split into two aspect-aligned groups. The within-C genetic structure is better described as a single gradient of C-factor saturation (C5 highest, C1/C6 lowest) than as two discrete aspects.**- Is C6's steep developmental increase a C phenomenon or an impulsivity phenomenon?** **Genetically, it's a C phenomenon.** C6 clusters genetically with other C facets (rG > .50), not with N5. The developmental C6–N5 convergence appears to be environmentally mediated. However, C6's genetic cross-loadings on low-O (−.43) and low-N (−.39) suggest its genetic basis involves behavioral constraint more broadly, not just tidiness/organization.**- Is C4 Achievement Striving genetically a Conscientiousness facet?** **Partially.** C4 loads .78 on genetic C (its primary loading), but it also clusters genetically with E4 Activity (rG = .775) — more strongly than it clusters with its C domain-mates. C4's genetic variance comes from both a C-factor source and a cross-domain motivational/energetic source shared with E4.**

One question is sharpened rather than resolved: C1 Competence's genetic cross-loadings (N = −.41, E = .36) confirm it sits at a domain intersection, but the C loading (.62) is still its primary. C1 is genetically a C facet with substantial N/E coloring — consistent with its IQ overlap (Luciano 2006, Nikolašević 2021) and its wellbeing profile (Røysamb 2018).

Revision to Step 3 convergence assessment**

The Pilia supplement data strengthen the genetic evidence stream. The key revision:

**The genetic evidence now supports a single C cluster {C1, C2, C3, C5, C6} with C4 as an outlier.** This does not support the two-aspect split at the genetic level. It does support C as a coherent genetic domain (five of six facets, average rG = .623) — more coherent than the developmental evidence would suggest.

The updated picture: C is **genetically coherent as a domain** but **developmentally fragmented within**. The structural two-aspect model captures neither the genetic organization (single cluster + C4 outlier) nor the developmental organization (three trajectory types crosscutting aspects). It captures only the phenotypic factor-analytic structure — which, to be fair, is its intended target.

**Conscientiousness Domain Profile — v2 Synthesis**

**NEO-PI-R Facets**

  - C1 Competence
  - C2 Order
  - C3 Dutifulness
  - C4 Achievement Striving
  - C5 Self-Discipline
  - C6 Deliberation

Published aspect assignments (DeYoung et al., 2007):

  - Industriousness: C1, C4, C5
  - Orderliness: C2, C3, C6

**Step 1: Facet Profile Table**

**C1 Competence**

**Heritability estimates:**

  - Jang (2002): Loads on both genetic factors (German: .21 on Factor 1 "Methodical/Dutiful", .52 on Factor 2 "Ambitious/Driven"; Canadian: .37/.28 across factors).
  - Yamagata (2006): Genetic C loading = .61 / .60 / .74 (Canada / Germany / Japan). Consistent genetic N cross-loading = −.48 / −.53 / −.38.
  - Bleidorn (2009): Stability a² = 24 (lowest Industriousness facet). Change a² = 6, e² = 17 (most environmental change among Industriousness facets). Significant increase (slope = +2.91**).
  - Mitrović (2024): COMT Val+ group significantly higher (d = 0.15). One of two C facets associated with COMT.

**Developmental trajectory:**- Bleidorn (2009): Largest C facet increase (slope = +2.91**). Change more pronounced in younger individuals. Predominantly environmental change. [L quality, small N]
  - Jackson (2009): Roberts' Industriousness (≈ C1/C4/C5) increases young→middle, then plateaus. [M quality]

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): .75 Industriousness / .30 Orderliness. Clean primary.
  - Soto & John (2017): BFI-2 Productiveness (partially C1) correlates .77 with BFAS Industriousness. Positive E secondary (.22/.29).
  - Jang (2002): Primary marker of genetic Factor 2 "Ambitious/Driven" (.52). C1 and C4 form the core of this factor.
  - Yamagata (2006): Straddles C and low N genetically across all three countries.

**Dissociation flags:**- **C1 genetically bridges C and low N.** Yamagata (2006): N cross-loading −.48 to −.53 in Canada/Germany. **Strong signal** (3-country replication).
  - **C1 change is more environmental than C4/C5.** Bleidorn (2009): a² = 6 vs. C4's 13, C5's 11. **Suggestive** (small N).
  - **C1 and C4 share COMT association; C5 does not.** **Weak** (candidate-gene design).

**C2 Order**

**Heritability estimates:**

  - Jang (2002): Implied h² massively unstable: Canada ~.08, Germany ~.70.
  - Yamagata (2006): Genetic C loading .77 / .70 / .82. Clean genetic C marker; no cross-domain loadings.
  - Bleidorn (2009): Stability a² = 36. Change a² = 14 (highest genetic influence on change of any C facet). Significant increase (slope = +2.11**).

**Developmental trajectory:**- Bleidorn (2009): Increases (slope = +2.11**). Change is highly genetic. [L quality]
  - Jackson (2009): Flat (d₁₃ = 0.07). Replicated across 2 studies, 2 methods, ethnic subgroups. [M quality]
  - **Conflict:** Bleidorn finds increase; Jackson finds flatness. Different instruments, cultures, designs.

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): .81 Orderliness / .17 Industriousness. Strongest Orderliness marker.
  - Soto & John (2017): BFI-2 Organization = .74 with NEO Order. "Remarkably factor-pure."
  - Jang (2002): .50 on genetic Factor 1 "Methodical/Dutiful."
  - Yamagata (2006): Purest genetic C marker (.70–.82, no cross-loadings).

**Dissociation flags:**- **Heritability massively unstable** (Jang 2002: .08 Canada, .70 Germany). **Strong instability flag.**
  - **Developmental trajectory conflicted** (increases in Bleidorn; flat in Jackson). **Moderate conflict.**

**C3 Dutifulness**

**Heritability estimates:**

  - Jang (2002): Primary marker of genetic Factor 1 "Methodical/Dutiful" (.53). Highest loading on this factor.
  - Yamagata (2006): Genetic C loading .72 / .70 / .83. Genetic A cross-loading in Canada (.41) and Japan (.34).
  - Bleidorn (2009): CE model — **no additive genetic effects**. Stability c² = 26, e² = 20. Only C facet with CE model.

**Developmental trajectory:**- Bleidorn (2009): Significant increase (slope = +2.44**). Entirely environmental. [L quality]

**Structural placement:**- DeYoung (2007): .63 Industriousness / .50 Orderliness. **Dual-loading.**
  - Soto & John (2017): Orphaned as "Responsibility" — no BFAS aspect. A secondary (.26/.22).
  - Zakrisson (2025): BFI-2 Responsibility items dual-load on C and A in 2 of 3 Swedish samples.
  - Yamagata (2006): Only C facet bridging genetically toward A.

**Dissociation flags:**- **Straddles the Industriousness/Orderliness boundary.** Converging structural and genetic evidence. **Strong signal.**
  - **Genetically bridges C and A.** Yamagata (2006) A cross-loading in 2/3 samples; Zakrisson (2025) C/A item dual-loading. **Moderate signal.**
  - **Only C facet with CE model** in Bleidorn (2009). **Suggestive** (power concern).

**C4 Achievement Striving**

**Heritability estimates:**

  - Jang (2002): Loads .41 on genetic Factor 2 "Ambitious/Driven." 41–43% facet-specific genetic variance — most independent C facet genetically.
  - Yamagata (2006): Genetic C loading .79 / .80 / .77. Very clean, very consistent. No cross-loadings.
  - Bleidorn (2009): Stability a² = 32. Change a² = 13. Significant increase (slope = +2.20**).
  - Mitrović (2024): COMT Val+ higher (d = 0.21). One of two C facets associated with COMT.

**Developmental trajectory:**- Bleidorn (2009): Significant increase (slope = +2.20**). Moderate genetic influence on change. [L quality]
  - Jackson (2009): Industriousness increases young→middle then plateaus. Curvilinear (β = −.12). [M quality]

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): .75 Industriousness / .24 Orderliness. Clean.
  - Yamagata (2006): Purest genetic Industriousness marker.
  - Jang (2002): 41–43% facet-specific genetic variance — nearly half its heritability is independent of all other C facets.

**Dissociation flags:**- **Most genetically independent C facet** (41–43% facet-specific, Jang 2002). **Moderate signal.**
  - **C1 and C4 share COMT association; C5 does not.** Approach-motivated pair within Industriousness. **Weak.**

**C5 Self-Discipline**

**Heritability estimates:**- Jang (2002): Implied h² unstable: Canada ~.18, Germany ~.62. German genetic model: C5 loads .51 on Factor 1 "Methodical/Dutiful" (Orderliness-like) and .31 on Factor 2 "Ambitious/Driven." **55% of genetic variance on Orderliness factor vs. 20% on Industriousness factor.** In Canada, C5 leads its own factor (.57).
  - Yamagata (2006): Genetic C loading .77 / .81 / .82. Clean. Weaker N cross-loading than C1 (−.21 to −.33).
  - Bleidorn (2009): Stability a² = 41 (highest of any C facet). Change a² = 11. Significant increase (slope = +1.67**, smallest among C facets).

**Developmental trajectory:**- Bleidorn (2009): Smallest increase among C facets (slope = +1.67**). Moderate genetic influence on change. [L quality]
  - Jackson (2009): Roberts' Impulse Control (≈ C5 + C6 + reverse N5) shows d₁₃ = 0.88 — largest C effect. Monotonic increase. [M quality]

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): .86 Industriousness / .18 Orderliness. Strongest Industriousness loading.
  - Soto & John (2017): BFI-2 Productiveness correlates .73 with NEO Self-Discipline.
  - Jang (2002): **Genetically clusters with Orderliness, not Industriousness.** German data: 55% of genetic variance on Orderliness-like factor. This directly contradicts the phenotypic assignment.

**Dissociation flags:**- **C5 genetically clusters with Orderliness, not Industriousness** (Jang 2002 German). The strongest genetic challenge to the published C aspect assignments. **Moderate signal** (German only; Canadian pattern ambiguous).
  - **Heritability massively unstable** (Canada .18, Germany .62). **Strong instability flag.**
  - **Not associated with COMT** unlike aspect-mates C1 and C4. **Weak.**

**C6 Deliberation**

**Heritability estimates:**

  - Jang (2002): .36 on genetic Factor 1. 67% facet-specific genetic variance in Germany; 100% common-factor in Canada. Maximally unstable.
  - Yamagata (2006): Genetic C loading .56 / .68 / .61. Lowest and most variable of any C facet. Sporadic E cross-loading in Germany only (−.40).
  - Lewis & Bates (2014): HEXACO Prudence (≈ C6/C5 impulse control) **genetically independent** from other C facets. Zero loading on common genetic factors. All heritable variance is facet-specific dominance (d₄ = .45).
  - Bleidorn (2009): Stability a² = 34. Almost no slope variance — negligible individual differences in change. Significant increase (slope = +1.40**).

**Developmental trajectory:**- Bleidorn (2009): Modest increase (slope = +1.40**). Minimal individual differences in change. [L quality]
  - Jackson (2009): Roberts' Impulse Control (≈ C6 content) d₁₃ = 0.88. Roberts' Orderliness (also partially C6 content) d₁₃ = 0.07. C6's content maps to both trajectories. [M quality]

**Structural placement:**

  - DeYoung (2007): .66 Orderliness / .41 Industriousness.
  - Soto & John (2017): BFI-2 Responsibility correlates .47 with NEO Deliberation (secondary).
  - Jang (2002): Genetically extreme — 100% common-factor (Canada) vs. 67% specific (Germany).
  - Lewis & Bates (2014): Impulse-control content genetically independent from organization/diligence content.

**Dissociation flags:**- **Impulse-control content genetically independent from other C facets.** Lewis & Bates (2014): Prudence has zero common genetic factor loading. **Strong signal.**
  - **Most genetically unstable C facet across samples.** Jang (2002), Yamagata (2006). **Strong instability flag.**
  - **Content maps to both "flat" and "increasing" developmental trajectories.** Jackson (2009). **Moderate signal.**

**Step 2: Three Independent Clusterings**

**Clustering A — Developmental Trajectories Only**

Sources: Bleidorn (2009), Jackson (2009).

**Limitation: All six C facets increase in Bleidorn (2009).** The uniform positive direction limits developmental differentiation. I must rely on magnitude and etiology differences.

**Pattern 1: Large environmentally-driven increase**- C1 Competence: +2.91**, a² = 6
  - C3 Dutifulness: +2.44**, CE model

**Pattern 2: Moderate genetically-influenced increase**- C2 Order: +2.11**, a² = 14
  - C4 Achievement Striving: +2.20**, a² = 13
  - C5 Self-Discipline: +1.67**, a² = 11

**Pattern 3: Modest uniform increase**- C6 Deliberation: +1.40**, minimal individual differences

**Jackson (2009) adds a cross-cutting lens** (non-NEO):

  - Orderliness (≈ C2): flat
  - Industriousness (≈ C4/C5): front-loaded increase
  - Impulse Control (≈ C6 + reverse N5): monotonic, d = 0.88

**Assessment:** Developmental evidence alone cannot cleanly partition C facets into two groups. All increase. The etiology differs: {C1, C3} change environmentally; {C2, C4, C5} change with genetic influence; C6 barely varies. Jackson's non-NEO data suggest the strongest developmental distinction is between orderliness-as-neatness (flat) and impulse control (increasing), but this cuts through C6 rather than separating the two aspects.

**Clustering B — Structural Mapping Only**

Sources: DeYoung (2007), Soto & John (2017), Zakrisson (2025), Haehner (2025 BFAS).

**Industriousness core:** {C4, C5} — high, clean loadings (.75/.86). **Orderliness core:** {C2} — highest, cleanest loading (.81). **Boundary facets:** C1 (.75/.30 but E/N coloring), C3 (.63/.50, orphaned in BFI-2), C6 (.66/.41, content-heterogeneous).

**Key structural finding:** Industriousness–Orderliness within-domain r = .39 (Haehner 2025). Industriousness–Withdrawal cross-domain r = −.54. Industriousness has *more structural affinity with Withdrawal than with Orderliness.*

**Clustering C — Genetic Evidence Only**

Sources: Jang (2002), Yamagata (2006), Lewis & Bates (2014), Mitrović (2024), Bleidorn (2009).

**Two genetic factors within C (Jang 2002, German):**

  - Factor 1 "Methodical/Dutiful": {C3, C5, C2, C6} — behavioral regulation/constraint
  - Factor 2 "Ambitious/Driven": {C1, C4} — proactive achievement

**Critical: C5 is genetically on Factor 1 (Orderliness-like), not Factor 2 (Industriousness-like), despite being the strongest phenotypic Industriousness marker.**

**Impulse-control content (Lewis & Bates 2014):** Genetically independent from organization/diligence content within C.

**C1 straddles C and low-N** across 3 countries (Yamagata 2006).

**Assessment:** Genetic evidence supports a split close to "proactive achievement" {C1, C4} vs. "behavioral regulation" {C2, C3, C5, C6}, not Industriousness {C1, C4, C5} vs. Orderliness {C2, C3, C6}. The single point of disagreement with the phenotypic model is C5.

**Step 3: Convergence Assessment**

**Two-stream convergence:**- **C1–C4:** Structural (both Industriousness) + Genetic (same factor, shared COMT). This is the most robustly paired dyad in C.

**One-stream only:**

  - C4–C5: structural only; genetic stream actively dissents.
  - C2–C6: structural label only; genetic and developmental streams disagree.
  - C2–C3 and C5–C6: genetic factor pairing only (Factor 1).

**No facet pair achieves three-stream convergence.**

**Overall grouping:** If forced to partition, the genetic evidence suggests {C1, C4} vs. {C2, C3, C5, C6}, which I would describe as "proactive achievement" vs. "behavioral regulation/constraint." This does not match the published Industriousness/Orderliness split — the disagreement is C5's placement. I flag that this description is post-hoc and driven primarily by the Jang (2002) German data.

**Step 4: Misbehaving Facets**

**C5 Self-Discipline:** Strongest Industriousness marker phenotypically (.86) but genetically clusters with Orderliness (Jang 2002 German). Not COMT-associated (unlike C1/C4). C5 may phenotypically express as "getting things done" while being genetically driven by "behavioral regulation." The single most important facet misalignment in C. Flag for Conversation 6.

**C3 Dutifulness:** Consistent boundary positioning — dual factor loadings, BFI-2 orphan, genetic A cross-loading, CE model in Bleidorn. Captures interpersonal reliability that bridges C and A. Flag for Conversation 6 (C–A boundary).

**C6 Deliberation:** Genetically unstable across samples. Impulse-control content genetically independent from other C facets (Lewis & Bates 2014). NEO C6 items may blend two distinct functions (planfulness and impulse inhibition) that are developmentally and genetically dissociable. Flag for Conversation 6 (C6–N5 content overlap).

**C1 Competence:** Genetically bridges C and low N across 3 countries (Yamagata 2006). Change is more environmental than C4/C5. Self-evaluative content overlaps with emotional stability. Flag for Conversation 6 (N–C boundary).

**Step 5: Domain Coherence Assessment**

**Domain-level coherence: Moderate.**

The C domain is genetically real — Yamagata (2006) recovers a clear genetic C factor across three countries with high congruence (.93–.97). All six facets load primarily on genetic C.

**Internal structure coherence: Weak.**- **The published Industriousness/Orderliness split is not recovered genetically.** C5 is on the wrong side. The genetic split is closer to {C1, C4} vs. {C2, C3, C5, C6}.**- Industriousness–Orderliness within-domain r = .39** (Haehner 2025 BFAS). Industriousness correlates more strongly with Withdrawal (−.54) than with Orderliness. The two aspects are weakly connected and externally divergent (LS: .44 vs. .14; SE: .48 vs. .13).**- Three of six C facets are boundary facets** (C1 → N/C, C3 → C/A, C6 → unstable/content-heterogeneous). Only C2 and C4 sit cleanly in their assigned aspect.**- Common pathway model rejected for C** (Lewis & Bates 2014; converges with Jang 2002 requiring two genetic factors). Genetic effects on C facets cannot be represented through a single latent variable.**- External correlates diverge sharply between aspects.** Industriousness carries the domain's psychological weight (wellbeing, self-esteem); Orderliness contributes little.**C is a phenotypically coherent domain with a genetically complex internal structure that does not match its phenotypic aspect assignments.** The two-aspect model is a useful structural simplification but misplaces C5 genetically and cannot account for C3's orphan status or C6's content heterogeneity.

**Step 6: Evidence Gaps**

  - **No bivariate genetic correlations between C facets.** The pairwise rG between C5 and C2 vs. C5 and C4 would directly test C5's genetic home.**- No targeted aspect-level genetic model.** No study tests whether a two-genetic-factor model with Industriousness and Orderliness specified a priori fits the C genetic covariance.**- C6–N5 genetic overlap not tested.** Developmental convergence between C6 and N5 is well-documented elsewhere; this corpus cannot speak to whether they share genetic variance.**- Longitudinal NEO-PI-R data in this corpus is limited to one small study.** Bleidorn (2009) has N = 344 and finds all C facets increasing. Larger, better-powered longitudinal NEO data would clarify whether C2 truly increases (Bleidorn) or is flat (Jackson), and whether C6 has the monotonic increase seen in other corpora.**- No observer-report genetic data.** C1's genetic N cross-loading could reflect self-evaluation bias rather than genuine shared biology.**- The Jang (2002) Canadian vs. German discrepancy for C5 is unresolved.** C5 genetically clusters with Orderliness in Germany but is ambiguous in Canada. A third independent sample would determine whether C5's genetic misalignment is robust or sample-specific.**

*Cross-Domain*********

**Cross-Domain Patterns: Conversation 6 Synthesis**

**1. Cross-Domain Facet Clustering**

1A. The Impulsivity Axis: N5 ↔ C6 (and C5)

**The single strongest cross-domain signal in the entire corpus.**

N5 Impulsiveness (N domain) and C6 Deliberation (C domain) show developmental convergence across at least three independent studies:

        **Study****** **Quality****** **N5 Finding****** **C6 Finding****** **Convergence******

        Terracciano (2005)

        L

        Monotonic linear decline — NO late-life upturn. Qualitatively unique among N facets.

        Steepest increase of any C facet (+1.70 T/decade). Monotonic.

        Authors explicitly note the convergence (p. 10). Both facets move toward reduced impulsivity.

        Brandt (2023)

        L

        Decreased

        Increased

        Replicates.

        Jackson (2009)

        M

        Roberts' Impulse Control facet (conflates C6/N5 content): d₁₃ = 0.88, the largest cumulative age difference of any C-related facet. Replicated across 2 studies, 2 methods.

        (Included in Impulse Control composite)

        The combined measure shows the strongest age signal of anything in C.

        Löckenhoff (2009)

        M/S

        Health covariates affected N5

        Health covariates affected C6

        Same external moderator affects both.

**Developmental evidence quality: Strong signal.** Three independent studies, Tier 1 evidence (developmental trajectories). N5's monotonic trajectory is qualitatively different from every other N facet (which all show curvilinear patterns with late-life upturn). C6's monotonic increase is the steepest signal in the C domain. They are mirror images.

**Structural evidence:** N5 has the weakest N factor loading of any N facet (.35–.48 across three samples; Jang 2001, L). C6 loads .66 on Orderliness and .41 on Industriousness — primarily Orderliness, but with substantial secondary loading. Both are within their respective domains structurally, but at the margins. N5 was explicitly flagged by Jang (2001) authors as a candidate for relocation.

**Genetic evidence:** Pilia (2006, XL) Table S3 now provides the genetic factor loadings for N5: .45 on N, −.41 on C, .36 on O, −.31 on A. N5's genetic loading on reversed-C is nearly as large as its loading on N. Its variable congruence coefficient (.86) is the lowest in the N domain — meaning N5 is the worst-fitting N facet at the genetic level. Additionally, **N5 appears in zero genetic clusters** (Table S2: no rG > .50 with any other facet, within or across domains). N5 is genetically orphaned — it doesn't strongly belong anywhere.

No direct bivariate rG between N5 and C6 exists in this corpus (neither appears in any shared cluster in Table S2, suggesting their pairwise rG < .50). But the genetic factor loading evidence is the next best thing: N5 loads −.41 on the C genetic factor, meaning it shares substantial genetic variance with C-domain facets collectively, even if no single C facet reaches rG > .50.

Additional indirect signals:

  - N5 shares no biological pathways with any other N facet (Kim 2017, S). Pathway isolation confirmed by cluster isolation (Pilia S2, XL).
  - N5's heritability age pattern (h² higher in young; Pilia 2006, XL) matches C facets (C1–C5 all show h² concentrated in young), not other N facets (N2, N3 show higher h² in old).
  - C6 has no heritable specific variance (Jang 1998, L) — its genetics come entirely from the C domain factor. N5 has moderate residual heritability (.27; Jang 1998).
  - Pilia 2006 (XL): N5 has the largest nonadditive genetic component in the N domain (narrow .105, broad .332). C6 has the largest nonadditive component in the C domain (narrow .093, broad .364). Both are nonadditive outliers within their respective domains.

**Assessment:** N5 and C6 form a cross-domain developmental axis that is better documented than most within-domain facet pairings. The developmental convergence is Tier 1 evidence (strong signal). **The genetic evidence now actively supports the cross-domain link:** N5's genetic loading on reversed-C (−.41) is nearly as large as its loading on N (.45), and it is genetically isolated from all other facets. This upgrades the genetic stream from "mostly silent" to "supportive." The convergence across developmental (Tier 1) and genetic (Tier 3, but XL quality) evidence streams makes this a **strong signal** overall.

**What this means for domain boundaries:** N5 Impulsiveness has stronger developmental affinity with C6 Deliberation (different domain) than with N2 Angry Hostility (same published aspect). The published {N2, N5} aspect grouping is supported by factor analysis but contradicted by developmental trajectories, pathway analysis, intelligence genetic correlations, and heritability age patterns. If the developmental evidence is weighted appropriately (Tier 1), N5 is structurally misplaced.

C5 Self-Discipline is a weaker third member of this axis. Its late-life decline (toward lower self-control) converges directionally with N5's decline and inversely with C6's increase — all three involve impulse regulation. Soto & John (2012, M) found the strongest cross-domain change correlation in their dataset was Self-Discipline (C) ↔ Rumination (N) at r = −.48. But C5's trajectory shape (inverted-U) differs from N5's (monotonic) and C6's (monotonic), suggesting C5 shares content overlap but not the same developmental dynamics.

**1B. The N2–A Genetic Bridge**

**The second-strongest cross-domain signal.**

N2 Angry Hostility (N domain) and A-domain facets — especially A1 Trust — show extensive genetic entanglement.

        **Evidence****** **Study****** **Quality****** **Finding******

        Genetic correlations

        Jang (2001)

        L

        N2 rG with A1 Trust: −.75, −.47, −.51 across three independent samples. N2 rG with A3 Altruism: −.42, −.43, −.53. N2 rG with A4 Compliance: −.51, −.50, −.21.

        Genetic factor loading

        Pilia (2006) S3

        XL

        N2 genetic loading: .79 on N, −.35 on A. The A cross-loading is the largest off-domain genetic loading for any N facet. VCC = .91 (adequate but below N1's .94 or N6's .97).

        Phenotypic cross-loading

        Jang (2001)

        L

        N2 loads on A factor at −.45 to −.53.

        Cross-taxonomy

        Ludeke (2019)

        L

        The BFAS aspect containing N2 maps to reversed HEXACO Agreeableness (r = −.61, HEXACO Patience r = −.70). The BFAS aspect containing N1/N3/N6 maps to HEXACO Emotionality (r = .53). These are completely different HEXACO domains.

        HEXACO placement

        Ashton & Lee (2016)

        L

        HEXACO explicitly rotated anger/irritability content out of Emotionality and into the Agreeableness domain.

        A1 Trust genetics

        Jang (2001)

        L

        A1 shows rG = −.53 to −.75 with N facets. Significant 5-HTTLPR association. "Behaves like partially inverted Neuroticism at the genetic level."

        A1 Trust genetic factor loading

        Pilia (2006) S3

        XL

        A1 genetic loading: −.41 on N, .55 on A. The N loading is 75% as large as the A loading. Genetically, A1 is roughly half inverted-N, half A. A1 also appears in **no rG > .50 cluster** (Table S2) — genetically orphaned from both A clusters.

        A6 genetics

        Jang (2001)

        L

        A6 Tender-Mindedness does NOT show consistent genetic correlations with N (rG ranges −.54 to +.26, inconsistent). This dissociates A6 from A1 within the Compassion aspect.

**Assessment:** This is a well-powered finding (Jang 2001 uses three independent samples; Ludeke 2019 is a separate structural replication). The genetic and structural evidence converge: N2's anger/antagonism content sits at the N–A boundary at both the genetic and phenotypic levels. The HEXACO model resolved this by explicitly reassigning anger content to reversed-A.

From the A side: A1 Trust is the reciprocal. It genetically resembles inverted-N more than other A facets. A1 dual-loads .42/.42 on both A aspects (DeYoung 2007), is standalone in BFI-2, and has the most pronounced late-life decline of any A facet (inverted-U trajectory).

**What this means:** The N–A boundary is genuinely misdrawn at the facet level. The evidence supports the HEXACO solution: anger/hostility content (N2) is more parsimoniously placed with reversed-Agreeableness, and anxiety/vulnerability content (N1, N3, N6) belongs in a separate Emotionality factor. A1 Trust sits at the same boundary from the other side. The fact that two independent rotation traditions (Big Five and HEXACO) disagree about where to place this content is itself structural evidence that the N–A boundary passes through the middle of both domains.

**Developmental complement:** N2 robustly declines across adulthood (strong signal: Terracciano L, Schwaba M, Brandt L, Ringwald L). A-domain facets broadly increase. This is directionally consistent with their genetic antagonism — they move in opposite directions across the lifespan — though developmental convergence doesn't require genetic mediation.

**1C. The E4–C Genetic Cluster**

**The strongest genetic cross-domain signal from the highest-quality study.** **Evidence****** **Study****** **Quality****** **Finding******

        Genetic factor loading

        Pilia (2006) S3

        XL

        **E4 genetic loading: .65 on C, .47 on E.** E4's primary genetic loading is on Conscientiousness, not Extraversion. This is the only facet in the entire NEO-PI-R whose primary genetic loading is on a different domain than its assigned one. VCC = .96 (high — but this reflects the Procrustes rotation forcing it toward the phenotypic target).

        Genetic correlation cluster

        Pilia (2006) S2

        XL

        **E4 clusters with C4 Achievement Striving at rG = .775.** This is one of the highest cross-domain genetic correlations in the entire matrix. C4 is pulled out of the C cluster (Cluster 17: C1, C2, C3, C5, C6) and placed in its own cluster with E4 (Cluster 9).

        Genetic cluster analysis

        Pilia (2006) S2

        XL

        E4 appears in NO E-domain cluster. The E-domain genetic cluster (Cluster 18) contains E1, E3, E6 — but not E4, not E2, and not E5. E4 is genetically a C facet, not an E facet.

        Heritability age pattern

        Pilia (2006)

        XL

        E4's h² is much higher in young than old — identical to the C-domain pattern (C1–C5 all show this). Other E facets do NOT show this pattern (except E6, partially).

        Domain-level classification conflict

        Multiple

        —

        E4 classified as: Assertiveness (DeYoung 2007), Social Vitality (Roberts 2006 XL), Enthusiasm (Soto & John 2017 BFI-2). Three taxonomies, three different placements.

        Developmental trajectory

        Multiple

        Strong

        E4 shows the steepest monotonic decline of any E facet, accelerating in late life. Only E facet responding to retirement (Löckenhoff 2009, S). Trajectory matches a "physical vitality" decline curve more than a social-personality change curve.

**Assessment:** The Pilia (2006) data are now fully quantified and they are unambiguous. **E4 Activity is genetically a Conscientiousness facet.** Its primary genetic factor loading (.65 on C) exceeds its E loading (.47). Its strongest genetic correlation is with C4 Achievement Striving (rG = .775) — higher than most within-domain pairings. It does not appear in any E-domain genetic cluster. This is from the highest-quality genetic study in the corpus (XL: 6,148 individuals, extended pedigree design). The finding is not subtle or ambiguous.

**Measurement confound concern:** Multiple authors flag that E4 items ("often feel as if I'm bursting with energy," "fast pace of living") may increasingly measure physical vitality rather than personality in older adults (Terracciano 2005, Brandt 2023, Löckenhoff 2009). If so, E4's late-life decline and genetic clustering with C might reflect shared physical-health variance rather than shared personality structure. This confound is unresolved — no study in the corpus directly tests it.

**What this means:** E4 Activity does not belong with E3 Assertiveness on any evidence stream. It clusters genetically with C (XL quality), developmentally with the Social Vitality/declining group (strong signal), and is structurally contested across three major taxonomies. E4 is the single strongest case for cross-domain reassignment in the corpus, with the caveat that the health-confound interpretation would weaken the structural implication.

**1D. The A6–Emotionality Boundary**

A6 Tender-Mindedness (A domain) sits at the A–N boundary.

        **Evidence****** **Study****** **Quality****** **Finding******

        HEXACO placement

        Ashton & Lee (2016)

        L

        Sentimentality (≈ A6) lands in the HEXACO Emotionality domain, not Agreeableness.

        Structural loading

        DeYoung (2007)

        —

        Clean Compassion loading in Big Five space.

        Genetic factor loading

        Pilia (2006) S3

        XL

        A6 genetic loading: **.73 on A, .53 on O.** The O cross-loading is the second-largest off-domain genetic loading of any A facet (after A1's −.41 on N). A6 also loads .28 on N. VCC = .88 (lowest in A domain).

        Genetic cluster placement

        Pilia (2006) S2

        XL

        A6 clusters genetically with A2 and A5 (Cluster 11, average rG = .574). NOT with A1 (which is clusterless) or A3/A4 (Cluster 8).

        Genetic dissimilarity from A1

        Jang (2001)

        L

        A1 Trust has large negative rG with N facets (−.53 to −.75). A6 does NOT (rG inconsistent, −.54 to +.26). Within-Compassion genetic dissociation.

        Developmental trajectory

        Multiple

        Moderate

        A6 increases across adulthood (A-like pattern), not decreases (N-like pattern).

**Assessment:** A6 is structurally at the A/N/O boundary — HEXACO rotates it into Emotionality, and **Pilia S3 now confirms a large genetic cross-loading on O (.53), the largest O cross-loading of any non-O facet.** But its developmental trajectory (increasing) is A-like, not N-like (decreasing). And genetically, it does NOT behave like A1 Trust (which acts like inverted-N). So A6 shares emotional-reactivity content with N and aesthetic/experiential openness with O, but does not share N's developmental dynamics or A1's genetic architecture. The genetic O cross-loading is a new finding that strengthens the case for A6's boundary position. **Moderate-to-strong signal** — the genetic cross-loading from XL-quality data upgrades this from the previous assessment.

**1E. The C1–Intellect Overlap**

C1 Competence shares genetic variance with intelligence, unlike all other C facets.

        **Evidence****** **Study****** **Quality****** **Finding******

        Genetic overlap with IQ

        Luciano (2006)

        M

        Multivariate model: general A factor loading = .15 on C1. ~2% of C1 variance from shared genetic factor with IQ.

        Genetic correlation with g

        Nikolašević (2021)

        S

        rG = +0.16 with fluid intelligence. Only C facet with a notable positive genetic correlation with g.

        Replication

        Both

        M + S

        Two independent samples (Australian vs. Serbian), different IQ measures. Replicated.

        Contrast with O5 Ideas

        Nikolašević (2021)

        S

        O5 rG = .38 with g (highest of any facet). C1 rG = .16. Same direction, smaller magnitude.

**Assessment:** C1's genetic overlap with intelligence is replicated cross-culturally. It is small (~2% shared variance) but real. O5 Ideas shows the same direction of genetic overlap, at larger magnitude. C1's content (self-perceived competence, capability, self-efficacy) conceptually overlaps with Intellect content. Its wellbeing profile (Røysamb 2018) resembles N/E more than C. Its residual heritability is near-zero (.11; Jang 1998) — what makes C1 distinct from C is overwhelmingly environmental. **Moderate signal** for the IQ overlap (replicated), **weak signal** for the structural implication (the overlap is small and could reflect measurement artifact — self-perceived intelligence inflating self-reported competence).

**1F. The O3 Feelings Content Ambiguity**

O3 Feelings (O domain) has content that overlaps with N (emotional reactivity) and A (empathy/prosocial emotion).

        **Evidence****** **Study****** **Quality****** **Finding******

        Content analysis

        Multiple

        —

        O3 items tap emotional awareness, sensitivity, depth of feeling — closer to N affect or A empathy than to aesthetic/imaginative O content.

        No genetic variance (one study)

        Jang (1996)

        M

        CE model — unique in the entire NEO-PI-R. Interpreted as possibly reflecting environmental shaping (emotion socialization).

        Replication failure

        Jang (1998)

        L

        AE model — genetic variance detected. The "no genetics" finding did not replicate.

        Cross-taxonomy

        —

        —

        HEXACO Emotionality–Sentimentality is a partial correspondent (also maps to A6).

**Assessment:** The cross-domain signal for O3 is conceptual/content-based rather than empirically demonstrated. **Pilia (2006) Table S3 now shows O3's genetic loading is .81 on O** — the highest O genetic loading of any facet, tied with O5 Ideas. Genetically, O3 is a core O facet, not a boundary facet. It also shows modest cross-loadings on E (.41) and C (.19), but nothing toward N. The Jang 1996 "no genetics" finding was suggestive but did not replicate. **Weak signal, now contradicted by genetic evidence** — O3's content suggests cross-domain affinity with N, but its genetic architecture says it belongs firmly in O.

**1G. The N6–Cognitive Decline Link**

N6 Vulnerability's late-life trajectory may share variance with C-domain decline.

        **Evidence****** **Study****** **Quality****** **Finding******

        Late-life increase

        Terracciano (2005)

        L

        N6 shows the largest late-life increase of any N facet. Authors explicitly link to brain aging and Alzheimer's.

        Late-life C decline

        Terracciano (2005), Seifert (2023)

        L, XL

        C1 Competence and C5 Self-Discipline decline in late life. Work-contextualized C items (Seifert, XL) decline.

        Rank-order stability

        Brandt (2023)

        L

        N6 has consistently higher rank-order stability than the N domain — unusual.

**Assessment:** The N6 late-life increase may reflect cognitive/executive decline (C-domain-adjacent) rather than affective change (N-domain). This is speculative — Terracciano proposes the link but no study directly tests it. **Weak signal** — one author's interpretation, no independent confirmation.

**2. Recurring Structural Motifs**

**2A. Developmentally Inert Facets Exist in Every Domain**

Every domain contains at least one facet that resists the normative age-graded change pattern of its domain:

        **Domain****** **Domain trajectory****** **Inert facet(s)****** **Trajectory****** **Key evidence******

        N

        Declines broadly

        N1 Anxiety

        Flat or increases

        Brandt L, Ringwald L, Schwaba M

        E

        Mixed (Social Dominance increases, Social Vitality declines)

        E1 Warmth

        Most stable E facet (flat)

        Brandt L (no change while E2, E6 decreased)

        O

        Declines (structurally O-side facets)

        O2 Aesthetics, O5 Ideas

        Stable across lifespan

        Terracciano L, Schwaba M, replicated in childhood (Brandes L)

        C

        Increases broadly

        C2 Order, C4 Achievement Striving

        Flat across adulthood

        5+ studies for C2; Terracciano L, Schwaba M for C4

        A

        Increases broadly

        A3 Altruism, A5 Modesty

        Flat

        Schwaba M (A3 at ceiling); Terracciano L, Schwaba M (A5 minimal variability)

**Is this a pattern or a coincidence?** The inert facets don't share obvious content features. N1 is affective anxiety, E1 is interpersonal warmth, O2 is aesthetic sensitivity, C2 is orderliness, A5 is self-effacement. They don't form a cross-domain cluster.

However, a possible motif: several of the inert facets are the ones whose content is most "trait-like" in the folk-psychology sense — stable dispositions rather than context-dependent behaviors. C2 Order (you're either a tidy person or you're not), O2 Aesthetics (you either respond to beauty or you don't), A5 Modesty (culturally fixed self-presentation). The changing facets tend to involve behavioral regulation, social role adaptation, or cognitive evaluation — processes that are more responsive to life experience. This is speculative, and I flag it as such.

**Alternative interpretation:** Some inert facets may be at ceiling (A3 Altruism: Schwaba 2022 notes this explicitly) or floor, making change undetectable. For these, flatness is a measurement artifact, not a structural signal.

**2B. The Heritable-Specificity vs. Domain-Dependent Split**

Within each domain, some facets have heritable specific variance (what makes them distinct from the domain factor is genetically influenced) while others do not (their genetics come entirely from the domain factor):

        **Domain****** **Heritable specificity (AE residual)****** **No heritable specificity (CE residual)******

        N

        N1 (.25), N2 (.21), N3 (.29), N4 (.26), N5 (.27), N6 (.29)

        None — all N facets have heritable specificity

        E

        E1 (.23), E2 (.25), E3 (.29), E4 (.27), E5 (.36), E6 (.30)

        None — all E facets have heritable specificity

        O

        O1 (.25), O2 (.37), O4 (AE), O5 (.33), O6 (.35)

        O3 Feelings (CE in Jang 1996 M; AE in Jang 1998 L — did not replicate)

        C

        C2 (.26), C3 (.28), C5 (.28)

        C4 Achievement Striving (CE), C6 Deliberation (CE). C1 Competence (.11 — barely above zero).

        A

        A1 (.25–.31), A2 (.25), A4 (.26), A6 (.28)

        A3 Altruism (CE), A5 Modesty (CE)

**Pattern:** N and E have heritable specificity across all facets. C and A each have 2–3 facets with no heritable specificity. O has one borderline case (O3).

The CE-residual facets (no heritable specificity) are:

  - C4 Achievement Striving
  - C6 Deliberation
  - A3 Altruism
  - A5 Modesty
  - (C1 Competence, marginally)

These facets' heritability comes entirely from the domain factor. What makes them *distinct* from their domain is environmentally shaped.

**Does this group share anything?** Not obviously. C4 (work motivation) and C6 (careful decision-making) are both C facets but from different aspects. A3 (altruism) and A5 (modesty) are from different A aspects. The only shared feature is that they are all "well-behaved" domain members at the phenotypic level — their content is prototypically domain-relevant, and their genetic specificity is low because they *are* the domain, genetically speaking.

**Motif assessment: Moderate signal.** The split is from a single study (Jang 1998, L) for all five domains. No replication exists for the CE/AE residual distinction. But the study is well-powered (998 twin pairs, cross-cultural) and the finding is informative if treated cautiously.

**2C. Age-Heterogeneity in Heritability Crosscuts Domains**

Pilia (2006, XL) found that genetic variance is concentrated in younger individuals for most personality facets. But the exceptions form a cross-domain pattern:

        **h² higher in YOUNG****** **h² higher in OLD****** **h² stable across age******

        C1, C2, C3, C4, C5 (entire C domain except C6)

        N2 Angry Hostility

        C6 Deliberation

        E4 Activity

        N3 Depression

        Most E facets (no age heterogeneity)

        A3 Altruism, A4 Compliance

        N4 Self-Consciousness

        Most O facets (no age heterogeneity)

        N5 Impulsiveness

        N6 Vulnerability

        **Cross-domain grouping by age-heterogeneity:**

The "h² higher in young" group is dominated by C facets plus the cross-domain travelers: E4 Activity (which clusters genetically with C) and N5 Impulsiveness (which has developmental affinity with C6). A3 and A4 also appear here.

The "h² higher in old" group is exclusively N facets: N2, N3, N4, N6. This means the N domain's genetic influence becomes relatively stronger with age — environmental variance shrinks or genetic effects amplify. N5 is the exception: its age pattern matches C, not N.

**Motif assessment: Moderate signal.** Single study (Pilia, XL quality), but the pattern is clean and it independently corroborates the E4→C and N5→C cross-domain signals from other evidence streams.

**2D. Dual-Loading Facets Are Domain Markers, Not Aspect Markers**

Across domains, facets that load nearly equally on both aspects tend to be high-validity domain markers that don't contribute to internal differentiation:

        **Facet****** **Loadings (DeYoung 2007)****** **Assessment******

        A1 Trust

        .42/.42

        Domain marker. Standalone in BFI-2.

        A3 Altruism

        .65/.64

        Domain marker. DeYoung explicitly noted no good NEO markers for Compassion.

        N4 Self-Consciousness

        .67/.66

        Boundary facet. Underdetermined.

        N6 Vulnerability

        .66/.72

        Boundary facet. Slight lean but not clean.

        C3 Dutifulness

        .63/.50

        Straddler. Soto & John spun it off as standalone "Responsibility."

        O1 Fantasy

        .64/.45

        Moderate dual-loading. Strongest cross-aspect loading in O.

**Motif:** Dual-loaders are consistently the facets that carry the most domain-general variance and the least aspect-specific information. This is a structural property of the two-aspect model: when a facet correlates with everything in the domain equally, it loads on both aspects equally. This does not mean dual-loaders are uninformative — it means they mark the domain, not the subdivision.

**Implication for assessment:** If you were building an instrument, dual-loaders are your best single-facet indicators of the domain. But they are your worst indicators of internal structure. The BFI-2 handled this in various ways: making some standalone (A1 Trust, C3 Responsibility), excluding others (E5, O4, O6), and rewriting items for others.

**3. Evidence That Domain Boundaries Are Misdrawn**

**3A. The N–A Boundary**

**Strength of evidence: Strong.**

The N–A boundary is the most heavily evidenced misdrawn boundary in the Big Five. The case rests on:

  - **Genetic evidence (L quality, replicated):** N2 Angry Hostility shares as much genetic variance with A facets (especially A1 Trust) as with N facets. Three independent samples in Jang (2001). A1 Trust reciprocally behaves like inverted-N genetically.**- Cross-taxonomy evidence (L quality):** The two N aspects map to completely different HEXACO domains. The {N2, N5} side maps to reversed HEXACO-A. The {N1, N3, N6} side maps to HEXACO Emotionality. Ludeke (2019) quantifies this: the correlation between BFAS Volatility and HEXACO-E is r = .08, essentially zero — it's entirely an A-domain construct in HEXACO space.**- The HEXACO precedent:** The HEXACO model explicitly resolved this by reassigning anger/antagonism content to the A domain and creating a narrower Emotionality factor. The developmental data broadly support this: HEXACO Patience/Forgivingness (reversed anger/antagonism) shows a U-shaped developmental curve distinct from the Emotionality facets' linear declines (Ashton & Lee 2016, M).**- From the A side:** A6 Tender-Mindedness maps to HEXACO Emotionality (not A). A1 Trust is genetically entangled with N. The Compassion aspect of A is structurally and genetically porous to the N domain.**Net assessment:** The Big Five N–A boundary runs through the middle of what should probably be separated into (a) an Emotionality/Withdrawal factor (N1, N3, N6, plus A6 Sentimentality content) and (b) an Antagonism factor (reversed N2, reversed A1 Trust, A2, A4 Politeness content). This is essentially what the HEXACO model does, and the evidence reviewed here independently supports that rotation.

**3B. The E–C Boundary**

**Strength of evidence: Strong (XL quality, now quantified).**

The case rests primarily on E4 Activity, and the Pilia (2006) supplementary tables now provide decisive quantification:

  - Pilia (2006) S3, XL: E4 genetic factor loading = **.65 on C, .47 on E.** Primary genetic loading is on a different domain than its assignment.
  - Pilia (2006) S2, XL: E4 and C4 form a cross-domain genetic cluster at **rG = .775.**
  - Pilia (2006) S2, XL: E4 is absent from the E-domain genetic cluster (Cluster 18: E1, E3, E6).
  - Pilia (2006, XL): E4's age-heterogeneity in h² matches C exactly.
  - Roberts (2006, XL): E4 belongs to Social Vitality, which declines — opposite from Social Dominance (E3), which increases.
  - E4's structural placement is contested across three taxonomies.

This is now a quantified, single-facet signal from XL-quality evidence. E4 is genetically a C facet. The boundary redrawing is narrow (one facet) but the evidence is stronger than for the N–C boundary (Section 3C). The health-confound interpretation (Section 5, Conflict 1) remains unresolved but does not weaken the structural conclusion.

**Secondary signal:** C4 Achievement Striving is pulled out of its C-domain genetic cluster and placed with E4 (rG = .775). C4 also has no heritable specific variance (Jang 1998, L) — its genetics come entirely from the domain factor. This suggests C4 and E4 share their genetic variance through a cross-domain pathway (possibly related to energetic engagement/vigor) rather than through the C domain factor alone.

**3C. The N–C Boundary (Impulsivity Zone)**

**Strength of evidence: Moderate-to-strong (developmental Tier 1, now with partial genetic confirmation).**N5 Impulsiveness has stronger developmental affinity with C-domain facets than with N-domain facets. **Pilia (2006) Table S3 now provides partial genetic confirmation:** N5's genetic loading on reversed-C (−.41) is nearly as large as its loading on N (.45). N5 also appears in zero genetic clusters (Table S2) — it has no rG > .50 with any other facet in any domain. Genetically, N5 is a blend of N and reversed-C content, isolated from everything else.

The pairwise rG(N5, C6) remains unavailable — neither appears in any shared cluster, suggesting their pairwise rG < .50. But N5's genetic factor loading on C (−.41) provides indirect evidence that N5 shares genetic variance with C-domain facets collectively, even without a specific C6 link. The developmental convergence with C6 specifically may reflect shared content overlap within a broader N5–C genetic relationship.

**Upgraded assessment:** The N–C boundary for impulsivity is now supported by developmental (Tier 1, strong), structural (N5 weakest N loading, Tier 2), AND genetic (N5 genetic loading −.41 on C, XL quality) evidence. The genetic loading evidence is from a single study but it is the best genetic study in the corpus.

**3D. The O Internal Boundary (Intellect vs. Openness)**

**Strength of evidence: Strong for the split; ambiguous for the grouping.**

The Intellect/Openness aspect distinction within O has the lowest inter-aspect correlation (.51) of any domain. The two aspects map to different external criteria (intelligence vs. aesthetic sensitivity) and different HEXACO subdomains. This is the most well-separated within-domain split in the Big Five.

However, the developmental evidence crosscuts the structural split: {O2, O5} cluster together (both stable) despite being in opposite aspects. {O1, O3, O4} cluster together (all declining) despite O1 dual-loading toward Intellect. This means the structural boundary is real phenotypically but does not correspond to the developmental fault line.

This is not evidence that the O boundary is "misdrawn" relative to other domains — it's evidence that within-O structure is complex and may not reduce to a single split.

**5. Quality-Weighted Conflict Resolution**

Where studies conflict on cross-domain claims, the study quality table applies:

**Conflict 1: Is E4's cross-domain signal genetic or a health confound?**- **Genetic clustering with C:** Pilia 2006 (XL). Best-quality evidence in the corpus.
  - **Health confound hypothesis:** Multiple authors (Terracciano L, Brandt L, Löckenhoff S) suggest E4 items may measure physical vitality rather than personality. No study directly tests this.
  - **Resolution:** The genetic clustering is established fact (XL quality). The health-confound interpretation doesn't contradict the genetic finding — it offers a mechanism (shared physical-health genetic variance between E4 items and C-domain self-regulation). Both can be true simultaneously. The structural implication (E4 should be reassigned) holds regardless of mechanism.

Conflict 2: Does the N5→C developmental convergence reflect shared structure or shared content?

  - **Shared structure interpretation:** N5 and C6 change together because they tap the same latent impulsivity dimension.
  - **Shared content interpretation:** N5 and C6 items overlap in content (both mention impulse control) but the underlying traits are different.
  - **New evidence:** Pilia (2006) Table S3 shows N5 genetic loading = −.41 on C (XL quality). This means N5 shares genetic variance with C-domain facets. This supports the shared-structure interpretation over the shared-content interpretation, because genetic factor loadings reflect biological reality, not item wording.
  - **Resolution:** The genetic loading evidence tilts toward shared structure but does not fully resolve the conflict. N5's genetic loading on C (−.41) is substantial but not overwhelming — it's still smaller than its N loading (.45). The pairwise rG(N5, C6) would add further resolution. The balance of evidence now favors shared structure (developmental Tier 1 + genetic loading XL), but a pure content-overlap account cannot be ruled out.

**Conflict 3: Is the N–A boundary redrawing supported by genetics or only by taxonomy?**- **Genetic support:** Jang 2001 (L quality), three independent samples. N2–A rG values are large and replicated.
  - **Taxonomic support:** Ludeke 2019 (L), Ashton & Lee 2016 (L). HEXACO rotation places these facets differently.
  - **Counterargument:** The Big Five rotation is also valid; different rotations of the same factor space will always disagree about where to draw boundaries.
  - **Resolution:** The genetic evidence (Jang 2001, L) goes beyond rotation preference — it shows that N2 and A facets share actual genetic variance, not just phenotypic factor space. This is stronger evidence than the taxonomic argument alone. However, it doesn't prove the Big Five rotation is "wrong" — it shows the boundary is fuzzy at the genetic level, and reasonable rotations can resolve it differently. The study quality table gives Jang 2001 L quality for genetic correlations. This is adequate to establish the finding but not definitive.

**6. Synthesis: What Cross-Domain Structure Looks Like**

**The three clearest cross-domain signals, ranked:**- E4 → C (vitality/energy axis): Genetic factor loading .65 on C vs .47 on E (XL). rG = .775 with C4 (XL). Absent from E genetic cluster. Developmental convergence. **This is now the single strongest cross-domain signal in the corpus.** Upgraded from #3 to #1 because the Pilia supplementary tables provide quantified, unambiguous genetic evidence from the best study in the corpus. E4 is genetically a C facet.**

  - N2 ↔ A (antagonism axis): Genetic correlations replicated across three samples (Jang 2001, L). Genetic factor loading −.35 on A (Pilia S3, XL). Cross-taxonomy convergence (HEXACO). The N–A boundary is genuinely porous.

  - N5 → C (impulsivity axis): Developmental convergence (strong signal, Tier 1). Genetic factor loading −.41 on C (Pilia S3, XL). Zero genetic clusters (complete isolation). Upgraded from "circumstantial genetic evidence" to "partial genetic confirmation."

Domain-level genetic coherence (Schwaba 2025 GWAS, XL):** **Domain****** **h²_SNP****** **Cross-geography rG****** **Cross-instrument rG****** **PFC enrichment****** **Assessment******

        N

        .10–.13

        .86–.99

        .82–.97

        Yes

        Genetically coherent

        E

        .06–.08

        .86–.99

        .82–.97

        Yes

        Genetically coherent

        O

        .13–.19

        .86–.99

        Yes (unique within-family discrepancy)

        Coherent with caveat

        C

        Yes

        Genetically coherent

        **A****** **.04–.06****** **.65****** **.68****** **No****** **Anomalous******

Agreeableness is the genetic outlier among the Big Five: lowest SNP heritability, lowest cross-geography and cross-instrument genetic correlations, and the only domain without prefrontal cortex gene expression enrichment. At the domain level, A has the weakest genetic basis. This is consistent with the within-domain finding that A facets have the lowest co-development (mean r_change = .21; Schwaba 2022, M) and the most variable rank-order stability patterns (Brandt 2023, L).

**Implication:** The cross-domain boundary signals are concentrated around A (N2↔A, A1↔N, A6↔Emotionality) and C (N5→C, E4→C). These are the two domains where the boundaries appear most porous. N, E, and O have internal structure problems (misbehaving facets, weak aspect coherence) but their domain-level boundaries are more defensible.

**7. Evidence Gaps (Cross-Domain Specific)**

**Critical missing data:**- **rG(N5, C6) — specific pairwise value.** The Pilia S2 data show neither N5 nor C6 appears in any shared genetic cluster (rG > .50 threshold), suggesting their pairwise rG is below .50. However, N5's genetic factor loading of −.41 on C (Table S3) confirms substantial shared genetic variance with C collectively. A specific bivariate rG(N5, C6) would still add precision — it would tell us whether the N5–C relationship is diffuse (shared with all C facets equally) or concentrated on C6 specifically.**- ~~rG(E4, C facets) — the full matrix.**~~ **RESOLVED.** Pilia S2 shows E4 clusters with C4 at rG = .775. E4 is absent from the E cluster and from the main C cluster. The specific pairwise answer is C4 Achievement Striving.**- ~~Pilia (2006) supplementary Tables S2 and S3.**~~ **RESOLVED.** Both tables retrieved. See integrated findings throughout this document. Key results: E4 genetic loading .65 on C; N5 genetic loading −.41 on C; A1 genetic loading −.41 on N; A6 genetic loading .53 on O; E4–C4 rG = .775; N5 in zero clusters; A1 in zero clusters; O4 and O6 excluded from O genetic cluster.**- Facet-level GWAS.** Schwaba et al. (2025) analyzed domain-level only. Confirmed: no facet-level or aspect-level GWAS results exist in the supplementary materials. A facet-level GWAS from this consortium remains the single most transformative data source that does not yet exist.**- Genetic correlations between O facets and C or N facets.** Pilia S3 now provides genetic factor loadings for O facets on all five domains. Key findings: O3 Feelings loads .81 on O (genetically a core O facet despite content concerns); O6 Values loads .70 on O with a −.28 N cross-loading; O1 Fantasy loads .78 on O with a .44 E cross-loading. No O facet shows a substantial genetic loading on A. The O domain is genetically more self-contained than the content analysis of its facets would suggest.**- Whether the published aspect structure replicates at the genetic level.** Pilia S3 provides a five-factor Procrustes rotation (targeted to American normative structure) but not a ten-factor exploratory analysis. Jang et al. (2002), which reportedly found two genetic factors per domain, remains behind a paywall and was not retrievable. The Pilia S3 data are informative about cross-domain structure but do not directly test within-domain two-factor genetic structure.**What I flagged but cannot resolve:**

  - **E4 health confound.** Does E4's late-life decline and genetic C-clustering reflect shared physical vitality or shared personality structure? No study controls for objective health.**- A5 Modesty's nature.** Is A5 a personality trait with unusual environmental sensitivity, or a culturally shaped behavioral norm that doesn't belong in a trait taxonomy? The HEXACO HH age trend (~1.0 SD increase) vs. A5's flatness remains unexplained.**- O6 Values' cross-domain home.** Does O6 show affinity with A-domain facets (tolerance ↔ agreeableness)? No data available.**- Whether N4 Self-Consciousness belongs with N1/N3 or is genuinely independent.** 5-HTTLPR isolation (Jang 2001, L) and near-equal structural loadings (.67/.66) leave N4 underdetermined. No genetic correlation between N4 and any other specific N facet exists in this corpus.**Transparency Notes**

**Pattern-matching flag**

The cross-domain signals I've identified — the N–A boundary porosity, the E4→C genetic link, the N5→C developmental convergence — are well-known findings in the personality literature. The HEXACO model is built partly on resolving the N–A boundary. The E4→C link is discussed by Pilia et al. themselves. The N5 impulsivity question is long-standing.

I want to flag that I am likely recovering known patterns rather than discovering new ones. The value of this synthesis is not novelty — it's that these patterns emerge from an independent weighing of the evidence streams rather than from importing an existing framework. The fact that the evidence independently points to the same cross-domain signals that have been discussed in the literature is either (a) confirmation that those signals are real, or (b) evidence that I'm pattern-matching to prior knowledge despite instructions not to.

I believe (a) is more likely, because the evidence trail is explicit and auditable: specific studies, specific quality ratings, specific effect sizes. But I cannot rule out (b).

**What surprised me**

**The E–N genetic mega-cluster.** Pilia S2 Cluster 18 contains E1, E3, E6, N1, N2, N3, N4, and N6 — eight facets from two domains in a single genetic cluster (all pairwise rG > .50 threshold for cluster membership). This is by far the largest cross-domain genetic cluster, and it implies that E and N share more genetic variance than either shares with O, A, or C. The phenotypic Big Five structure treats E and N as separate dimensions, but genetically they form a mega-cluster. This is consistent with the well-known phenotypic correlation between E and reversed-N, but the genetic magnitude is striking. Note what is excluded: E2 Gregariousness, E4 Activity, E5 Excitement-Seeking, and N5 Impulsiveness. The E–N genetic core is {Warmth, Assertiveness, Positive Emotions} × {Anxiety, Hostility, Depression, Self-Consciousness, Vulnerability}.

**The clearest cross-domain developmental signal ({O2, O5} — both stable across the lifespan despite being in opposite structural aspects) has no cross-domain implications.** It's a within-O finding that crosscuts the Intellect/Openness boundary. I mention it here because it was the single cleanest developmental convergence in any domain that contradicted the structural mapping, and it has no obvious connection to any published model I'm aware of.

**The genetic coherence of A as a domain** (Schwaba 2025, XL) is worse than I would have expected from the literature. The .65 cross-geography genetic correlation and .68 cross-instrument genetic correlation are dramatically lower than the other four domains (.86–.99). Combined with the within-domain evidence (lowest co-development, most variable stability patterns, no PFC enrichment), this raises a genuine question about whether Agreeableness is a coherent genetic entity or a phenotypic alliance of convenience. The Pilia S2 data add nuance: A does have two genetic clusters ({A3, A4} and {A2, A5, A6}), but A1 Trust is orphaned from both. The A domain has genetic substructure but its most prominent facet is genetically unmoored.

**E4 Activity's genetic loading on C (.65) exceeds its loading on E (.47).** I expected the genetic clustering with C to be real (based on the dendrogram in the main text), but I did not expect the genetic loading to be *primary* on the wrong domain. This is not a cross-loading — it's a misclassification. The fact that a Procrustes rotation targeted to the American normative structure still places E4 genetically in C, despite the rotation's explicit bias toward replicating the phenotypic structure, makes this finding even more striking.
