**Overall factor congruence coefficients:** N = .94, E = .89, O = .87, A = .96, C = .97. Mean = .93.

**Key observations from Table S3:**

  - E4 Activity is the ONLY facet whose primary genetic loading is on a different domain than its phenotypic assignment (.65 C > .47 E).
  - N5 Impulsiveness has the lowest VCC (.86) in the N domain and near-equal loadings on N (.45) and reversed-C (−.41).
  - C6 Deliberation has the lowest VCC (.79) in the entire table, with a substantial O cross-loading (−.43). Genetically, C6 is partly reversed-O (closed-mindedness/deliberate caution).
  - A1 Trust loads −.41 on N — the largest reversed-N loading of any A facet. Genetically half inverted-N.
  - A6 Tender-Mindedness loads .53 on O — the largest O cross-loading of any non-O facet. Genetically a blend of A and O.
  - E5 Excitement-Seeking loads −.43 on A — the largest reversed-A loading of any E facet. This is a previously unflagged cross-domain signal.
  - The E genetic factor has the lowest congruence coefficient (.89) after O (.87), suggesting E's genetic structure is the second-least-faithful reproduction of its phenotypic structure.

**Cross-Domain Synthesis — Conversation 6 – v2**

**Orientation**

This synthesis brings together the five domain profiles (N, E, O, A, C) produced in Conversations 1–5, all built from the v2 study corpus. The tasks are:

  - Identify facets that cluster across domain boundaries
  - Identify recurring structural motifs across domains
  - Flag evidence that domain boundaries are misdrawn
  - Compile a cross-domain anomalies list

**Critical limitation carried forward from all five domain profiles:** The developmental evidence stream (Tier 1 per project instructions) is severely underrepresented. The only developmental study covering all 30 NEO facets is Bleidorn (2009, M quality, N = 344, ages 18–59). Jackson (2009, M quality) adds cross-sectional evidence for C facets only. The large-N longitudinal studies that should anchor this project (Roberts 2006, Schwaba 2022, Brandt 2023, Terracciano 2005) are not in this corpus. This means most cross-domain claims rest on structural (Tier 2) and genetic (Tier 3) evidence, which is an inversion of the intended evidence hierarchy.

**Study quality reference**

When studies conflict in this synthesis, I apply the quality ratings from the study quality table:

        **Study****** **Key quality ratings******

        Yamagata et al. (2006)

        **XL** structural; **L** genetic correlations

        Soto & John (2017)

        **XL** structural

        Jang et al. (2002)

        **L** heritability, **L** genetic correlations

        Bleidorn et al. (2009)

        **M** developmental, **S** heritability

        Haehner et al. (2025)

        **L** structural

        Zakrisson et al. (2025)

        **L** structural

        Jackson et al. (2009)

        **M** developmental, **M** structural

        Lewis & Bates (2014)

        **M** heritability, **M** genetic correlations

        Mitrović et al. (2024)

        **S** genetic

**Section 1: Cross-Domain Genetic Bridges**

The strongest cross-domain evidence comes from Yamagata et al. (2006, XL for structural claims, L for genetic correlations). This study reports genetic factor loadings for all 30 NEO-PI-R facets across three national twin samples (Canada, Germany, Japan). Cross-loadings ≥ |.30| that replicate across at least two of three countries are the gold standard for cross-domain genetic affinity in this corpus.

1.1 The Impulse Control Axis: N5 ↔ C6 (and the broader N–C interface)

**The evidence:**

N5 Impulsiveness loads genetically on C at −.53 / −.33 / −.52 across Canada, Germany, Japan (Yamagata 2006, XL). Its genetic N loading is only .45 / .37 / .51 — weaker than its C cross-loading in Canada and Japan. Within the N domain, N5 has 66–85% facet-specific genetic variance and loads on neither within-N genetic factor (Jang 2002, L). Its developmental change is entirely environmental (Bleidorn 2009, S for biometric). N5 was excluded from BFI-2 as interstitial content (Soto & John 2017, XL).

C6 Deliberation occupies the opposite end of this same dimension. Jackson (2009, M) reports that Impulse Control content (≈ C6 + reverse-N5) shows the largest cumulative developmental increase of any C facet (d₁₃ = 0.88). Yamagata (2006) confirms N5 and C6 are on opposite poles of the genetic C factor. Lewis & Bates (2014, M) find that HEXACO Prudence (≈ C6 content) is genetically independent of other C facets.

**N6 Vulnerability also crosses this boundary.** N6 has a genetic C cross-loading of −.37 / −.34 / −.57 across three countries (Yamagata 2006, XL). N6 is a legitimate N facet (loads .71–.85 on genetic N) that also has consistent roots in C. Its content — difficulty self-regulating under stress — maps conceptually to the same constraint/regulation dimension that N5 and C6 index.

**C1 Competence is the C-side mirror of N6.** C1 has a genetic N cross-loading of −.48 / −.53 / −.38 across three countries (Yamagata 2006, XL) — the largest of any C facet. C1 sits at the intersection of "I can handle things" (C) and "I don't fall apart under pressure" (low N).

**Assessment of the N–C interface:**

Four facets participate in a genetic gradient between negative emotionality and self-regulation:

        **Facet****** **Genetic N loading****** **Genetic C loading****** **Primary domain****** **Cross-domain affinity******

        N5 Impulsiveness

        .37–.51 (weak)

        −.33 to −.53 (moderate-strong)

        Nominally N

        **Genetically more C than N** in 2/3 samples

        N6 Vulnerability

        .71–.85 (strong)

        −.34 to −.57 (moderate)

        N with C roots

        C component present but secondary

        C1 Competence

        −.38 to −.53 (moderate)

        .58–.67*

        C with N roots

        N component present but secondary

        C6 Deliberation

        —

        variable, genetically unstable

        C (weak member)

        Opposite pole from N5

(*C1's genetic C loading is inferred from its position in Yamagata 2006's genetic C factor rather than reported as an explicit cross-loading value. It is described as part of the genetic C core.)

**Confidence:** Strong signal for the N5 ↔ C cross-domain bridge (XL quality, three independent samples, multi-study convergence). Moderate signal for N6 → C and C1 → low-N (XL quality for genetic loadings, but these are secondary rather than primary signals). Moderate signal for C6 as the C-domain pole of impulse control (M quality developmental data, M quality HEXACO genetic data).

**Pattern-matching flag:** I notice this cluster describes what many frameworks call an "impulse control" or "constraint" dimension cutting across N and C. I want to flag that the evidence independently supports this cross-cutting dimension: Yamagata 2006 reports it as raw genetic factor loadings without reference to any cross-domain construct, and Jackson 2009 discovered it empirically through developmental trajectory analysis using Roberts' facet model. The convergence of these independent approaches strengthens the finding.

1.2 The Anger/Antagonism Axis: N2 ↔ A

The evidence:**

N2 Angry Hostility has a genetic A cross-loading of −.32 / −.41 / −.60 across Canada, Germany, Japan (Yamagata 2006, XL). The cross-loading *strengthens* from Western to Eastern samples. In Japan, the genetic A loading (−.60) approaches the genetic N loading (.56). Within the N domain, N2 anchors its own genetic factor ("Hostility"), separate from the "General distress" factor carrying N1/N3/N4/N6 (Jang 2002, L).

BFI-2 developers deliberately stripped anger content from their N facet "Emotional Volatility" during instrument development (Soto & John 2017, XL), recognizing the contamination.

**From the A side:** A1 Trust has a genetic N cross-loading of −.59 / −.39 / −.29 across three countries (Yamagata 2006, XL). In Canada, Trust is genetically more a low-N facet than an A facet (N loading exceeds A loading). A1 also has 62% facet-specific genetic variance within A (Jang 2002, L) and was orphaned from both BFAS aspects by the BFI-2 developers (Soto & John 2017, XL).

**Assessment:**

N2 and A1 are opposite-pole markers of the same cross-domain genetic dimension: hostility/distrust vs. trust/equanimity. The evidence:

        **Facet****** **Genetic N loading****** **Genetic A loading****** **Where it "lives"****** **Where its genetics point******

        N2 Angry Hostility

        .56–.79

        −.32 to −.60

        N

        Bridges to low-A

        A1 Trust

        −.29 to −.59

        .49–.60

        A

        Bridges to low-N

These are not the same facet measured in reverse — N2 is anger/hostility, A1 is interpersonal trust. But genetically, they share a dimension: the tendency toward hostile attributions and reactive anger (N2) vs. the tendency toward secure, trusting interpersonal expectations (A1).

**HEXACO alignment:** Lewis & Bates (2014, M) report that HEXACO Agreeableness (which is anger-management content, not NEO-A prosociality content) has Forgiveness as a genetically partially independent facet. Forgiveness/anger-management content maps to this same N2–A boundary.

**Confidence:** Strong signal for N2 → A (XL quality, three independent samples, strengthens cross-culturally). Strong signal for A1 → N (XL quality, three independent samples, though weakens cross-culturally — opposite gradient from N2). Moderate signal for them indexing the same dimension (inferred from opposing cross-loadings; no study directly tests rG between N2 and A1).

1.3 The Activity/Conscientiousness Bridge: E4 → C

The evidence:**

E4 Activity has a genetic C cross-loading of .48 / .53 / .54 across Canada, Germany, Japan (Yamagata 2006, XL). This is one of the most robust cross-domain findings in the entire corpus:

  - It replicates across all three national samples.
  - The genetic C loading (.48–.54) is stronger than the phenotypic C loading (.39) — the cross-domain affinity is *more* apparent genetically than phenotypically.
  - In some samples, E4's genetic C loading rivals its genetic E loading.

BFI-2 Energy Level (which absorbs E4 content) had its between-domain correlation with C-Productiveness exceed its within-E correlation with Assertiveness in 2 of 3 Swedish samples (Zakrisson 2025, L). BFI-2 developers deliberately reclassified E4-like content from Assertiveness to Enthusiasm (Soto & John 2017, XL), but the structural reclassification doesn't resolve the genetic problem — E4's genes pull toward C, not just toward a different E aspect.

**Assessment:**

E4 is genetically a hybrid E–C construct. No other facet in the corpus has a cross-domain genetic loading this strong and this stable across three independent samples. The domain boundary between E (specifically, the energetic-activity component) and C is genuinely blurred at the genetic level.

**Confidence:** Strong signal — XL quality, three independent samples, replicated. This is arguably the single most robust cross-domain genetic finding in the corpus.

1.4 The Warmth/Prosociality Bridge: E1 → A

The evidence:**

E1 Warmth has a genetic A cross-loading of .45 (Yamagata 2006, from Pilia S3 data, XL). In Jang (2002, L), E1 loads on both within-E genetic factors — it doesn't anchor either one cleanly. Its content (interpersonal warmth, affection) is conceptually A-adjacent.

A3 Altruism shows the mirror pattern: it loads genetically across A (.32–.62), C (.39 in Canada), and E (.37 in Canada) — Yamagata 2006, XL. A3's genetic A loading drops to .32 in Japan, barely above threshold.

**Assessment:**

E1 and A3 share the interpersonal warmth/prosociality space. Both are genetically multi-domain. Neither is a clean marker of its assigned domain at the genetic level. This suggests a genetic "warmth/affiliation" dimension that the Big Five factor structure assigns partly to E and partly to A, but which has a partially unitary genetic basis.

**Confidence:** Moderate signal. E1's A cross-loading is from one data source (Pilia S3 via Yamagata). A3's genetic diffuseness replicates across three countries but with cross-cultural instability. The conceptual connection is clear; the genetic evidence is suggestive but thinner than for the N5→C or N2→A bridges.

1.5 The Assertiveness/"Agency" Complex: E3 → multiple domains

The evidence:**

E3 Assertiveness is genetically the most diffuse facet in the corpus. Yamagata (2006, XL) reports genetic loadings on four domains simultaneously:

  - E: .45 / .55 / .50
  - Low-N: −.43 / −.35 / −.19
  - Low-A: −.26 / −.38 / −.44
  - C: .30 / .38 / .39

No other facet in this corpus touches four genetic domains. BFI-2 Assertiveness has phenotypic cross-loadings on C (.23/.24) and O (.24/.24) as well (Soto & John 2017, XL).

**Assessment:**

E3 is not a facet that "bridges" two domains — it genetically spans a cross-domain space that might be called "agentic self-assertion": confident (low N), dominant (low A), organized (C), socially assertive (E). Its placement in E may be somewhat arbitrary. It loads highest on E, but not by a large margin.

**Confidence:** Strong signal for genetic diffuseness (XL quality, three samples). Whether this represents a coherent "agency" dimension or simply a noisy facet is not answerable from this corpus.

**1.6 Smaller Cross-Domain Signals**

O3 Feelings → E and N. Yamagata (2006, XL) reports mild E and N genetic cross-loadings for O3. O3's content (emotional awareness/sensitivity) overlaps affective domains. However, O3 is one of the genetically purest O facets at the five-factor level — the cross-loadings are small. **Weak signal.**A6 Tender-Mindedness → O (possible). A6 has 56–58% facet-specific genetic variance within A (Jang 2002, L). A previous-round study reportedly found A6 with a .53 genetic loading on O (Pilia 2006 Table S3) — but this study is not in the current corpus. The flag was noted in the A domain synthesis but cannot be confirmed here. **Weak signal — gap in evidence.**O6 Values → social-attitudinal domain. O6 has near-zero heritability (Jang 2002, L), 51–59% facet-specific genetic variance, and content (tolerance, open-mindedness) that is social-attitudinal rather than cognitive or perceptual. Jackson (2009, M) found that Conventionality (≈ reverse O6) has a unique late-onset developmental trajectory. O6 may not belong in any of the Big Five domains — its content might be better understood as a social-attitude dimension influenced primarily by shared environment. **Moderate signal for non-belonging; weak signal for specific alternative placement.**

**E5 Excitement-Seeking.** E5 has 68–70% facet-specific genetic variance within E (Jang 2002, L) and a genetic A cross-loading of −.43 (from Pilia S3 via Yamagata, XL). It was excluded from BFI-2 (Soto & John 2017, XL). Its genetic specificity raises the question of whether its non-E genetic variance overlaps with N5 Impulsiveness (also genetically orphaned from its domain, also excluded from BFI-2). No study in this corpus tests rG(E5, N5). **Moderate signal for non-belonging; speculative for cross-domain affinity.**

**Section 2: Recurring Structural Motifs**

**2.1 Every domain has a phenotypic aspect structure that does not map cleanly onto its genetic factor structure.**

This is the single most consistent finding across all five domains.

        **Domain****** **Phenotypic aspect structure****** **Genetic factor structure****** **Alignment******

        N

        Withdrawal {N1,N3,N4,N6} vs. Volatility {N2,N5}

        "General distress" {N1,N3,N4,N6} vs. "Hostility" {N2 alone}. N5 isolated.

        Partial. N5 breaks the Volatility aspect genetically. N2 stands alone.

        E

        Enthusiasm {E1,E2,E6} vs. Assertiveness {E3,E4,E5}

        Factor 1 "Sociability" (led by E2) vs. Factor 2 (content shifts across cultures). E5 isolated. E4 → C.

        Weak. Factor content shifts between samples. COMT provides weak molecular support.

        O

        Openness {O1,O2,O3} vs. Intellect {O4,O5,O6}

        German: clean Openness/Intellect split (best of any domain). Canadian: O5 cross-loads. O6 mostly independent.

        Best of any domain in one sample, but O4 is cross-culturally unstable and O6 is genetically adrift.

        A

        Compassion {A1,A3,A6} vs. Politeness {A2,A4,A5}

        "Compassionate concern" vs. "Social propriety" — content shifts across samples. A1 is genetically N. A6 is 56–58% specific.

        Weak. Only A2 and A4 are stable genetic core.

        C

        Industriousness {C1,C4,C5} vs. Orderliness {C2,C3,C6}

        "Ambitious/Driven" {C1,C4} vs. "Methodical/Dutiful" {C2,C3,C5,C6}. C5 migrates to Orderliness genetically.

        Partial. C5 is genetically misassigned.

**Assessment:** The two-aspect phenotypic structure is a real feature of all five domains — it captures variance that exists. But in no domain does the genetic architecture cleanly replicate that structure. The closest is Openness in the German sample (Jang 2002); the worst is Agreeableness. The phenotypic aspect structure appears to be shaped substantially by environmental factors and measurement method, not just by genetic architecture.

**Confidence:** Strong signal — this pattern replicates across five domains using independent genetic analyses (Jang 2002, L; Yamagata 2006, XL).

**2.2 Every domain has at least one genetically orphaned facet.**

        **Domain****** **Orphan facet****** **% facet-specific genetic variance****** **Excluded from BFI-2?******

        N

        N5 Impulsiveness

        66–85%

        Yes

        E

        E5 Excitement-Seeking

        68–70%

        Yes

        O

        O6 Values

        51–59%

        Yes

        A

        A6 Tender-Mindedness

        56–58%

        No (Compassion)

        C

        C4 Achievement Striving

        41–43%

        No (Productiveness)

The first three are the most dramatic: N5, E5, and O6 all have >50% facet-specific genetic variance and were excluded from BFI-2 as interstitial or non-core content. The BFI-2 developers' structural intuitions converge with the genetic evidence — these facets don't belong cleanly in their assigned domains.

A6 Tender-Mindedness is unusual: it was retained in BFI-2 (as part of Compassion) despite having the second-highest facet-specific genetic variance in its domain. Structurally it's a clean Compassion marker; genetically it's doing its own thing.

C4 Achievement Striving has the highest facet-specific variance in C, but at 41–43% it's less extreme. C4's genetic uniqueness may reflect a specific "competitive ambition" component with distinct molecular-genetic correlates (Mitrović 2024 COMT finding, S quality).

**Assessment:** The pattern is not random. High facet-specific genetic variance appears to concentrate in facets whose content sits at domain boundaries or captures something not well-represented by the domain's core genetic factor. The BFI-2 exclusion decisions track this pattern remarkably well.

**Confidence:** Moderate signal — the pattern is consistent but the genetic evidence comes primarily from Jang (2002, L) with within-domain-only analyses. Cross-domain multivariate models might reveal that "facet-specific" variance is actually shared with facets in other domains.

**2.3 Aspect correlations are asymmetric within every domain.**

Haehner et al. (2025, L) provides the most direct data on within-domain aspect correlations:

        **Domain****** **Aspect pair****** **Correlation****** **Assessment******

        N

        Withdrawal–Volatility

        .61

        Highest — most cohesive domain at aspect level

        E

        Enthusiasm–Assertiveness

        .49

        Moderate

        O

        Openness–Intellect

        .29

        Lowest — nearly independent aspects

        A

        Compassion–Politeness

        .34

        Low — substantially distinct constructs

        C

        Industriousness–Orderliness

        .39

        Low-moderate

Haehner (2025) also reports divergent external correlates for aspects within every domain: Industriousness dwarfs Orderliness on life satisfaction (.44 vs .14) and self-esteem (.48 vs .13); Compassion drives A's wellbeing correlations while Politeness is near-zero; Openness and Intellect diverge on self-esteem (.29 Intellect vs −.02 Openness) and political orientation.

**Assessment:** The Big Five domains are not internally homogeneous at the aspect level. The two aspects within each domain predict different things and correlate only moderately with each other. This is expected from the domain syntheses but worth highlighting as a cross-domain motif: the *degree* of within-domain coherence varies substantially, from N (.61) to O (.29).

**Confidence:** Strong signal — Haehner 2025 (L quality) provides these correlations in a large probability sample across three language groups.

**2.4 BFI-2 structural orphans cluster with genetic boundary-crossers.**

Five NEO facets were excluded from BFI-2: N5, E5, O4, O6, A5. Three BFI-2 facets were deliberately given no BFAS aspect assignment: A1 Trust, C3 Responsibility, O1 Creative Imagination (though Creative Imagination ≠ O1 Fantasy).

Comparing these structural orphans against genetic evidence:

        **Structural orphan****** **Genetic status******

        N5 Impulsiveness

        Genetically more C than N; 66–85% specific

        E5 Excitement-Seeking

        68–70% genetically specific within E

        O4 Actions

        Most cross-culturally unstable genetic O loading (.65 → .37)

        O6 Values

        Near-zero heritability; 51–59% specific

        A5 Modesty

        Cross-culturally unstable genetic A loading (.39–.60)

        A1 Trust (aspect-orphaned)

        Genetically more N than A in Canada; 62% specific

        C3 Dutifulness (aspect-orphaned)

        Structurally straddles C and A; genetically firmly C

The overlap is striking: facets that expert psychometricians (Soto & John) judged as structurally interstitial or non-core are overwhelmingly the same facets that show genetic boundary-crossing, instability, or isolation.

**Confidence:** Strong signal — the convergence between independent structural judgment (Soto & John 2017, XL) and genetic architecture (Jang 2002, L; Yamagata 2006, XL) is robust. The one exception is C3 Dutifulness, which is structurally orphaned but genetically well-placed in C.

**2.5 COMT associations track a cross-domain "Enthusiasm + Competence" cluster.**

Mitrović (2024, **S quality** — this is low-confidence evidence) found that the Val+ allele of COMT V158M was associated with higher scores on: E1 Warmth, E2 Gregariousness, E6 Positive Emotions, C1 Competence, C4 Achievement Striving, and N4 Self-Consciousness.

It was NOT associated with: E3 Assertiveness, E4 Activity, E5 Excitement-Seeking, C2 Order, C3 Dutifulness, C5 Self-Discipline, C6 Deliberation, or any other N, O, or A facet.

**Assessment:** This splits neatly into (a) the Enthusiasm E facets + the dopaminergically-linked C facets {C1, C4}, and (b) everything else. It is the only molecular evidence in this corpus that provides a cross-domain biological boundary. The boundary it draws — Enthusiasm/Competence vs. Orderliness/Constraint — crosscuts the E/C domain boundary.

**Confidence:** Weak signal. Single candidate gene, small effects (d ≈ 0.15–0.21), no replication in this corpus. The N4 finding is an additional anomaly (N4 is the only N facet associated). I report it because the pattern is cross-domain and internally consistent, but it should be treated as hypothesis-generating only.

**Section 3: Evidence That Domain Boundaries Are Misdrawn**

**3.1 N5 Impulsiveness is not a Neuroticism facet.**

This is the strongest domain-misplacement signal in the corpus. Evidence:

  - Genetically, N5 loads more on C (−.33 to −.53) than on N (.37–.51) in two of three countries (Yamagata 2006, XL).
  - 66–85% of N5's genetic variance is facet-specific within N — it doesn't participate in either common genetic N factor (Jang 2002, L).
  - Its developmental change is entirely environmental, unlike all other N facets (Bleidorn 2009, S for biometric).
  - Excluded from BFI-2 as interstitial content (Soto & John 2017, XL).
  - Jackson (2009, M) found that Impulse Control (≈ reverse-N5 + C6 content) shows the largest developmental age difference of any C-domain facet (d₁₃ = 0.88).

N5's proper domain home is C (reversed), not N. The NEO-PI-R's assignment of impulsivity to Neuroticism is not supported by the genetic or structural evidence.

**Confidence:** Strong signal — multi-study, multi-stream, XL quality core evidence.

**3.2 E4 Activity is genetically as much a C facet as an E facet.**

  - Genetic C loading .48–.54 across three countries (Yamagata 2006, XL).
  - BFI-2 Energy Level pulls toward C in 2/3 Swedish samples (Zakrisson 2025, L).
  - Not COMT-associated, unlike Enthusiasm facets (Mitrović 2024, S).

E4 is not misplaced in E — it does load on genetic E. But it is equally a C facet genetically. The domain boundary between "being energetically active" (E) and "getting things done" (C) is not sharp at the genetic level.

**Confidence:** Strong signal for dual domain membership. The evidence does not support removing E4 from E — rather, it suggests the boundary between E4-type content and C-type content is blurry.

**3.3 A1 Trust may belong in low-N rather than A.**

  - In Canada, A1's genetic N loading (−.59) exceeds its genetic A loading (.49) — Yamagata 2006, XL.
  - The N loading weakens cross-culturally (−.39 Germany, −.29 Japan), but it is present in all three samples.
  - 62% facet-specific genetic variance within A (Jang 2002, L).
  - Orphaned from both BFAS aspects (Soto & John 2017, XL).
  - Only A facet with genetic influence on developmental change (Bleidorn 2009, M).

Trust's content — the tendency to believe others are well-intentioned vs. anticipating betrayal — has an obvious emotional-security component (feeling safe in the world) alongside its interpersonal component (believing people are good). The genetic evidence suggests the emotional-security component is primary in Western samples, with the interpersonal component becoming more prominent in Japanese data.

**Confidence:** Strong signal from genetic data (XL quality). The cross-cultural gradient complicates interpretation — A1 may be more genuinely an A facet in collectivist cultures.

**3.4 The C–A boundary is blurred at the "Dutifulness/Respectfulness" junction.**

C3 Dutifulness was given no aspect assignment in BFI-2 (Soto & John 2017, XL). BFI-2 Responsibility has a .26 secondary loading on A. In 2/3 Swedish samples, Responsibility's between-domain correlation with A-Respectfulness exceeded its within-domain C correlations (Zakrisson 2025, L). Jackson (2009, M) found that Roberts' Reliability facet correlates almost equally with C and A.

However, C3 loads firmly on genetic C (clean loading, Yamagata 2006 XL; .53 on "Methodical/Dutiful," Jang 2002 L). The C–A overlap is phenotypic/environmental, not genetic.

**Assessment:** The phenotypic blurring of C3 and A (Politeness) content likely reflects environmental covariation — dutiful, responsible people appear agreeable because the same life experiences (socialization, role demands) produce both. Genetically, C3 belongs in C.

**Confidence:** Moderate signal. Structural evidence (XL quality) shows the boundary is blurred. Genetic evidence (XL, L quality) shows C3 is firmly C. The resolution is that the blurring is environmental, not genetic — but this interpretation rests on the assumption that genetic placement is more "real" than phenotypic placement, which is a theoretical stance rather than an empirical finding.

**3.5 O is the most structurally questionable domain.**

Multiple lines of evidence converge:

  - Lowest within-domain aspect correlation: .29 (Haehner 2025, L).
  - Worst CFA fit of any BFI-2 domain (Zakrisson 2025, L).
  - Two facets excluded from BFI-2 (O4, O6).
  - Only metric (not scalar) cross-cultural invariance (Zakrisson 2025, L).
  - O4 Actions has the most cross-culturally unstable genetic loading in the entire corpus (.65 → .43 → .37; Yamagata 2006, XL).
  - O6 Values has near-zero heritability and 51–59% facet-specific genetic variance (Jang 2002, L).

The Openness aspect {O2, O3; O1 extended} is well-supported as a coherent unit. The Intellect aspect {O5 primary; O4, O6 peripheral} is fragmented — O5 stands alone as a coherent construct with weak domain integration. O4 and O6 are peripheral to the entire domain.

Whether "Openness to Experience" is one domain or two loosely correlated constructs sharing a label is a question this corpus raises but cannot settle. The aspect correlation of .29 puts the two halves of O further apart than most between-domain correlations in many datasets.

**Confidence:** Strong signal for O being the weakest domain structurally. The evidence is multi-study and multi-stream.

**3.6 A is the most genetically incoherent domain.**

  - Lowest cross-national genetic factor congruence coefficient (.86 Canada–Japan; Yamagata 2006, XL).
  - Only two facets (A2, A4) are cross-nationally stable genetic A markers.
  - Complete etiological heterogeneity in developmental change (Bleidorn 2009, M): no two same-aspect facets share the same etiological profile.
  - Lowest BFI-2 congruence with US original in Sweden (.75; Zakrisson 2025, L).
  - A1 genetically straddles A and N. A3 is genetically diffuse across A, C, and E. A6 is 56–58% facet-specific.

The domain holds together phenotypically in factor analysis, but at the genetic level it is a loose coalition. The genetic core is just {A2, A4}.

**Confidence:** Strong signal — Yamagata 2006 (XL) provides the cross-national genetic instability data, and it converges with Jang 2002 (L), Bleidorn 2009 (M), and structural evidence from multiple sources.

**The big picture:** N and E hold together as domains. O holds together weakly, with a very clean internal split but two peripheral members. C holds together but its aspect structure is genetically wrong. A barely holds together — four of six facets are genetically problematic.

**Section 6: Evidence Gaps**

**What this corpus cannot answer**- **No cross-domain genetic correlation matrix.** Jang (2002) analyzed facets within domains only (6 × 6). Yamagata (2006) reports genetic loadings on the five genetic factors, which gives cross-domain information at the factor level but not facet-to-facet rG. I can see that N5 loads on genetic C and that C6 is on the C factor, but I don't know rG(N5, C6) directly. A full 30 × 30 genetic correlation matrix would be transformative.**- No robust developmental evidence.** The entire developmental stream rests on Bleidorn (2009, M quality, N = 344) for NEO facets and Jackson (2009, M quality, cross-sectional) for C facets. The large longitudinal studies (Roberts 2006, Schwaba 2022, Brandt 2023, Terracciano 2005) are not in this corpus. Developmental evidence should be Tier 1 but it is functioning as Tier 3 here due to thinness.**- No test of whether E5 and N5 share genetic variance.** Both are genetically orphaned from their domains, both excluded from BFI-2, both capture sensation-seeking/impulsivity-adjacent content. Whether they are genetically related or genetically independent is an important question for understanding the Big Five's periphery.**- No test of whether A6 loads on O genetically.** A previous-round finding (Pilia 2006) reportedly found this, but it is not in the current corpus.**- No GWAS evidence at facet level for most domains.** The molecular-level architecture of the cross-domain bridges is completely unknown.**- No observer-report data.** All evidence is self-report. Yamagata (2006) explicitly flags that the environmental factor structure may be inflated by implicit personality theory bias. Observer-report twin data would help separate this from genuine phenotypic structure.**- No explicit test of alternative domain structures.** All studies assume the Big Five as a starting point and analyze facets within that framework. No study tests whether a six-factor structure (splitting O into Openness and Intellect), or a restructured model reassigning boundary facets (N5 → C, A1 → N), fits the genetic data better.**- Late-life developmental trajectories are absent.** Bleidorn covers 18–59. Many of the most interesting developmental phenomena (N6 Vulnerability increase in late life, O6/Conventionality late-onset change) occur after age 60.**Transparency Notes**

**Pattern-matching flag: The "Stability/Plasticity" metatrait shadow**

Several cross-domain findings in this synthesis align with the published Stability/Plasticity metatrait model (DeYoung 2006): the N–C boundary (Stability), the E–O boundary (Plasticity), the N–A hostility axis. I want to flag that I may be perceiving these as a coherent cross-domain structure partly because the metatrait model is in my training data.

What the evidence independently shows: specific cross-domain genetic bridges between specific facets (N5→C, N2→A, E4→C, A1→N, E1→A). These are facet-level findings, not domain-level findings. Whether they aggregate into metatrait-like super-factors cannot be determined from this corpus — that would require a full 30×30 genetic correlation matrix and a model comparison approach.

**What I notice but cannot confirm**

The cross-domain bridges I've documented cluster into what look like two groups:

  - Group A: N5↔C6, N6↔C, C1↔low-N (a constraint/emotional-stability axis crossing N and C)
  - Group B: E1↔A, E4↔C, N2↔A (a sociability/antagonism/activity axis crossing E, A, and C)

I report this observation but flag it as pattern-matching from my end. The studies in this corpus do not test whether these form coherent higher-order groupings. The human should evaluate whether this pattern is signal or pareidolia.
